Freeborn defines "systematic understanding" as four conditions: an internal model, non-memorizing tracking of a property, bridge principles fixed in the deployed pipeline, and derivation by the agent itself. Four small demonstrations (the genus of a torus, Mars's eccentricity e≈0.096 with Kepler's form built in, grokked modular addition mod 113, and Othello-GPT from Li et al.) show it can be met. The Fractured Understanding Hypothesis (§7.5), which the paper argues for but does not test, says the understanding deep networks reach is characteristically symbolically misaligned, non-reductive, locally fractured, proxy-dependent and representationally fragmented. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-123); worth reading as a careful, explicitly "thin" explication that turns the proxy disputes (memorization vs generalization, interpolation vs extrapolation) into testable conditions. The FUH is a programmatic hypothesis, not a finding, and two of the four worked examples do not meet the paper's own bridge-principle conditions (see Limitations). |
| [LIT-132](../../record/literature.d/LIT-132.md) v2 | Luck and Intentional Action: A Causal Account | Quillien (2026), DOI-10.1162/OPMI.a.361. Across three Prolific studies (N = 198, 673, 142; Likert 1–7), judgments that "X happened because the agent wanted X" track intentionality judgments under manipulations of luck. Causation is the strongest predictor of intentionality: standardized β = .46, .56, .53, against know-how β = .33, .20, .27 and probability-raising β = .14, .16, .10. Across the 12 condition means, r = .98. Causation only partly mediates the effect of luck (64%, 45% and 49%; exploratory analyses). As the causal account predicted, Study 3 found that the effect of Process (normal vs mix-up) on intentionality is larger when the agent knows how the buttons work (interaction F(1, 423) = 32). In the morally neutral Kraemer vignette, intentionality and causation came apart. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-092](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-092.md)); Worth reading as the cleanest current statement and test of the causal (desire-causes-outcome) account of folk intentionality under luck. It has pre-registered designs, open data and an honest discussion of the half-mediation and the Kraemer divergence. Its evidence is correlational and its "model" is verbal, not computational. |
| [LIT-133](../../record/literature.d/LIT-133.md) v2 | Towards a Generalized Theory of Observers | Elshatlawy et al. (2025), [ARXIV-2504.16225](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16225). The paper defines a "minimal observer" as a finite-state transducer with a boundary: a tuple O = (X, Y, Z, f, g, B) with \|X\| > 1, \|Y\| ≥ 1, \|Z\| ≥ 1 and feedback closure (Def. 4.1, p. 57). It then asserts, without derivation, that measurement outcomes, reference frames, coarse-graining and the quantum–classical cut are all defined by such observers. The paper's only formal results are that isomorphism of these machines is an equivalence relation ("Theorem 2", p. 93), and a complexity bound C(O) ≥ log 2 (Prop. 4.1, p. 94) whose correction term Λ is never defined and whose proof supports only C(O) ≥ 0. | Rejected — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-121); not worth a reader's time as a theory of observers. Its one definition is a Moore machine with an unformalised boundary, its "theorems" are definitional, and its quantum claims are asserted, including the claim to identify "the exact moment and mechanism of quantum collapse" (p. 75). A reader wanting minimal observers or agents is better served by the sources it surveys: Ashby, Hartle's IGUS, Fields, and Gielen & Wise's observer space in §4.10.3. |
| [LIT-134](../../record/literature.d/LIT-134.md) | The Duality of Content | Prinz (2000), DOI-10.1023/A:1018612122389. Concepts have two distinct wide contents — a "nominal" content fixed by the observable features used to categorize, and a "real" (referential) content fixed by incipient causes plus nomological covariation — echoing Locke's nominal vs real essences. (Reconstructed from secondary sources; unverified against the paper.) | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and found the article paywalled, with no open or repository copy (PhilPapers and Academia.edu refused the request, and the author's site does not list it). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-135](../../record/literature.d/LIT-135.md) v3 | Conscious artificial intelligence and biological naturalism | Seth (2024), DOI-10.1017/S0140525X25000032. Seth argues that conscious AI needs computational functionalism *and* substrate flexibility extending to silicon, and that both are doubtful (§3.9). Against them he gives a positive, explicitly non-vitalist case, running from interoceptive predictive processing through the free energy principle to autopoiesis, that consciousness is a property "of only (but not necessarily all) living systems" (§4). He concludes that real artificial consciousness is "unlikely along current trajectories" and would need "living AI" (§5.8), while conscious-*seeming* AI is "almost certain" and may be "cognitively impenetrable" (§6.2). He concedes that "my case is not watertight" (§5.8). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-169](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-169.md)); worth reading as the leading statement of biological naturalism about AI, with a useful five-scenario map (Table 1) of what conscious AI would require. Its positive case rests on the free energy principle, which the paper itself calls "much debated" (§4.2), and it claims plausibility rather than necessity. The commentaries and the Author's Response (BBS 49, 2026) are the next thing to read, and the response was not reached here. |
| [LIT-136](../../record/literature.d/LIT-136.md) v2 | The Informational Nature of Personal Identity | Floridi (2011), DOI-10.1007/s11023-011-9259-6. Floridi deflates the diachronic question of personal identity, holding that sameness is always relative to a purpose-fixed level of abstraction and that metaphysical versions of the question "do not deserve to be taken seriously" (p. 554). He puts the synchronic question first: what binds a self's information into a unity. His answer is an admittedly "figurative" 3C model in which the self is the last of three successive encapsulations, corporeal (bonded by chemical bonds), cognitive (bonded by mutual information) and conscious (bonded by semantics, and so dependent on a community). He then reads ICTs as "egopoietic" technologies because they act on all three membranes. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-130](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-130.md)); worth reading as the programmatic statement of Floridi's informational, level-of-abstraction approach to the self and its link to his information ethics; its central model is a self-described figure, not an argued theory, and the individuation-first thesis is asserted in a few lines. |
| [LIT-137](../../record/literature.d/LIT-137.md) v2 | Philosophy of Fame and Celebrity | Robb et al. (2024), DOI-10.5040/9781350401327. The first introductory collection on the philosophy of fame and celebrity, arguing that work on admiration, achievement, epistemic authority, virtue and moral psychology can be brought to bear on what fame is and how it shapes how we live. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and the volume could not be obtained (OpenAlex lists it closed; Crossref shows no licence; the introduction is behind a Bloomsbury Collections login). One chapter, Widdows & MacCallum on "Instafame", has an accepted version on the Warwick repository, but one chapter is not the volume. It stays Deferred until the volume is available. |
| [LIT-138](../../record/literature.d/LIT-138.md) v2 | The Depth of the Body | Register (2025), DOI-10.3998/phimp.7576. Register argues that body parthood "for moral purposes" is bounded below by depth. Depth is the microphysical commitment of an object's real definition, and objects that are "too deep" (a water molecule, the parcel of matter now composing the heart, a particular cloud server) are not body parts, though they are parts in the ordinary sense. His two principles ("bodily interests/rights are high-level") say that events which are high-level neutral do not bear on bodily rights or interests. So a BCI user's implemented software can be a body part owned by the user while the third-party server that runs it is not. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-151](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-151.md)); worth reading as a short, clearly argued proposal that separates a moral "body-part" relation from ordinary parthood by level of abstraction, and applies it to cloud-hosted BCIs. It is an argument from cases with the key ontological step hedged, not a worked-out mereology. |
| [LIT-139](../../record/literature.d/LIT-139.md) v2 | Inductive Risk and Values in Science | Douglas (2000), DOI-10.1086/392855. Hempel's "inductive risk" (the chance of wrongly accepting or rejecting a hypothesis) arises at three internal stages of science: choosing a methodology such as a significance level, characterising data, and interpreting results. Wherever the non-epistemic consequences of error can be foreseen, weighing them requires non-epistemic values. Douglas shows this on dioxin cancer studies. Three pathologist panels (1978, 1980, 1990) scored the same Kociba rat-liver slides differently (e.g. 34/50, 33/47, 18/50 tumours at 100 ng/kg/day). Choosing between threshold and no-threshold readings of the same data has meant regulatory limits about 100× apart. Values enter only indirectly, through the consequences of error, never as evidence that a claim is true. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-145](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-145.md)); worth reading as the canonical, compact statement of the modern inductive-risk argument. It is carefully scoped, with an explicit direct/indirect distinction and exemptions for negligible uncertainty and consequence-free research, and it is well illustrated. Its normative premise, that scientists bear moral responsibility for foreseeable consequences of error, is explicitly left unargued (§2, p. 563). |
| [LIT-140](../../record/literature.d/LIT-140.md) v2 | Large Language Models Are Stochastic Measuring Devices | Mallory (2025), DOI-10.1093/9780191998317.003.0003. The chapter argues by analogy that deep neural networks are "stochastic measuring devices". Training alters the parameters in response to data, so that the resulting configuration carries information about the data ("training is measurement"). The trained weights are a model, and generation is simulation. DNNs are stochastic because random initialisation and non-convex loss mean runs need not converge to the same weights, failing Baird's "regularity" condition. The problems of interpreting model states and generated text are therefore the familiar problems of instruments, models and simulations. The problem of interpretable AI is an instance of the coordination problem in the philosophy of measurement. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-090](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-090.md)); worth reading as the most developed instrument-centred alternative to agentive framings of LLMs, and for its tidy division into model-interpretation, text-interpretation and linking problems; it offers an analogy, not a refutation (its own words, § Interpretation and Communication), and should be read in the published OUP version where possible. |
| [LIT-141](../../record/literature.d/LIT-141.md) v3 | Emergence: from physics to biology, sociology, and computer science | McKenzie (2025), [ARXIV-2508.08548](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08548). Sole-authored survey by a condensed-matter physicist. It takes novelty (a property the parts lack) as the defining mark of emergence and sets it against twelve other characteristics in §2. The six "objective" ones are discontinuities, order, modification of parts, universality, diversity and mesoscale modularity. The six "subjective" ones are self-organisation, unpredictability, irreducibility, contextuality/downward causation, complexity and intra-stratum closure. Case studies from the Ising model to Schelling segregation and LLMs show that each can come apart from novelty: novelty without discontinuity (Kondo, Fermi liquids, spin ice), without unpredictability (BKT, spin ice), and with reduction (Butterfield). §22 sides with weak emergence and argues that strong-emergence claims from molecular structure are weak. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-124); worth reading as a hub reference and example catalogue on emergence from physics outward, with unusually concrete case studies and a sceptical physicist's §15 on molecular structure. It is a survey and a position, not a proof. Its definitional framework is stipulated and its logical relations between characteristics are left open by its own account (§24). |
| [LIT-142](../../record/literature.d/LIT-142.md) v2 | One or two? A Process View of pregnancy | Meincke (2021), DOI-10.1007/s11098-021-01716-y. Meincke argues that substance ontology forces a binary count of individuals. The Containment View fails empirically, following Kingma. Kingma's Parthood View either loses foster–neonate identity ("Parts-of-Substances") or cannot count consistently ("Substances-as-Parts"). If organisms are autopoietic processes, a pregnant individual is instead an asymmetrically bifurcating "hypercomplex" process. The foster is "something in between zero and one" and the whole is "something in between one and two", and the bifurcation runs on past birth through lactation ("postnatal pregnancy"). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-115](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-115.md)); worth reading as the clearest short worked case of process ontology changing a counting and parthood question, and for its sharp internal critique of Kingma's Parthood View (§3.2). Its positive view is a programmatic sketch, as the paper itself says (fn 52), and it rests on five process-biology premises that are assumed rather than argued here. |
| [LIT-143](../../record/literature.d/LIT-143.md) v2 | What is a Mathematical Structure of Conscious Experience? | Kleiner & Ludwig (2023), [ARXIV-2301.11812](https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.11812). The paper proposes a definition, (MSC). A mathematical structure S = ((A_i), (S_j)) is a structure *of* conscious experience iff (S1) its domains are sets of experiential aspects, and (S2) every relation or function S_j has an "S_j-aspect". An S_j-aspect is an aspect that a variation of experience changes exactly when the variation fails to preserve S_j. The older condition (MDC), that a structure is "of" experience if its domains are aspects and its axioms hold, survives only as the necessary condition for *describing* experience, because as a sufficient condition it admits incompatible structures (discrete and non-discrete topologies on the same set), arbitrary re-definitions (every C·d for C > 0) and consciousness-indifferent structures. Worked examples: experienced relative similarity is a strict partial order that satisfies (MSC), given transitivity (assumed). Visual regions form a topology that satisfies (MSC), given Bayne and Chalmers's subsumptive unity thesis. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-135); worth reading as the most explicit criterion for when a formal model is *of* experience rather than a convenient description of it. Its two positive results are conditional: transitivity of relative similarity is assumed, and the topology result assumes the subsumptive unity thesis. Cite the Synthese version, which has dropped v1's argument against metric structure. |
| [LIT-144](../../record/literature.d/LIT-144.md) v2 | Causal Exclusion and Downward Counterfactuals | Pernu (2015), DOI-10.1007/s10670-015-9781-7. The counterfactual theory of causation can answer the causal exclusion problem without being committed to downward causation, provided the semantics of counterfactuals is handled carefully. The exclusion problem itself, however, comes in several distinct forms. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and no full text could be obtained (Springer paywalled on both hosts; OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar list it closed; not among the articles of Pernu's open 2013 dissertation in HELDA, though its §5.2 covers the counterfactual response at an earlier stage; PhilPapers, ResearchGate and Academia.edu refused). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-145](../../record/literature.d/LIT-145.md) v2 | Ultrafilters as Propositional Theories | Turner (2025), DOI-10.1111/phc3.70047. An expository piece that redefines a filter over a set W as a "propositional theory" (a set of W-propositions closed under pairwise conjunction and implication, not containing ∅), proves maximal ⇔ prime ⇔ ultra (Thm 2), the ultrafilter extension theorem as a propositional Lindenbaum's lemma (Thm 3, assuming choice), Łoś's theorem M_T,[A] ⊨ φ iff ⟦φ⟧_A ∈ T for ultraproducts of "intensional spaces" (Thm 9), and first-order compactness from it (Thm 12); "None of the results are novel" (p. 2). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-177](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-177.md)); worth reading as the most accessible philosopher's route into ultrafilters and Łoś's theorem, provided the reader supplies the fixes to a few slips in the proofs (listed under Limitations). |
| [LIT-146](../../record/literature.d/LIT-146.md) v2 | The Moral Status of Animals | Gruen & Monsó (2003), <https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/moral-animal/>. The entry reviews five proposed grounds of moral considerability (species membership, human-exceptional capacities, personhood, sentience and agency). Its conclusion is that attempts to establish human uniqueness by capacities are "not the most promising" (§1.2). It then separates *having* moral status from the *weight* of a claim ("showing up on a moral radar screen", §2.2). Weight depends on the value theory adopted for animal interests (hedonism, preference satisfaction, objective list; §2.1) and on the normative framework (rights, which are absolute but generate forward-looking duties when violated, or a multi-factor utilitarianism). It closes with virtue-ethical, feminist, relational and empathy-based approaches that reject argument from capacities altogether (§3). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-181](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-181.md)); worth reading as the standard current survey of the grounds and weighing of animals' moral status. It is balanced for most of its length, but §2.2 argues at length for a multi-factor utilitarian view and §3 ends on Gruen's own "entangled empathy", so cite it for the map, not as neutral on the weighing. |
| [LIT-147](../../record/literature.d/LIT-147.md) v3 | Mental causation via neuroprosthetics? A critical analysis | Pernu (2018), DOI-10.1007/s11229-018-1713-z. The interventionist/difference-making case for nonreductive mental causation from BCIs (List & Menzies 2009; Menzies 2015; Woodward 2008a,b, 2017) misplaces multiple realisation. What the ensemble recordings show is that one cortical-level variable N (the ensemble's average firing) is realised by many single-neuron patterns N1, N2. Each intention M corresponds to one value of N. So the counterfactual contrast "∼M □→ ∼B true, ∼N □→ ∼B false" fails, and neuroprosthetics supports mental causation only by identifying M with N, not nonreductive physicalism. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-091](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-091.md)); Worth reading as a short, precise diagnosis of a specific equivocation in the interventionist BCI argument (multiple realisation at the wrong level). Its scope is limited to difference-making with a proportionality constraint (fn 1), and its identity conclusion rests on a simplified picture of neural decoding that it asserts rather than argues. |
| [LIT-148](../../record/literature.d/LIT-148.md) | Computational Functionalism for the Deep Learning Era | López-Rubio (2018), DOI-10.1007/s11023-018-9480-7. Deep networks' success comes from extracting successively higher-level features across multiple layers. López-Rubio argues that this calls for a new version of computational functionalism, keyed to deep neural computation rather than to classic program-based functional organization. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and found the article paywalled, with no open copy (the Málaga repository has none, and PhilPapers refused the request). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-149](../../record/literature.d/LIT-149.md) v2 | The information-processing perspective on representation | Martínez (2025), DOI-10.33735/phimisci.2025.11321. A position paper, not an argument to a conclusion. It proposes that representations are signals adapted to transmit information for its own sake (Transmission), and that the evidence for this is efficiency in trading off rate, distortion and coder complexity (RDC Trade-Off). It supports the proposal by showing that cognitive scientists already attribute representational status on those grounds: compression, error protection, and explicitness understood as ease of decoding. It proves no theorem and reports no data of its own. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-165](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-165.md)); worth reading as the clearest short statement of a rate–distortion–complexity (teleo)semantics of representation, provided it is read as the position paper it says it is (§1: "comparatively light" on objections); the defence is in Martínez 2019b/2024 and in work the paper says is to come. |
| [LIT-150](../../record/literature.d/LIT-150.md) v3 | What is emergence, after all? | Rizi (2025), [ARXIV-2507.04951](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.04951). A perspective, not an argument with results. Rizi proposes that emergence is present when a many-to-one coarse-graining F (Eq. 1, N′ < N) keeps the macro description predictive after most micro detail is discarded. He says the onset of emergence is located by order parameters, diverging susceptibility and correlation length, and RG fixed points, and that dualities show macro behaviour is not dictated by micro "stuff". Herd immunity on networks (percolation, the S–R interface density ρ_SR) is offered as a social case. The paper then asserts that science needs only weak emergence, and that invoking strong emergence "steps outside the scope of scientific method". | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-153); worth reading as a compact, well-referenced statement of the physicist's weak-emergence view and a reading list into it (Carroll & Parola, Hoel, Rosas, Witten, De Haro & Butterfield, Dennett). Its framework is stated, not developed: no distortion measure is given for F, and "predictive" is never quantified. |
| [LIT-151](../../record/literature.d/LIT-151.md) v2 | Stating Structural Realism: Mathematics-First Approaches to Physics and Metaphysics | Wallace (2021), DOI-10.1111/phpe.12172. Wallace argues that reading a physical theory "math-first", as mathematical models plus representation rather than as sentences aiming at truth, already makes scientific realism structural. Math-first theoretical equivalence and reduction (as instantiation) are coarse-grained enough to dissolve the standard underdetermination and theory-change objections. On this footing epistemic and ontic structural realism differ only in whether metaphysics itself is language-first or math-first. On math-first OSR, objects are whatever the best "predicate precisification" of a theory quantifies over, real patterns are defined through instantiation plus best precisification, and ontology is scale-relative. At every non-fundamental level the two versions coincide. The paper is explicitly a statement of the position, not a defence of it. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-142](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-142.md)); worth reading as the sharpest statement of what structural realism could mean once theories are taken math-first. It gives precise definitions of math-first ESR/OSR and of real patterns, its diagnosis of why priority-thesis OSR drifted is useful, and it is candid about the undeveloped notion of mathematical equivalence it relies on. |
| [LIT-152](../../record/literature.d/LIT-152.md) v2 | A defence of informational structural realism | Floridi (2007), DOI-10.1007/s11229-007-9163-z. Floridi argues informally in four steps. (1) ESR and (non-eliminativist) OSR are compatible once indexed to levels of abstraction. ESR's minimal commitment to knowable relations holds at a first-order LoA. At a reflective ("transcendental") second-order LoA, a second Ockham principle licenses taking the relata to be structural objects. (2) The relata-need-relations regress is blocked by Eddington's "package hypothesis": not all relata are logically prior to relations. (3) Structural objects are informational objects, clusters of data understood as concrete "differentiae de re", made scalable by the object-oriented-programming model. (4) Together these give "informational structural realism", on which "the ultimate nature of reality is informational" (p. 32). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-099](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-099.md)); worth reading as the source statement of informational structural realism, which the literature cites and builds on (Karpenko [LIT-215](../../record/literature.d/LIT-215.md), Bynum, Beni, Long, Wheeler). Read §§2.4, 3 and 5 for the position and §6.9 for what "informational" does and does not mean. The LoA reconciliation is a proposal, not a result. |
| [LIT-153](../../record/literature.d/LIT-153.md) v2 | Making sense of transformer success | Angius et al. (2025), DOI-10.3389/frai.2025.1509338. Current attempts to explain transformer language models' competence fall into three kinds, and the paper maps each onto an explanatory form from philosophy of cognitive science: machine psychology is Cummins-style functional analysis, induction-head work is Machamer–Darden–Craver mechanistic explanation, and NLM–fMRI brain-score studies are a bidirectional "co-simulation". Its only thesis is that these do not differ from how cognitive science explains humans. Functional analyses are to be reduced to mechanisms as "mechanism sketches". The paper rests on existing literature and adds no new analysis or data. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-106](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-106.md)); worth reading only as a philosopher's map of interpretability methods sorted by explanation type, and for the "co-simulation" label; the argument is thin, the Transformer primer contains a technical error, and a reader wanting the underlying results should go to the cited primary work (Elhage et al. 2021, Olsson et al. 2022, Caucheteux et al.). |
| [LIT-154](../../record/literature.d/LIT-154.md) v3 | Criteria of Identity and Structuralist Ontology | Leitgeb & Ladyman (2007), DOI-10.1093/philmat/nkm039. Graph theory counts unlabelled graphs whose nodes, or whose isomorphic connected components, cannot be told apart even by an irreflexive relation. Examples are the edgeless two-node graph G′ and any graph with two or more isolated nodes (11 of the 156 unlabelled 6-node graphs). Mathematicians treat these as determinately distinct objects, so the authors conclude that the identity or difference of places in a structure is given by the structure itself, with identity counted among its relations, and not by any criterion of identity. The argument appeals to mathematical practice; it is not a proof. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-133](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-133.md)); worth reading as the short, standard counterexample to weak-discernibility versions of the PII for mathematical structuralism. It is widely cited, and its graph cases are simple enough to check by hand. Its physics coda is explicitly left open. |
| [LIT-155](../../record/literature.d/LIT-155.md) v2 | Studying Philosophy Does Make People Better Thinkers | Prinzing & Vazquez (2025), DOI-10.1017/apa.2025.10007. In HERI/CIRP freshman–senior survey data (N = 649,511 students at 804 US institutions, 4,843 philosophy majors), one SD more SAT Verbal raises the odds of majoring in philosophy by 57% (OR 1.57), and freshman Habits of Mind by 34%. After adjusting for SAT or freshman scores, philosophy majors still score higher than non-majors on GRE Verbal (+33 points, SMD 0.48), LSAT (+2, SMD 0.35), Habits of Mind (SMD .24) and Pluralistic Orientation (SMD .21), with no difference on GRE Quantitative. On point estimates they rank first of 57 or 62 majors on GRE Verbal, LSAT and Habits of Mind. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-167](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-167.md)); worth reading as the largest baseline-adjusted observational study of what studying philosophy does, and it is candid about its design; but its causal claim rests on covariate adjustment with self-reported scores, and a reader should take "philosophy causes better thinking" as well supported for verbal-test gains and weakly supported beyond that. |
| [LIT-156](../../record/literature.d/LIT-156.md) v4 | An Emerging Dilemma for Reciprocal Causation | Hazelwood (2023), DOI-10.1017/psa.2023.124. Hazelwood defines RC as "the process of natural selection is both a cause and effect of processes such as niche construction" and EC as "natural selection is an emergent causal process". He argues they are incompatible. Emergence here is Wilson's synchronic dependence plus autonomy, cashed out as Pence's composition. If EC holds, niche construction partly composes selection, so the two fail Woodward's independent-fixability condition and cannot stand in a causal relation. If EC fails, selection is either non-causal (statisticalism) or individual-level, which is incoherent because relative fitness needs a population (Millstein, Sober). EES proponents should drop RC but can keep niche construction as a genuine cause of the evolutionary *product*. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-137](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-137.md)); worth reading as a tight, well-sourced argument that brings the reciprocal-causation debate into the composition, exclusion and interventionism literature. Its force depends on accepting a process/product split for selection and a compositional reading of realization, both argued briefly and flagged as contestable. |
| [LIT-157](../../record/literature.d/LIT-157.md) v3 | Reproducees, reproducers, and Darwinian individuals | Bourrat (2025), DOI-10.1007/s11229-024-04880-w. Bourrat builds a nested vocabulary. A reproducee is a B that comes after A, is caused by A, and is of A's relevant class. A reproducer is a reproducee with reproductive autonomy, meaning it can reproduce across a range of backgrounds. A Darwinian individual is a reproducer with high reproductive causal control, meaning heredity survives as the parent's description is made finer. On that vocabulary, genes of multicellular organisms are, like hearts, mere reproducees, but with high control. Material overlap is necessary for reproducers, which are whole life cycles, and unnecessary for reproducees, so Griesemer and Godfrey-Smith partly talk past each other. Both quantities get an information-theoretic recipe (conditional MI after Bertschinger et al. 2008 for autonomy; interventional MI across grains, Griffiths et al.'s INF, for control), and neither is computed. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-100](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-100.md)); worth reading as the clearest statement of the reproduced/reproducing distinction and a tidy, well-scoped settlement of the material-overlap dispute. Its value is conceptual. Its two measures are recipes that are never computed, and the autonomy recipe does not match its own verbal definition. |
| [LIT-158](../../record/literature.d/LIT-158.md) v2 | Status Distrust of Scientific Experts | Desmond (2022), DOI-10.1080/02691728.2022.2104758. Desmond gives a "how-possibly" argument, not a demonstration, that stubborn distrust of scientific experts can be valid reasoning. A low-status truster who is vulnerable in collective decision-making (Premise 1) and has reason to think the prevailing "service-for-prestige" ethos leaves out their core interests (Premise 2) has reason to distrust a high-prestige expert because of that prestige (§6, p. 16–17). The paper explicitly declines to say when those premises are true. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-113](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-113.md)); worth reading as a compact, clearly structured rationalising account of distrust in experts that connects the trust literature (Baier, Hawley) with the sociology of professions and prestige; its §6 argument scheme is the citable part, but it shows only validity, not soundness. |
| [LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md) v4 | If materialism is true, the United States is probably conscious | Schwitzgebel (2014), DOI-10.1007/s11098-014-0387-8. A conditional, informal argument. Any materialist criterion liberal enough to credit rabbits (and rabbit-level distributed aliens, the "squidbits") and behaviourally human-like but oddly built aliens (Sirian supersquids, Antarean antheads) with consciousness also credits the United States. On this argument the US meets the usual materialist marks of consciousness: sophisticated information processing (roughly 10^14 person-to-person visual "connections", comparable to the brain's about 10^14 synapses), goal-directed environmental responsiveness, self-representation and historical embedding. The only exits are eliminativism, extreme sparseness or neurochauvinism, each costlier than the conclusion. The paper argues for no universal criterion. It ends undecided between three readings: US consciousness, a challenge to materialists, or grounds to distrust universal metaphysics of mind. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-131](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-131.md)); read in full (manuscript) 2026-09-26; the canonical over-generation argument against liberal materialist criteria of consciousness, and the standard critique of anti-nesting and exclusion principles, worth reading for anyone who attributes consciousness from functional or indicator properties. |
| [LIT-160](../../record/literature.d/LIT-160.md) v2 | Units and Levels of Selection | Lloyd (2005), <https://plato.stanford.edu/ENTRIES/selection-units/>. Lloyd's own four-question anatomy of "unit of selection": interactor, reproducer (formerly replicator), manifestor of adaptation, and beneficiary. She combines it with a split between two multilevel schools, Wrightian "Evolutionary Change" and Fisherian "Adaptationist/KS". She uses the two to argue for a verdict, not merely to map the debates. The verdict is that most rejections of group, species and holobiont selection come from demanding that an interactor also be a manifestor of *engineering* adaptation. By her account, genic pluralism, the ETI models and Godfrey-Smith's Darwinian populations each fuse roles that should be kept apart. The support is argument plus citation of Wade, Goodnight and others. The entry reports no new results of its own. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-184](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-184.md)); worth reading as the authoritative statement of the interactor / reproducer / manifestor / beneficiary distinction and as a dense map of the units-of-selection literature to 2023. It has to be read as one side's case. It is not a neutral survey: it argues for the Evolutionary Change school, and it criticises by name several authors this record holds or cites. |
| [LIT-161](../../record/literature.d/LIT-161.md) v2 | Why Replication Is Overrated | Feest (2019), DOI-10.1086/705451. Every experiment and every replication rests on individuation judgments about what is being manipulated and measured ("conceptual scope"). So direct replication can at most help rule out random error, and "perhaps" establish that some effect exists but not which effect, while conceptual replication begs the question by presupposing that two operationalisations individuate the same effect. What productive research actually does, as in the Mozart-effect debate, is probe systematic errors, which is better called exploration than replication. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-196](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-196.md)); worth reading as a short, clearly argued statement of why replication cannot settle "what effect?" questions, built on one worked case (the Mozart effect). It is suggestive for construct-validity arguments about benchmarks, but its strongest lines are asserted rather than argued. |
| [LIT-162](../../record/literature.d/LIT-162.md) v2 | Is Unsupervised Clustering Somehow Truer? | Søgaard (2024), DOI-10.1007/s11023-024-09699-5. Søgaard rebuts two arguments that unsupervised clustering is epistemically superior to supervised classification. The No-Bias Argument fails because clustering is also sensitive to sample bias (a toy example moves the boundary from x = 7 to x = 6), and it is more sensitive to representation bias. The Simplicity-Truth Argument fails because clustering is also function estimation and overfits in the same way (Hansen & Larsen 1996). From the fact that every clustering hypothesis is in a supervised classifier's hypothesis class, he concludes that supervised classification is "at least as justified", but that positive step is under-argued. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-108](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-108.md)); worth reading as a short corrective to Rochefort-Maranda & Liu's claim that unsupervised clustering cannot overfit and to "no-bias" readings of Watson. Its positive thesis rests on a thin hypothesis-class reduction and has several technical slips, so it should be read critically rather than cited for that thesis. |
| [LIT-163](../../record/literature.d/LIT-163.md) v2 | Philosophy of Probability: Foundations, Epistemology, and Computation | Wenmackers (2011), <https://philpapers.org/rec/WENPOP>. A fair lottery on ℕ gets a uniform, regular probability on all of 𝒫(ℕ) by normalising Benci–Di Nasso numerosities, P_num(A) = num(A)/α. This is hyperrational-valued, determined only up to the choice of free ultrafilter, and "hypercountably" (really hyperfinitely) additive rather than countably additive, with st∘P_num = generalised asymptotic density (§2.5–2.6). The same "rounding-error" diagnosis gives Stratified Belief, a contextual Lockean thesis on which conjunction holds for a standard number of beliefs (Ch. 3). A separate agent-based model (Ch. 5) finds that one round of bounded-confidence averaging sends an agent to the inconsistent theory with probability at most 1.81 % in the cases computed (M = 2, 3 atomic sentences). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-139](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-139.md)); worth reading for the Ch. 1 survey of interpretations of probability and the Ch. 4 synthesis, which appear only here; for the rest, each chapter has a later journal version that is the better citation, none of them held on this record: Wenmackers & Horsten, "Fair infinite lotteries", Synthese 190 (2013) 37–61 (Ch. 2); Wenmackers, "Ultralarge lotteries", J. Applied Logic 11 (2013) 452–467 (Ch. 3); Wenmackers, Vanpoucke & Douven, "Probability of inconsistencies in theory revision", Eur. Phys. J. B 85 (2012) 44 (Ch. 5); and Benci, Horsten & Wenmackers, "Non-Archimedean probability", Milan J. Math. 81 (2013) 121–151 (the Ch. 6 outlook). |
| [LIT-164](../../record/literature.d/LIT-164.md) v3 | Wave-Function Realism and the Scope of Physical Causal Closure | Poltorak (2026), <https://philsci-archive.pitt.edu/30720/>. A closure principle has two tacit parameters, its domain and its completeness relation (causal, dynamical or explanatory). Wave-function realism falsifies spatiotemporal-domain closure (STC) but is compatible with fundamental-physical closure (FPC), so there is no "trans-domain causal breach". The physicalist is left with a dilemma: STC is too narrow, and FPC needs an independent criterion of physicality plus a dynamical-to-causal bridge. The paper tests this across primitive-ontology, spacetime-state, multi-field, Everettian, nomological and dispositional readings. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-112](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-112.md)); worth reading as the clearest statement of what quantum ontology does and does not do to causal-closure arguments. Its conclusions are modest, and it concedes that its main target is a picture rather than a published argument. Unrefereed. |
| [LIT-165](../../record/literature.d/LIT-165.md) v2 | Coherence-based measures of explanatory power | Hartmann & Trpin (2026), DOI-10.1007/s11098-026-02517-x. If explanatory power is measured contrastively over sets of explanantia, as E_CohOG+(E; H) = (Coh(H∪{E}) − Coh(H∪{¬E})) / (Coh(H∪{E}) + Coh(H∪{¬E})) with Olsson–Glass overlap normalised by its independence value (CohOG+), then the measure provably satisfies Roche & Sober's Two Likelihood Inequalities for single hypotheses (Prop. 2) and violates the One Likelihood Inequality (OLI). Two numerical examples show that adding a distal cause (for P(H1) < .7) or a negatively interacting cause (for P(H1) = P(H2) < .75) can raise explanatory power. Both are existence demonstrations, not general theorems. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-168](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-168.md)); worth reading as a current, compact reply to Roche & Sober (2023) and Lange (2022) with checkable formulas, though its escape is representational (sets instead of conjunctions) and its positive case rests on two worked examples rather than a characterisation. |
| [LIT-166](../../record/literature.d/LIT-166.md) v2 | Sacrificing Humans for Insects and AI: A Critical Review of Jonathan Birch, The Edge of Sentience, Jeff Sebo, The Moral Circle, and Webb Keane, Animals, Robots, Gods | Schwitzgebel & Sinnott-Armstrong (2026), DOI-10.1086/739660. Each book's own principles, read at face value, can make human interests outweighed by insects in aggregate or by near-future AI. Each author then retreats to moderate near-term advice without principled grounds: Birch through human-centred citizens' panels, Sebo through contingent "relational" and "co-beneficial" considerations, Keane through deference to slowly adapting cultures. The reviewers defend only the conditional ("given recent science, common grounds of moral standing put human priority under serious threat") and "take no stand on ponens vs. tollens" (§1, p. 6; §5). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-116](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-116.md)); worth reading as the sharpest short map of how the AI- and animal-sentience literature argues. It covers precaution and its ambiguity, the edges of the "zone of reasonable disagreement", aggregation, and the retreat to moderation. It also gives a close critique of Birch's sentience-candidate criterion, the one operational test that already covers AI. |
| [LIT-167](../../record/literature.d/LIT-167.md) v3 | The Organism | Baedke (2025), DOI-10.1017/9781009495035. Baedke treats "organism" as a nexus concept with an organizational dimension and a relational dimension, joined by the various kinds of biological individuality. He argues that the standard "persister" (self-maintenance, closure) criterion cannot single organisms out, because colonies, holobionts and ecosystems meet it too, and that organisms are also "overcomers", able to destabilize and reorganize their own closure. Organisms are then individuated relationally by tracing diachronic causal paths in the unrolled O/E model of Baedke et al. 2021 ([LIT-110](../../record/literature.d/LIT-110.md)). The overcomer claim rests on three illustrative cases, anglerfish, sea slug and jumping ant, and is asserted, not shown, to hold of organisms only (p. 40). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-148](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-148.md)); worth reading as a compact, open-access and historically well-informed synthesis of the organism/individuality debate, and as the clearest statement of the persister-vs-overcomer distinction. Its positive account is, in the author's words, drawn "with rough brushstrokes" (p. 51), so cite it for the problem statement and the distinction, not as having settled organism individuation. |
| [LIT-168](../../record/literature.d/LIT-168.md) v3 | Biological Individuals | Wilson & Barker (2007), <https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/biology-individual/>. A survey that maps biological individuals by crossing two axes: organism / some parts of organisms / some groups of organisms, against living agent (physiological) / Darwinian (evolutionary) individual. The result is nine regions, each given one example (fruit fly, gene, eusocial colony, lysosome, clade, heart, coral reef, virus, squid+Vibrio). "Part" is never defined, and the entry gives no criterion for *which* parts or groups count: the placements are the authors' tentative judgements, "moving from less contentious to more contentious examples" (§7). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-114](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-114.md)); worth reading as the standard map of the biological-individuality literature up to 2023 and the hub for this record's cluster ([LIT-049](../../record/literature.d/LIT-049.md), [LIT-157](../../record/literature.d/LIT-157.md), [LIT-188](../../record/literature.d/LIT-188.md), [LIT-142](../../record/literature.d/LIT-142.md), [LIT-210](../../record/literature.d/LIT-210.md)); it is a survey with one tentative framework of its own, not an argument for a thesis, and for parthood it only supplies examples. |
| [LIT-169](../../record/literature.d/LIT-169.md) v2 | Defining the niche for niche construction: evolutionary and ecological niches | Trappes (2021), DOI-10.1007/s10539-021-09805-2. Trappes compares NCT's evolutionary niche with the ecological niche. The evolutionary niche is the sum of selection pressures on a population (Odling-Smee, Laland & Feldman 2003), read "externally" as the environmental factors that produce fitness differences. The ecological niche is the conditions that permit continued existence; for a population, mean absolute fitness ≥ 1. Even at their closest, against the realized population niche, the two differ in extension. Habitat degradation is in the evolutionary niche but not the ecological one, and commensalism and non-limiting resources are in the ecological niche but not the evolutionary one. So NCT's niche is not the "simple translation" of Hutchinson's niche its proponents claim, and the evolutionary significance of niche construction must be shown case by case rather than following from the definition. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-155](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-155.md)); worth reading as a short, clean conceptual analysis that turns "persistence versus fitness differences" into a precise wedge between two fields' uses of "niche"; its empirical cases are admittedly thin (the CO2 case is offered as "possible"), and it analyses only the 2003 book's niche concept. |
| [LIT-170](../../record/literature.d/LIT-170.md) v2 | Unitary inequivalence as a problem for structural realism | French (2010), DOI-10.1016/j.shpsb.2011.09.001. French answers Howard's objection that QFT's unitarily inequivalent representations leave the structural realist with non-isomorphic, competing structures. He offers two routes. The first is to adopt cut-off "conventional" QFT with Wallace, on which the inequivalence is an artefact or harmless. The second is to follow Ruetsche's Swiss Army Knife and treat the algebra as a modal structure spanning all representations. On that reading spontaneous symmetry breaking preserves the symmetry across the degenerate vacua, and the DHR analysis derives quantum statistics from the algebra. Adding Baker's criterion, on which a symmetry mapping between representations suffices for intertranslatability, removes the assumption behind the objection, and French ends with a third option that combines the two routes. Every step is an informal argument layered on others' technical results; the paper proves nothing itself. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-162](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-162.md)); worth reading as a leading ontic structural realist's worked response to the strongest physics-based objection to OSR beyond finite-dimensional QM, and for its clear staging of the Wallace–Fraser and algebraic versus Hilbert-space debates. A reader should cite the 2012 journal version; this note reads the 2010 draft. |
| [LIT-171](../../record/literature.d/LIT-171.md) | The epistemology of accurate credences | Pettigrew (2025), <https://philpapers.org/rec/PETTEO-44>. Accuracy-first epistemology — scoring credences by their closeness to the truth (following Joyce 1999) — gives a purely epistemic foundation for Probabilism, Conditionalization and the Principal Principle, and extends to non-ideal agents, the value of inquiry, and collective credences. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and failed on access, not availability: the paper is open on PhilArchive, which the author's homepages link to, but PhilArchive refused automated requests and no other host was found. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied or PhilArchive can be reached. |
| [LIT-172](../../record/literature.d/LIT-172.md) v2 | Ethics of Parasocial Relationships | Archer & Robb (2025), DOI-10.1093/9780198928188.003.0012. Fan–celebrity parasocial relationships are personal relationships (the fan relates to the celebrity as a unique individual, with reasons for partiality), built from three structural asymmetries: attention, communication and epistemic. These yield an "identity asymmetry": celebrities mostly *direct* fans' identities, while fans mostly *interpret* celebrities'. Fans who refuse uptake of a celebrity's changed public persona commit a failure of Darwallian recognition respect. The chapter derives from this a responsibility for fans, which is mitigated when the celebrity's self-presentation is misleading or ambiguous. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-186](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-186.md)); worth reading as the clearest short philosophical analysis of parasociality as a relationship type. Its directive/interpretive split and its recognition-respect argument are portable to other one-sided relationships, though the chapter itself only gestures at them. |
| [LIT-173](../../record/literature.d/LIT-173.md) v2 | From ontic structural realism to metaphysical coherentism | Morganti (2018), DOI-10.1007/s13194-018-0233-5. Morganti argues from a survey rather than proving anything. Strong, moderate and contextualist OSR each fail as metaphysics: particle kinds have intrinsic features no group-theoretic analysis reduces, and the argument from permutation invariance is inconclusive. What OSR gets right about symmetry, entanglement and particle kinds is better captured by "metaphysical coherentism": object-like entities (in one domain) stand in symmetric *partial* ontological dependence on one another. Cycles of such dependence can be kept irreflexive, and so explanatorily non-trivial, by relaxing transitivity (quasi-transitivity) or by making only partial dependence symmetric. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-095](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-095.md)); worth reading as the clearest statement of a dependence-based, object-friendly alternative to relation-first OSR, and a hub for the grounding, holism and OSR literatures; its physics applications are programmatic, and he says so himself (pp. 18–19). |
| [LIT-174](../../record/literature.d/LIT-174.md) | Having Their Say: Athletes and Entertainers and the Ethics of Speaking Out | Bunton (2021), <https://philpapers.org/rec/BUNHTS>. Argues that celebrities' speaking out on public issues is justifiable only when speakers, journalists and audiences attend to platform, intent and harm, because digital audiences treat celebrity as authority. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and found no reachable copy: the book has no open or repository version, and the publisher and Google Books show only a description and a limited preview. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-175](../../record/literature.d/LIT-175.md) v2 | Evidence in Medicine and Evidence-Based Medicine | Worrall (2007), DOI-10.1111/j.1747-9991.2007.00106.x. Worrall argues that the EBM evidence hierarchy has no sound general rationale for giving randomisation extra epistemic weight, even though it lets high-quality RCTs "trump any number of observational studies". He examines five standard arguments. Four fail: (a) Fisher's significance-test logic, (b) control of unknown confounders, (d) that observational studies "exaggerate" effects, and (e) probabilistic causality. The fifth, (c) selection bias, is a "cast-iron argument", but it supports only taking allocation out of the clinicians' hands, and randomisation is one way to do that. What matters is balance on plausible confounders, a placebo or active control and blinding. Whether balance comes from randomising or from matching "seems epistemically unimportant", and historically controlled trials can give strong evidence for large effects. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-126](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-126.md)); worth reading as the fullest single statement of the philosophical case against the special epistemic standing of randomisation. It is clearly argued and well documented, and it explicitly concedes the selection-bias argument. It is a partisan position piece, and its defenders-of-randomisation opponents (Pocock, Elbourne, Papineau) are summarised, not engaged at length. |
| [LIT-176](../../record/literature.d/LIT-176.md) v2 | Events and the Ontology of Quantum Mechanics | Dorato (2015), [ARXIV-1503.01140](https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.01140). Dorato argues, without formal results, that events are the metaphysical category best suited to quantum mechanics. An event reading fits GRW flashes, GRW matter density and Bohmian worldlines, and also the antirealist views of ψ held by Bohr, Rovelli, Haag and Pashby. Because it is the same across interpretations, he calls it "interpretation-independent" (p. 12). Where ψ needs a realist reading, it should be read as intrinsic dispositions of events. Esfeld et al.'s view has the opposite structure: bare matter points plus a single holistic power of the universal configuration. Dorato argues that view is unintelligible, and that it must change its ontology when it passes from the universal wave function to the effective one (pp. 13–15). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-105); worth reading as a short, clear statement of event-primitivism for primitive-ontology theories, and of intrinsic (not holistic) dispositions against Esfeld et al. It is programmatic. By Dorato's own account (p. 15) the relation between events and powers is left for future work, and without that the event reading does little work beyond relabelling. |
| [LIT-177](../../record/literature.d/LIT-177.md) v2 | The Relational Interpretation of Quantum Physics | Rovelli (2021), [ARXIV-2109.09170](https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.09170). Rovelli restates RQM as an ontology of sparse relative facts, which arise in interactions and are labelled by the interacting systems. Its one technical rule is that an amplitude W(b,a) gives the probability P = \|W\|² only when a and b are relative to the same system. That rule removes the clash between P_collapse = Σᵢ\|W(c,bᵢ)\|²\|W(bᵢ,a)\|² and P_unitary = \|ΣᵢW(c,bᵢ)W(bᵢ,a)\|² (eqs. 2–4). Decoherence makes some facts approximately label-independent ("stable"). ψ is a relative-state bookkeeping device, like a Hamilton–Jacobi function S. The price, which Rovelli states, is giving up "strong realism": values are not held at all times, may be held at different times for different systems, and there is no coherent global view and no quantum state of the universe. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-174); worth reading as the author's own compact, current statement of RQM, and a good anchor citation for relational and perspectival readings. It is expository, not argumentative. It does not engage the inter-observer consistency objections in any depth, and the main technical rule is postulated, not derived. |
| [LIT-178](../../record/literature.d/LIT-178.md) v2 | Borges and AI | Bottou & Schölkopf (2023), [ARXIV-2310.01425](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01425). A short essay, not an argument with evidence. It claims that a "perfect language model", defined as sampling each next word from a random occurrence of the current text in the infinite collection of all plausible texts, is a fiction machine governed by "narrative necessity", to which "neither truth nor intention" matters. On that view hallucinations "are just confabulations", chat-bot answers take the role the user's framing implies, and fine-tuning and RLHF are attempts to prune the garden "against its nature". It warns that power over what LLMs write becomes "power over what we think". | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-102); worth reading as a short, well-made and much-cited statement of the "LLM as fiction machine" framing, provided it is read as an essay offering imagery. It supplies no evidence, and its central idealization (the perfect LM) is asserted, not argued, to describe trained and aligned chat models. |
| [LIT-179](../../record/literature.d/LIT-179.md) v2 | Algorithmic Nudging: The Need for an Interdisciplinary Oversight | Schmauder et al. (2023), DOI-10.1007/s11245-023-09907-4. A short position paper: two case studies show AI agents learning to exploit documented biases (in Dezfouli et al. 2020 an RL agent moved choices of a target lottery from 50% to 70%). Against a Friedman-style judgement of AI nudges by outcome alone, the authors extend Hausman's "look under the hood" rejoinder and propose obliging developers to consult cognitive scientists, because an AI system may exploit biases that are not yet known. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-171](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-171.md)); worth a short read as a clear, citable statement of the case for mechanism-level oversight of AI nudging, but it adds no new evidence: its empirical content is second-hand, and its central worry (AI exploiting undocumented biases) is argued, not shown. |
| [LIT-180](../../record/literature.d/LIT-180.md) | Symmetry and Conservation Laws | Wigner (1964), DOI-10.1073/pnas.51.5.956. Wigner argues that invariance principles stand to the laws of nature as laws stand to events — they give structure to the laws — and distinguishes geometrical invariances (formulated on events; yielding energy/momentum/angular-momentum conservation, most directly in quantum kinematics) from dynamical ones such as gauge invariance and, he holds, general covariance, which are tied to specific interactions. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract and a skim on 2026-09-26 (the skim read a Wayback copy of the PNAS scan); a close reading was attempted the same day and could not fetch the paper: the Wayback Machine reset its connections, Europe PMC refused the PDF, and PMC returned a challenge page. The paper is open in the PNAS archive, so this is an access failure, not a paywall. It stays Deferred until it can be fetched. |
| [LIT-181](../../record/literature.d/LIT-181.md) | Knowledge is not always more valuable than mere true belief | Pettigrew (2025), <https://philpapers.org/rec/PETKIN-2>. Knowledge is not always more valuable than mere true belief — we do not always want it for those we care about even when it costs nothing — and veritism, on which truth is the only fundamental epistemic value, predicts exactly when it is and is not. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and failed on access, not availability: the paper is open on PhilArchive, which the author's homepage (now richardpettigrew.github.io) links to, but PhilArchive refused automated requests and no other host was found. A Wayback Machine capture of the PDF (2026-04-08) exists but could not be fetched from here. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied or PhilArchive can be reached. |
| [LIT-182](../../record/literature.d/LIT-182.md) v3 | Towards ethical evolution: responsible autonomy of artificial intelligence across generations | Terziyan et al. (2025), DOI-10.1007/s43681-025-00759-9. The paper proposes, without implementation, formalism or experiment, that an AI system which designs further AI systems should pass on one "immutable" principle. The principle is: do not directly harm humanity or violate fundamental human values; monitor and mitigate misuse of your outputs; and make every derivative inherit this principle (p. 12). The mechanisms are a genetic-algorithm analogy, with immutable and mutable "genes", constrained crossover and filtered mutation, together with "temporal cloning". The paper is inconsistent about its central notion. The principle is enforced once by a penalty F = Q − α·P (p. 9) and once by "unbreachable" hard constraints (p. 13). And it is "immutable" only in its architecture, while its ethical content stays "adaptable" (p. 14). | Rejected — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-156](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-156.md)); not worth a reader's time. It is a programme statement whose one concrete proposal is contradicted by the paper's own later sections. It demonstrates nothing, even though its conclusion says it does (p. 21). It does not engage the alignment work on preserving goals across successors that the problem calls for. A reader interested in value preservation under recursive self-design would do better starting from that literature. |
| [LIT-183](../../record/literature.d/LIT-183.md) | Fitting Fulfilment – Fitting Objective or Rational Attractiveness? | Hiekel (2018), DOI-10.1007/s42048-018-0007-y. Examines whether the objective element in Susan Wolf's hybrid view of meaning in life ("subjective attraction meets objective attractiveness") should be understood as objective attractiveness or as rational attractiveness. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and found the article closed access at Springer, with no repository copy. The publisher abstract states the conclusion: objectivity in Wolf's account of meaning should be replaced by rationality. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-184](../../record/literature.d/LIT-184.md) v2 | Effective Ontic Structural Realism | Ladyman & Lorenzetti (2023), DOI-10.1086/729061. Ladyman and Lorenzetti argue that three recent "effective realisms" lack a metaphysics that makes them realist rather than constructive-empiricist, and that the Ladyman–Ross OSR package supplies one: realism about modal structure, "to be is to be a real pattern", and scale-relative ontology. The three are Fraser & Vickers (and Egg) on non-relativistic QM, Williams and Fraser on QFT, and Robertson & Wilson on "theoretical relicts". Scale relativity also dissolves Saatsi's inconsistency objection, so Newtonian gravitational forces and GR gravitation "both exist at different scales". Egg's 2019 anthropocentrism objection is answered by grounding measurement in decoherence. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-129](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-129.md)); worth reading as a short, clear map tying the effective-realism literature to OSR's modal-structure and real-patterns ontology; its arguments are programmatic, and each step leans on Ladyman & Ross (2007) rather than new argument. |
| [LIT-185](../../record/literature.d/LIT-185.md) v2 | Integrated information theory: the good, the bad and the misunderstood | Barrett et al. (2026), [ARXIV-2604.11482](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11482). A position paper with no new formal result. It argues four things. IIT's algorithm is undefined whenever any spatial, temporal or state graining of a system is non-Markovian (Barrett & Mediano 2019), and so gives no output for brains; it cannot handle unbounded continuous state variables; and it has never been computed on any real system. PCI and other "approximations" of Φ are proxies, capturing at most two of the six axioms. The theory would need recasting in continuous fields (the EM field) to fit the standard model. Against critics, the authors hold that IIT can be read with a multi-dimensional measure of global state (mean Φ, variance of cause-effect-structure quantities, structural complexity), on which Aaronson's inactive expander grid is only minimally conscious, and that IIT's implied "tiling" panpsychism is no worse than any rival metaphysics. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-122); worth reading as the most compact current statement of IIT's formal ill-definedness and its proxy-versus-approximation problem, from sympathetic insiders; its defences (panpsychism, expander grids) rest on the authors' proposed reinterpretation rather than on IIT 4.0 as stated. |
| [LIT-186](../../record/literature.d/LIT-186.md) | What is purely epistemic normativity, and why? A study in Wolfian epistemology | Pettigrew (2025), <https://philpapers.org/rec/PETWIP>. What we ought to believe "epistemically speaking" is fixed by the norms whose broad adherence across an epistemic community would give the best epistemic results — a transposition of Susan Wolf's rule consequentialism — which explains why we separate epistemic from all-things-considered oughts. | Deferred — seeded from the abstract on 2026-09-26; a close reading was attempted the same day and failed on access, not availability: the paper is open on PhilArchive, which the author's homepage (now richardpettigrew.github.io) links to, but PhilArchive refused automated requests and no other host was found. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied or PhilArchive can be reached. |
| [LIT-187](../../record/literature.d/LIT-187.md) v3 | On the Philosophy of Unsupervised Learning | Watson (2023), DOI-10.1007/s13347-023-00635-6. Watson argues that clustering, abstraction and generative modelling each come with an epistemic thesis (EC, EA, EG — we identify natural kinds, essential properties and unrealized possibilities via such algorithms or something like them), which he accepts. Each also comes with an ontological thesis (OC, OA, OG — these just *are* what the algorithms ought to find under ideal conditions), which he rejects because nothing guarantees they are effectively computable. He adds a pragmatic, context-relative view of natural kinds, and closes with the claim that unsupervised outputs are neither "objective" nor vacuous. They should be validated by error-statistical means: resampling stability, generalization of clusters to held-out data, out-of-sample likelihood, and train-on-synthetic scores. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-172](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-172.md)); worth reading as the most systematic philosophical treatment of unsupervised learning as a family, and as the target of Søgaard's reply. Its headline claims ("perhaps the most fundamental branch of all AI") are argued more thinly than its E/O structure. |
| [LIT-188](../../record/literature.d/LIT-188.md) v4 | Scaffolding individuality: coordination, cooperation, collaboration and community | Griesemer & Shavit (2023), DOI-10.1098/rstb.2021.0398. The paper sets out an idealized three-stage sequence of interaction types, which it calls a "first and rough sketch" (§1). Stage 1 is coordination in *collections* (pairwise, "aggregative" interactions of co-located individuals). Stage 2 is cooperation in *conexuses* (a connected web of binary interactions whose benefits can still be counted per member). Stage 3 is collaboration in *communities*: heterogeneous multi-way interactions around a shared goal, in which generatively entrenched "core" interactions give organizational closure. Table 1 puts the transition to a new level of individuality between stages 2 and 3 only. The paper asserts, rather than argues, that this describes evolutionary transitions non-circularly and that individuality is a special case of collectivity. Its one argued conclusion is hedged: community-level inheritance "seem[s] likely to evolve in tandem" with community-level individuality (§3). | Proposed — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-173](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-173.md)); a short programmatic sketch, and the authors call it one. It is worth knowing as the compact statement of Griesemer's scaffolding, reproducer and generative-entrenchment view applied to human social transitions. Its headline claims (non-circularity; individuality as a special case of collectivity) appear only in the abstract. Two things would settle it: the model of community "reliability" and endogenization the authors promise in §2 ("We hope to explore such models in future work"), or an application of the 3Cs to a worked case, such as the companion Neolithic paper, that separates collection, conexus and community by measurable criteria. |
| [LIT-189](../../record/literature.d/LIT-189.md) v2 | Philosophy of Open Science | Leonelli (2023), DOI-10.1017/9781009416368. Leonelli argues that mainstream, institutional Open Science treats openness as the unlimited, digital sharing of research objects, a transparency-first ordering running transparency → quality (reproducibility) → inclusion. This rests on an object-oriented, "resourcist" epistemology of science. Four implementation cases (GISAID COVID data, open-source software stigma, crop-data linkage, the reproducibility "crisis") show it can narrow epistemic diversity and entrench epistemic injustice, which lowers reliability. She proposes instead "openness as judicious connection", grounded in a process-oriented view of research as the cultivation of "active knowledge". Her Figure 2 inverts the ordering to start from inclusion, and sharing becomes "neither a necessary starting point nor a sufficient condition" for reliable OS (p. 8). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-098](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-098.md)); worth reading as a clear, openly normative philosophical reframing of Open Science by someone who took part in the policy process, and as a compact bridge between social epistemology (epistemic injustice, pluralism) and data practice. Its evidence is four illustrative cases and the author's own qualitative research, not systematic data, and "judicious" remains a demand for deliberation rather than a criterion. |
| [LIT-190](../../record/literature.d/LIT-190.md) v2 | The coherent and fluent mind: how unified consciousness is constructed from cross-modal inputs via integrated processing experiences | Winkielman et al. (2015), DOI-10.3389/fpsyg.2015.00083. An opinion piece proposing an extra route to the unity of consciousness across the senses. Different modalities feed one global, content-nonspecific experience of *processing quality* (fluency, familiarity, coherence), and this integrated feeling then enters decisions about its source and relevance. The main support is the authors' own finding (Ziembowicz et al. 2013; three experiments) that coherent but *unrelated* background patterns in one modality made targets in another modality be judged more "regular", "familiar" and "possible". The authors state that direct evidence is still needed that such effects run through changed *experience* and that its integration is causal (p. 2). | Proposed — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-146](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-146.md)); a promising, explicitly unproven hypothesis. It would be settled by showing, inside the cross-modal coherence paradigm itself, that the effect depends on an experienced feeling: for example, that it disappears when participants are led to attribute their sense of coherence to the background (the discounting manipulation the authors cite from other paradigms), and that reports of felt coherence mediate the judgement shift. |
| [LIT-191](../../record/literature.d/LIT-191.md) v3 | AI and Consciousness | Schwitzgebel (2025), [ARXIV-2510.09858](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09858). A book-length sceptical argument that neither introspection, conceptual analysis nor any current theory (GWT, HOT, IIT, local recurrence, unlimited associative learning, biological views) can tell us whether near-future AI is conscious. The best sceptical argument, Mimicry, only undercuts the inference from humanlike text to consciousness and does not show absence. On the Leapfrog Hypothesis the first disputably conscious AIs will be person-like, and the question will then be settled socially rather than scientifically (the "Social Semi-Solution"). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 (NOTE-104); worth reading as the most complete current map of the AI-consciousness debate, and as the source of the Mimicry Argument, the Problem of Minimal Instantiation and the Leapfrog Hypothesis. Its own positive contributions are informal arguments and predictions, not results. |
| [LIT-192](../../record/literature.d/LIT-192.md) v2 | What's at stake in the debate over naturalizing teleology? An overlooked metatheoretical debate | Nahas & Sachs (2023), DOI-10.1007/s11229-023-04147-w. The paper argues that "naturalizing teleology" serves at least two relatively autonomous projects with different success criteria. One is scientific: whether an organism-centred account improves biology, which requires operationalisable models that may trade truth for usefulness. The other is philosophical: whether organismal teleology reshapes the liberal-versus-scientific naturalism debate about mind in nature, which requires truth but no operationalisation. It applies the distinction to one recurring dispute, the demarcation of teleological systems from dissipative structures. In the scientific context, models "cannot and need not" demarcate (§4.1: Cusimano & Sterner; McShea & Babcock). In the philosophical context, preferences between accounts turn on unstated commitments (§4.2: García-Valdecasas's Aristotelian case for Deacon over the Organizational Approach). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-094](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-094.md)); worth reading as a compact map of organism-centred teleology (the Organizational Approach, Deacon, Walsh, McShea) and for one reusable move, asking which problem an account of goal-directedness is meant to solve. Its positive pluralism is only suggested ("tempting to suggest", §5), and it is shown on a single dispute. |
| [LIT-193](../../record/literature.d/LIT-193.md) v2 | Better to be a Pig Dissatisfied than a Plant Satisfied | Terrill & Veit (2024), DOI-10.1007/s10806-024-09922-4. The paper argues conditionally. Suppose (A1) sentience comes in degrees, (A2) plants may be "quasi-sentient", and (A3) moral status comes in degrees and is fixed by degree of sentience. Then plants may have partial moral status, generating "some" obligations that are "outweighed by animal interests in most cases" (Table 1). Animals keep prima facie priority, so granting plants moral status "does not require" treating eating plants and eating pigs alike. The authors propose assigning plants "a very low value" of moral status until evidence accumulates (p. 13). A1–A3 are assumed, not argued, and A2 rests on a survey of contested plant-behaviour findings. | Proposed — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-120](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-120.md)); a coherent but unproven conciliatory position, useful as a clear template of the graded-sentience-to-graded-status inference. What would settle it is evidence for or against plant valenced states. Footnote 21 names the two gaps: the degree of plant-specific valenced states, and whether plants have high harm thresholds or harm-numbing mechanisms. The second author's later work signals a move against plant consciousness (see [LIT-105](../../record/literature.d/LIT-105.md)), so check his current view before citing it as his. |
| [LIT-194](../../record/literature.d/LIT-194.md) v3 | Religion and Science | De Cruz (2017), <https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/religion-science/>. A survey, not an argument. It reports that the scholarly field rejects the conflict model the public holds, and it finds Barbour's four-way taxonomy (conflict, independence, dialogue, integration) influential but neither exhaustive nor exclusive. Its survey of five traditions shows the relation varies with creed and colonial history, and it describes the field fragmenting into claim-by-claim "science engaged theology". Its metaphysical content is concentrated in §1.2 (methodological vs ontological naturalism) and §3.1 (divine action under deterministic, chaotic and quantum physics; primary vs secondary causation; chance and providence). | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-107](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-107.md)); worth reading as the standard orientation to the science–religion literature and as a map of the divine-action and naturalism debates; it is a survey, so its claims are only as strong as the works it cites, and it is written from within the (largely Christian) field it describes. |
| [LIT-195](../../record/literature.d/LIT-195.md) v2 | World models, artificial general intelligence and the hard problems of life–mind continuity: toward a unified understanding of natural and artificial intelligence | Safron et al. (2026), DOI-10.1098/rsta.2024.0533. The editorial introduces a theme issue of 17 papers. It concludes that the issue "overall appears to converge" on one view: LLMs model the world in some senses but "probably" lack coherent causal world models, and are not likely paths to "artificial general superintelligence" (§1). It separates three senses of "world model": any information about the world a system cognizes over, which LLMs "clearly" have; representation of causal structure, "less certain"; and causal prediction "in the service of agentic functioning", where the divergence "may become even greater" (§3). It closes that world models are "context-dependent constructions shaped by embodiment, dynamics, goals and social learning". | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-136](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-136.md)); worth reading as the map of the theme issue and a short, citable statement of the three senses of "world model". It argues nothing itself. Its claims are editorial glosses on 17 contributions, which carry the evidence. |
| [LIT-196](../../record/literature.d/LIT-196.md) v3 | Ontological Priority: The Conceptual Basis of Non-eliminative, Ontic Structural Realism | Chakravartty (2011), DOI-10.1007/978-94-007-2579-9_10. Non-eliminative OSR needs relations to be prior to their relata in the determination of their identity. Chakravartty argues that every available account of objects (substance, bundle theory, bundles of dispositionally individuated properties) leaves identity-fixing intrinsic features, and that stripping all of them away is just eliminativism. So OSR faces an exclusive disjunction: eliminativism, or a role for the intrinsic in identity. The graph-theory analogy and the physics cases (entanglement, spacetime points) do not escape the dilemma. The argument is conceptual throughout, and it explicitly leaves eliminative OSR untouched. | Active — read in full 2026-09-26 ([NOTE-149](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-149.md)); worth reading as the clearest statement of the dilemma facing relation-priority versions of OSR. Its §10.3 explication of "ontological priority" as a dependence claim is reused widely, and it names its own soft spot: the intrinsic/extrinsic asymmetry argument in §10.5. |
| [LIT-197](../../record/literature.d/LIT-197.md) v2 | The Enigma Unveiled: How AI Compromises Free Will in Decision-Making | Sharman (2024), DOI-10.1109/ICAPAI61893.2024.10541271.
