A one-layer ReLU transformer (P = 113, 30% of pairs, full-batch AdamW with λ = 1, 40k epochs) that groks modular addition implements a "Fourier multiplication" algorithm. The embedding maps a and b to cos/sin(w_k·a), cos/sin(w_k·b) at five key frequencies k ∈ {14, 35, 41, 42, 52}, with w_k = 2πk/113. Attention and MLP form cos/sin(w_k(a+b)), and the neuron-logit map W_L (≈ rank 10) reads out Σ_k α_k·cos(w_k(a+b−c)). Progress measures built from this (restricted and excluded loss) show training splits into memorisation (0–1.4k epochs), circuit formation (1.4k–9.4k) and cleanup (9.4k–14k), with the test-accuracy jump inside cleanup. | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 (NOTE-283); worth reading as the fullest mechanistic account of a grokking network. Its learned structure is exactly the character basis of ℤ/113 (five real 2-dimensional irreps), which makes it the nearest existing empirical instance of the map's row-7 "predicate harmonics = characters" prediction, in a setting where the group was known in advance. |
| [LIT-346](../../record/literature.d/LIT-346.md) v2 | Analysis of Boolean Functions | O'Donnell (2014), DOI-10.1017/CBO9781139814782. Every f: {−1,1}ⁿ → ℝ has a unique multilinear (Fourier–Walsh) expansion f = Σ_S f̂(S)χ_S over the parities χ_S(x) = ∏_{i∈S} x_i, which are an orthonormal basis (Thm 1.1, Thm 1.5) and are the characters of the group F₂ⁿ, χ_S(x+y) = χ_S(x)χ_S(y) (eq. 1.5; §3.2; §8.5). The central operators are all diagonal in this basis with eigenvalues depending only on \|S\|: the noise operator T_ρχ_S = ρ^{\|S\|}χ_S (Prop. 2.47) and the Laplacian Lχ_S = \|S\|χ_S (Prop. 2.37). Influence, noise stability and Arrow's Theorem (via Stab_{−1/3}) become statements about the Fourier weight at each level (Thms 2.20, 2.38, 2.49, 2.56). | Active — read in part 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-291](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-291.md)); worth reading as the standard, freely available text on Fourier analysis of functions on the Boolean cube. Chapters 1–2 alone give the character basis, Plancherel and Parseval, convolution, and the level-by-level diagonalisation of the noise operator and Laplacian. Read it as abelian harmonic analysis only: it has no nonabelian representation theory, no irreps of dimension > 1 and no isotypic decomposition. |
| [LIT-347](../../record/literature.d/LIT-347.md) v2 | Information-theoretic analysis of generalization capability of learning algorithms | Xu & Raginsky (2017), [ARXIV-1705.07809](https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.07809). If the loss ℓ(w,Z) is σ-subgaussian for every w, then the expected generalisation error of any learning algorithm P_{W\|S} obeys \|E[L_μ(W) − L_S(W)]\| ≤ √(2σ²·I(S;W)/n) (Theorem 1). This extends Russo–Zou to uncountable hypothesis spaces, and the paper adds a high-probability version, n = (8σ²/α²)(ε/β + log(2/β)) (Theorem 3). Minimising empirical risk plus I(S;W), relaxed to a KL term against a prior Q, yields exactly the Gibbs algorithm (Theorem 5). Noisy ERM and adaptive composition are analysed through the same mutual-information quantity. | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 (NOTE-306); worth reading as the short, clean source of the input–output mutual-information generalisation bound, and of "a learning algorithm is a channel P_{W\|S}", whose capacity is sup_μ I(S;W). The bound is in expectation and is vacuous for deterministic algorithms on continuous hypotheses, which is what the later compression and rate–distortion refinements (LIT-236, LIT-233) address. |
| [LIT-348](../../record/literature.d/LIT-348.md) v2 | An Empirical Model of Large-Batch Training | McCandlish et al. (2018), [ARXIV-1812.06162](https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.06162). The paper derives a local model in which the loss progress one step can make at batch size B is ΔL_max/(1 + B_noise/B), with B_noise = tr(HΣ)/GᵀHG (eqs. 2.7–2.8). Averaged over a run at fixed B, the model gives the steps/examples hyperbola (S/S_min − 1)(E/E_min − 1) = 1, which defines the critical batch B_crit = E_min/S_min (eqs. 2.11–2.12). Across eight tasks, B_crit spans about six orders of magnitude (from 20 to over 10 million, §3), and the Hessian-free B_simple = tr(Σ)/\|G\|² tracks it to about an order of magnitude (Table 1: run-averaged B_simple/B_crit runs from 0.05 for the SVHN VAE and autoencoder to 8 for SVHN). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 (NOTE-293); worth reading as the origin of the batch-size/gradient-SNR/critical-batch/time–compute result that row 12 of the owner's map attributes to it. | [LIT-352](../../record/literature.d/LIT-352.md) v2 | A numerical algorithm for block-diagonal decomposition of matrix *-algebras with application to semidefinite programming | Murota et al. (2010), DOI-10.1007/s13160-010-0006-9. Given real symmetric A₁…A_m, the method finds an orthogonal P that block-diagonalises them all as simultaneously and finely as possible. That is the decomposition of the matrix *-algebra 𝒯 they generate into simple components (Algorithm 4.1) and then irreducible components (§4.2), with no group or algebra supplied in advance. The simple components are the connected components of the graph "Q_iᵀA_pQ_{i′} ≠ O" between the eigenspaces of one random combination A(r) = Σ r_pA_p. This is correct with probability 1 when span{I, A₁…A_m} = 𝒯 ∩ Sⁿ (Props. 3.2–3.4, 4.2). The structure theorem it relies on, Theorem 3.1(C) "if 𝒯 is irreducible then 𝒯 = Mₙ", is false over ℝ as stated. The companion paper (Maehara & Murota 2010) handles the complex and quaternion types. | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-276](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-276.md)); worth reading as the clearest short account of *numerical* block-diagonalisation of a *-algebra from generators alone, via eigenspaces of a random element. For the general real case, i.e. irreducible components of complex or quaternionic type, the companion Maehara & Murota (JJIAM 27:263–293, 2010) is needed, and is not in the record. For the map's use, both should be cited. |
| [LIT-353](../../record/literature.d/LIT-353.md) v3 | The Theory of Representations for Boolean Algebras | Stone (1936), DOI-10.2307/1989664. The paper identifies Boolean algebras with Boolean rings with unit (xx = x), and generalized Boolean algebras with Boolean rings (Thms 1–4). Theorem 67 proves that every Boolean ring A is isomorphic to the algebra B(A) of classes 𝔈(a) = {prime ideals p : a ∉ p}, under symmetric difference and intersection. Its ideal lattice is isomorphic to the system of classes 𝔈(𝔞) under arbitrary unions and finite intersections. The existence of enough prime ideals (Thm 63) is proved by transfinite induction, and Theorem 70 shows that "every Boolean ring has an isomorphic algebra of classes" and "every ideal is the product of its prime divisors" are equivalent without choice. Theorems 68–69 classify *all* set-representations as restrictions of B(A) to subclasses of prime ideals. The topology, i.e. Stone spaces and duality, is explicitly deferred to "another paper". | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-272](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-272.md)); worth reading as the primary source of the Stone representation theorem, via prime ideals of Boolean rings, and of the Boolean-ring viewpoint. It is long and technical, and for the representation alone Chapter IV plus Theorems 1–2, 33–40 and 49 suffice. For the topological duality (Boolean algebras ↔ totally disconnected compact Hausdorff spaces), the map's actual need, Stone 1937 (Trans. AMS 41, LIT-358) is the source. |
| [LIT-354](../../record/literature.d/LIT-354.md) v3 | Algebraic Geometry and Statistical Learning Theory | Watanabe (2009), DOI-10.1017/CBO9780511800474. Per publisher description: develops statistical learning theory for singular models (neural networks, mixtures, hidden Markov models) in which the Fisher information is degenerate, using resolution of singularities to show that the real log canonical threshold governs Bayesian free energy and generalization error. | Deferred — seeded 2026-09-29 from metadata and published descriptions, not read; no lawful full text could be reached, and it stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. Its core results are now read in a lawful substitute by the same author, Watanabe's A Widely Applicable Bayesian Information Criterion ([LIT-616](../../record/literature.d/LIT-616.md); arXiv 1208.6338, JMLR 2013). That paper restates the book's standard representation theorem, the real log canonical threshold and the free-energy asymptotics F = nL_n(w0) + λ log n − (m − 1) log log n, citing the book for each. Read it for the results; the book holds their proofs, the generalization-error theory and WAIC. |
| [LIT-355](../../record/literature.d/LIT-355.md) v2 | An Introduction to Hyperplane Arrangements | Stanley (2006), DOI-10.1090/pcms/013/08. The notes define the regions of a real arrangement (connected components of the complement) and the intersection poset L(A) with its Möbius function and characteristic polynomial χ_A(t) = Σ μ(x) t^{dim x}. They prove Zaslavsky's theorem (Thm 2.5): r(A) = (−1)ⁿ χ_A(−1) and b(A) = (−1)^{rank A} χ_A(1), so the numbers of regions and bounded regions depend only on L(A), not on the face structure (Cor. 2.1, Fig. 2). The same machinery counts faces of every dimension (Thm 2.6: f_k = Σ_{x≤y, dim x=k} \|μ(x,y)\|). Later lectures add Whitney's theorem, deletion–restriction, matroids and geometric lattices, broken circuits, supersolvability, the finite-field method, and the metric d(R, R′) = number of separating hyperplanes, whose zero set shows that a region is determined by its separating set (p. 89). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-313](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-313.md)); worth reading as the standard open introduction to real hyperplane arrangements. It is the best reachable source for "regions", the intersection poset and Zaslavsky's region-count theorem, with complete proofs of the region formula. Read it knowing that it never uses the oriented-matroid vocabulary of "topes" or "covectors" (it cites Björner et al., *Oriented Matroids*, only in passing) and that it does not cite Zaslavsky's own texts. |
| [LIT-356](../../record/literature.d/LIT-356.md) v2 | How much does your data exploration overfit? Controlling bias via information usage | Russo & Zou (2015), arXiv:1511.05219. Suppose an analyst reports statistic φ_T, chosen by any data-dependent rule T from m candidates each σ-sub-Gaussian. Then the selection bias obeys \|E[φ_T − µ_T]\| ≤ σ√(2·I(T;φ)) (Prop. 1), where "information usage" I(T;φ) ≤ H(T) ≤ log m. For argmax selection over φ ~ N(µ,I) the squared error is Θ(1 + H(T)) (Prop. 3). Adaptive analyses compose additively, with I(T_{k+1};φ) ≤ Σ_i I(Y_{T_i};φ_{T_i} \| H_{i−1},T_i) (Lemma 1). Answering each query with Gaussian noise of variance σ²√j/n therefore keeps the k-th answer's error at O(σk^{1/4}/√n) (Prop. 7). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-308](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-308.md)); Worth reading as the origin of the mutual-information bias and generalisation bound that [LIT-347](../../record/literature.d/LIT-347.md) (Xu & Raginsky) extends, and as the first place its adaptive-composition chain rule and its per-query "information budget" with Gaussian noise (½·log(1+SNR) per query) appear. Read it knowing that it is stated for a finite set of m candidate statistics, and that "the bound is tight" is shown only for Gaussian argmax and threshold selections, as squared-error (not bias) bounds except in the exponential case. |
| [LIT-357](../../record/literature.d/LIT-357.md) v2 | Studies on the Foundation of Quantum Mechanics. I | Husimi (1937), DOI-10.11429/ppmsj1919.19.0_766. Husimi derives Birkhoff and von Neumann's lattice axioms from statistical premises. Implication is P̄ ≤ Q̄ in every state (and requires compatibility), negation is Q̄ = 1 − P̄, and compatible propositions obey classical logic. Meet is obtained from "Ehrenfest's principle" (additivity of mean values) via the top eigenvalue of aP + bQ (pp. 778–779), and the modular law, equivalently the parallelogram law \|P\| + \|Q\| − \|P∩Q\| = \|P∪Q\|, is proved under a finite-chain assumption. The orthomodular identity is used but never named or stated as an axiom. It appears as the step "since we admit traditional logic to any classical part … S + P∩Q = R" for R ⊃ P∩Q (p. 784), and as the case "(Q − P) + P = Q provided Q ⊃ P" of a classical identity (p. 780). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-312](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-312.md)); worth reading as the first derivation of the Birkhoff–von Neumann propositional calculus from operational premises (states, mean values, compatibility), and for its physical account of meet and join. It is not, however, a clean citation for "the orthomodular law". The law is an unnamed lemma resting on "classical logic within compatible sets", and the paper's axiomatic target is modularity under a finite-chain assumption. A text that states orthomodularity as an axiom (e.g. Kalmbach, *Orthomodular Lattices*, 1983; unverified here) would settle row 1's citation. |
| [LIT-358](../../record/literature.d/LIT-358.md) v2 | Applications of the theory of Boolean rings to general topology | Stone (1937), DOI-10.1090/S0002-9947-1937-1501905-7. Stone topologises the set 𝔈 of prime ideals of a Boolean ring by taking the sets 𝔈(a) as a basis. 𝔈 is then a totally disconnected, locally bicompact Hausdorff space, bicompact exactly when the ring has a unit (Thm 1), and every such space arises this way (Thm 2). Theorem 4 states that "the algebraic theory of Boolean rings is mathematically equivalent to the topological theory of Boolean spaces": isomorphism ↔ homeomorphism, ideals ↔ open sets, quotients ↔ closed subsets, unit ↔ bicompactness, and subrings ↔ continuous images (Thm 7). The same paper also contains what are now called the Stone–Čech compactification (Thms 78–79, 88), the Stone–Weierstrass theorem (Thm 82), the Banach–Stone theorem (Thm 83) and closed ideals of C(X) ↔ closed sets (Thm 85). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-311](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-311.md)); worth reading as the primary source of Stone duality and the correct citation for map row 8's "Stone duality", replacing [LIT-353](../../record/literature.d/LIT-353.md). For that purpose Chapter I (pp. 378–394) is enough. The rest is a long general-topology memoir whose best-known results (Stone–Čech, Stone–Weierstrass, Banach–Stone) are stated in 1937 vocabulary (bicompact, H-space, analytical isomorphism) and are now better learned from textbooks. |

| [LIT-360](../../record/literature.d/LIT-360.md) v2 | Notes on Landauer's principle, reversible computation, and Maxwell's Demon | Bennett (2002), arXiv:physics/0210005. Bennett states Landauer's principle as a consequence of the Second Law. A logically irreversible step, meaning erasure or a *merge of control flow*, must export at least the lost entropy (k ln 2 per bit) to non-information-bearing degrees of freedom. That export need not take the form of heat (p. 1). Logically reversible operations, including copying onto a blank register and measurement, have no minimum cost. He calls "an intrinsic cost of order kT for every elementary act of information processing" a 20th-century "misconception" (pp. 5–6). Erasing random data can itself be thermodynamically reversible; the waste comes from applying a many-to-one operation to *known* data (pp. 1, 4). | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-310](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-310.md)); Worth reading as a short, first-hand statement, by the principle's main defender, of what Landauer's principle requires (logical irreversibility) and what it does not (any cost for acquiring, copying or evaluating information). It is the best reachable stand-in for the unreachable [LIT-328](../../record/literature.d/LIT-328.md). Read it knowing that it is an argued position paper, not a derivation. On the Earman–Norton "unnecessary or insufficient" dilemma Bennett concedes the point "with some justice" (p. 5) and defends the principle only as pedagogy. |

| [LIT-362](../../record/literature.d/LIT-362.md) v2 | Learning the Irreducible Representations of Commutative Lie Groups | Cohen & Welling (2014), arXiv:1402.4437. Proposes "Weyl's principle" (via Kanatani 1990) as a definition of disentangling: the elementary components of data are the irreducible subspaces of the representation of a symmetry group acting on it. It learns such a decomposition for one class of group fixed in advance, compact commutative subgroups of SO(D) ("toroidal"), from pairs (x, y) of raw data vectors related by an unobserved group element, y = W R(φ) Wᵀx + ε. The orthogonal basis W (2-D invariant subspaces) is fitted by SGD on a closed-form marginal likelihood, and the integer weights ω_j are then read off a batch rotated by a known 0.1°. The only experiment trains 100 filters on 250,000 white-noise 16×16 patches paired with random rotations of themselves (learned ω from −11 to 12), and uses the invariant √κ̂ for 1-NN on rotated MNIST, reported in a figure with no numbers. Spectral degeneracy plays no role. | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-307](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-307.md)); worth reading as the earliest ML paper in this batch to state that the "elementary components" of a representation are the irreps of a symmetry group and to learn them from data; read it as learning the irreducible decomposition of an assumed abelian group class from transformation pairs on raw pixels, not as detecting an unknown symmetry in a trained network, and with an experiment that is a one-group proof of concept on noise patches. |
| [LIT-363](../../record/literature.d/LIT-363.md) v2 | Understanding image representations by measuring their equivariance and equivalence | Lenc & Vedaldi (2014), arXiv:1411.5908. Given a fixed representation φ (HOG, or AlexNet Conv1–Conv5 before ReLU) and a transformation g of the input image chosen by the experimenter (flips, 90° and other rotations, rescaling by 2^{±1/2}, affine warps), it fits a linear map M_g on the representation space with φ(gx) ≈ M_g φ(x), by sparse or structured-sparse regression or by a "transformation layer" trained through the rest of the network, and fits "stitching layers" E with φ′(x) ≈ E φ(x) between different networks. In AlexNet, learned M_g recover most of the classifier's accuracy for vertical flips and 90° rotations (top-1 error 0.75 uncompensated vs 0.43–0.51 and 0.44–0.53 across Conv1–Conv5, original 0.43), channels scored invariant rise to 99.61% for horizontal flips at Conv5, and Conv1–Conv2 are interchangeable between AlexNet and networks trained on ImageNet, Places or both. It detects no group and uses no spectrum. | Active — read in full 2026-09-29 ([NOTE-309](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-309.md)); worth reading as the origin of two standard probes of trained networks, fitted linear equivariance maps and model stitching, and as the closest prior art for "measuring symmetry in a trained network's representation"; read it knowing that every transformation tested is named in advance and applied to the input, that each is fitted separately with no group structure, and that its deep-layer "equivariance" is judged by recovered task accuracy through a high-capacity learned layer. |
| [LIT-364](../../record/literature.d/LIT-364.md) v2 | The Theory of Signal Detectability. Part I: The General Theory; Part II: Applications with Gaussian Noise | Peterson & Birdsall (1953), <https://deepblue.lib.umich.edu/handle/2027.42/7068>. Peterson and Birdsall show that the criterion approach (maximise P_SN(A) − β·P_N(A), or maximise detection at fixed false-alarm k) and the Woodward–Davies a-posteriori approach are all solved by one receiver that computes the likelihood ratio ℓ(x) = f_SN(x)/f_N(x) and thresholds it (Part I, Theorems 1–7). They introduce the receiver operating characteristic, whose slope at each point is the threshold β (Theorem 8, Eq. 2.51). For a signal known exactly in white Gaussian noise, ln ℓ is normal with equal variance 2E/N₀ under both hypotheses and means 2E/N₀ apart, so the ROC is a one-parameter family indexed by the "detection index" d = (M_SN − M_N)²/σ² = 2E/N₀ (Part II, Eqs. 4.1–4.8, Fig. 4.1). The receiver is a correlator or matched filter (Eq. 5.3, 4.10). | Active — read in part 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-315](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-315.md)); worth reading as the founding engineering statement of signal detection theory: the likelihood-ratio receiver, the ROC and its slope property, and the equal-variance Gaussian case with an explicit d = 2E/N₀. Read the 1953 report knowing that the scan is poor and that the 1954 IRE paper (with W. C. Fox) is the citable journal form, which was not read here. |

Registered to cover signal detection theory, which the record lacked. | Deferred — seeded 2026-09-30 at the owner's request to cover signal detection theory; a close reading was attempted and no legitimate full text could be reached |
| [LIT-367](../../record/literature.d/LIT-367.md) v2 | Calculation of signal detection theory measures | Stanislaw & Todorov (1999), DOI-10.3758/BF03207704. A practical tutorial that gives closed-form SDT measures computed from the hit rate H and false-alarm rate F. They are d′ = Φ⁻¹(H) − Φ⁻¹(F); β = exp{[Φ⁻¹(F)² − Φ⁻¹(H)²]/2}; c = −[Φ⁻¹(H) + Φ⁻¹(F)]/2; the nonparametric A′ and Grier's B″ (single-formula versions, eqs. 3 and 9); A_z = Φ(intercept/√(1+slope²)) from the z-ROC, whose slope is σ_noise/σ_signal; and A_d′ = Φ(d′/√2), which equals 2AFC proportion correct under the d′ assumptions. It also gives one-line commands for seven software packages. Its worked rating example (50 signal and 25 noise trials, noise SD larger than signal SD) shows d′ ranging from 0.84 to 1.86 across criteria. The authors conclude that d′ should not be used without rating-task evidence of equal variance. | Active — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-314](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-314.md)); worth reading as the standard short, citable reference for how the SDT measures are computed and what each assumes. It is a tutorial with no new theory. Several of its software pointers (1999 FTP sites, Quattro Pro, SYSTAT commands) are dated, but the formulae are not. |
| [LIT-368](../../record/literature.d/LIT-368.md) v2 | A decision-making theory of visual detection | Tanner & Swets (1954), DOI-10.1037/h0058700. Tanner and Swets model visual detection as testing a statistical hypothesis. The observer compares one sample of neural activity with a cutoff, and signal+noise and noise are Gaussian with equal variance, separated by d′ (the mean difference in noise-SD units, defined as the square root of Peterson and Birdsall's d). The cutoff moves along a curve of P_SN(A) against P_N(A) (the curve now called the ROC; for d′ = 1 it passes through (.16, .50), (.50, .84) and (.84, .98)). The optimal operating point is where the slope equals β = [(1−P(SN))/P(SN)]·[(V_N·CA+K_N·A)/(V_SN·A+K_SN·CA)]. The 4AFC prediction is P(C)=∫F(x)³g(x)dx. With three paid observers, d′ estimated from yes-no data predicted 4AFC accuracy. The "false alarms are guesses" (high-threshold) hypothesis is rejected: false-alarm rate correlated with chance-corrected thresholds (ρ = .30, .71, .67; combined p ≪ .001), and all 12 fitted yes-no scatter lines miss the point (1, 1). | Active — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-316](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-316.md)); worth reading as the founding psychophysics paper of signal detection theory: it is short, it is the first place d′ is named, and it is the first to put the observer on the ROC and to set the likelihood-ratio criterion β from priors and payoffs. For the ideal-observer mathematics it defers entirely to Peterson and Birdsall (1953), and its experiment is small (three observers) with some statistics asserted rather than shown. |
| [LIT-369](../../record/literature.d/LIT-369.md) v2 | Grokking and Generalization Collapse: Insights from HTSR theory | Prakash & Martin (2025), arXiv:2506.04434. A depth-3, width-200 ReLU MLP trained on 1,000 MNIST images (the Omnigrok recipe, with weights and biases scaled ×8, MSE loss, AdamW, WD = 0) for 10⁷ steps goes through three phases read off the accuracy curves. In pre-grokking (to ~10⁵ steps) training accuracy saturates while test accuracy stays low. In grokking (~10⁵–10⁶) test accuracy rises to ~0.85. In "anti-grokking" (~10⁶–10⁷) it falls to ~0.45–0.5 while training accuracy stays near 1. The average HTSR power-law exponent α of the two hidden weight matrices goes 4.0 → 2.9 → 1.1 at the phase ends (Table 1). In the collapse phase, correlation traps (outlier eigenvalues of the elementwise-shuffled weight matrix) appear. The paper's own weight-decay control shows FC2's α below 2, and traps in both layers, *without* a collapse (Fig. 6, Table 2). That undercuts its headline claim that α < 2 "invariably" heralds collapse. | Proposed — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-317](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-317.md)); worth knowing as the report of a late generalisation collapse after grokking at WD = 0, but it is one architecture on one 1k-sample MNIST subset, with the seed count unstated, and its central diagnostic (α < 2 ⇒ collapse) is contradicted by its own WD = 0.01 control. What would settle it: several seeds and architectures, with α and traps reported for runs that reach α < 2 without collapsing, and a comparison against the ℓ² norm under the Omnigrok LU account, which the paper does not cite. |
| [LIT-370](../../record/literature.d/LIT-370.md) v2 | New Evidence of the Two-Phase Learning Dynamics of Neural Networks | Zhou et al. (2025), arXiv:2505.13900. VGG-16 and ResNet-20 on CIFAR-10 (SGD with momentum, lr 0.1, 160 epochs) are compared across pairs of training times. Before an early "inflection point" (~2,500 iterations for VGG-16, ~100–500 for ResNet-20), a tiny parameter perturbation under identical SGD noise sends the run to a different basin by the end: loss barrier up to ~2, test disagreement ~0.15–0.5. After it, the same perturbation leaves the runs linearly connected. Also after it, the empirical NTK keeps changing step to step but stays within a bounded cosine distance of any later reference kernel (the "cone effect", e.g. S ≈ 0.05–0.1 for VGG-16 vs ≈ 0.15–0.2 from initialisation). Switching to linearised training later gives better test accuracy (VGG-16 0.13 → 0.82 as the switch moves from ≤200 to 5,000 iterations). | Proposed — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-318](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-318.md)); a compact, readable demonstration that an early, short chaotic phase gives way to a confined, stable one in both basin and kernel terms, but it rests on two architectures on one dataset, one run each with no error bars. The chaos half largely re-measures Frankle et al.'s (2020) spawning / linear-mode-connectivity result with a parameter perturbation in place of fresh SGD noise, and the claimed role of the cone in generalisation is asserted. What would settle it: seeds and error bars, the batch size, a stated perturbation norm, other domains, and a quantitative test of confinement (e.g. an angular radius that stops growing) rather than a 3-D picture. |
| [LIT-371](../../record/literature.d/LIT-371.md) v2 | How do language models learn facts? Dynamics, curricula and hallucinations | Zucchet et al. (2025), arXiv:2503.21676. 'An 8-layer, 44M-parameter decoder-only transformer trained with AdamW on synthetic biographies (64k individuals by default) learns factual recall in three phases. It first fits the marginal attribute-value distribution, then sits on a plateau exactly at the no-knowledge baseline, then acquires individual-specific associations. Plateau end grows with population as 0.43·N^0.81 (R² = 0.998, N = 4k–256k, 5 seeds). Attention patching from post-plateau checkpoints removes the plateau, and attention from the recall position to name tokens rises through it, so the plateau is when the attention extraction circuit forms. Nothing in the three phases is a compression of input information: the last phase adds individual-specific information.' | Active — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-319](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-319.md)); worth reading as a clean, intervention-backed decomposition of a loss plateau into circuit formation followed by knowledge storage, in a setting where knowledge is countable; its curriculum and fine-tuning results are single-seed and synthetic, and it measures no information quantity, so it bears on phases of training but not on the IB account. |
| [LIT-372](../../record/literature.d/LIT-372.md) v2 | On the Information Bottleneck Theory of Deep Learning | Saxe et al. (2018), DOI-10.1088/1742-5468/ab3985. Replicating Shwartz-Ziv & Tishby with their own code, the paper shows the information-plane "compression phase" appears only for double-saturating nonlinearities measured through an imposed binning/noise model: tanh compresses, ReLU/softplus/linear do not (binning, KDE, Kraskov; SZT task and MNIST), and re-binning the same tanh run evenly in net input removes it. Compression and generalisation dissociate in all four combinations, full-batch GD compresses as much as SGD, and the drift→diffusion gradient-SNR transition occurs in networks that cannot compress (ReLU, MNIST, a 1-1-1 linear net), so it is real but unrelated to compression. | Active — read in full 2026-09-30 ([NOTE-320](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-320.md)); worth reading as the direct experimental test of all three claims of the IB account of deep learning (two phases; compression causes generalisation; SGD diffusion causes compression), each refuted as a general statement with the original code, and for its Appendix C argument that I(h;X) is infinite in a deterministic network so every finite information-plane value belongs to the analyst's noise model. |
| [LIT-373](../../record/literature.d/LIT-373.md) v4 | The two dragons of cognition: recursive condensation for predictive processing | Li (2026), DOI-10.3389/fncom.2026.1778902. A "Hypothesis and Theory" essay proposing that the neocortex is a "biological Savitch machine". Alternating expansion ("odd parity") and contraction ("even parity") phases manufacture the disjoint closed sets of Urysohn's Lemma, and storing ("memoizing") the condensed tokens converts exponential search into polynomial "navigation". Its one non-standard formal result, Theorem 2, R(N) ∝ b^{αN} (§5.2.2, p. 11), is stated without proof. There is no data, simulation or model. The two mathematical pillars are misdescribed: Savitch's theorem saves space at the cost of time and does not memoize, and Urysohn's Lemma gives a continuous separator, not a linear one, and is not an "if and only if". | Rejected — re-read jointly with [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md) and arXiv 2602.07974 on 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-321](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-321.md)); Read jointly with [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md) and the companion StrLT preprint (arXiv 2602.07974), the essay is the cognitive motivation of a coherent programme: structure is discovered by costly search, condensed into frozen tokens, and reused cheaply. It is not just a misuse of two theorems. Read charitably, its mathematical slips are repairable and are not the reason to set it aside: the Urysohn "iff" and linear readout, Savitch as memoisation, the Definition 2 clash. The reason is what is left after repair: an amortised-inference and complementary-learning-systems picture already in the literature it cites; a neural mapping whose predictions do not discriminate it from standard consolidation accounts; one original quantitative claim, Theorem 2, that is not argued. A reader who wants the programme should read [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md) and the companion, where it is made precise. |
| [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md) v3 | Structural Decoupling: A Scaffold-Flow Theory of Generalization and Alignment | Li (2026), arXiv:2506.20699. 'Proposes "width", the least number of cells in an open cover on each of which some predictor is γ-contractive with conditional risk ≤ δ, as a structural complexity separate from VC dimension. From an additive Rademacher bound, √(2d_str log(e(K−1)n/d_str)/n) + LK·R_n(G) (Thm 4.5), it argues that context routing (the "scaffold") should be trained by different signals from within-context prediction (the "flow"), and that alignment failures are scaffold failures. The routine results are proved: coupon-collector Ω(w log w), Lipschitz composition, penalised model selection, multiclass Sauer–Shelah. The new ones are not. The phase transition at width is assumed in its proof. The bouquet witness of width/VC separation contradicts its own construction. The "fundamental theorem" is false as stated, because infinite pseudo-dimension does not prevent learnability. No theorem links the CS-Laplacian estimator to width. There are no experiments.' | Proposed — re-read jointly with [LIT-373](../../record/literature.d/LIT-373.md) and arXiv 2602.07974 on 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-322](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-322.md)); promising and unproven. Read jointly with the companion arXiv 2602.07974, which carries the fuller statements and several definitions this paper leaves implicit. The formal core holds in repaired form: width as a structural complexity incomparable with the per-cell VC dimension; an error floor below width; the Ω(w log w) discovery cost; an additive Rademacher bound for routed predictors. The flagged slips are repairable and not load-bearing: the bouquet cover, the non-degeneracy assumption in the appendix, Massart, and pseudo-dimension against fat-shattering in Thm 4.6. What remains unproven are the claims that would make it matter: that the CS estimator converges to width itself and not to a predictor-relative count; that a monolithic model cannot match K ≥ w routing; above all, that router/scaffold training should be decoupled from task loss. This is presented as derived from the additive bound, but the bound holds for joint training too. The alignment section is asserted. What would settle it: on synthetic benchmarks of known width, (i) a K-sweep showing the floor at K < w for routed learners and its absence or presence for capacity-matched monolithic models; (ii) CS width recovery from a cold start; (iii) decoupled against joint router training, compared on forgetting and boundary error. |
| [LIT-375](../../record/literature.d/LIT-375.md) v2 | Structural Learning Theory: A Metric-Topology Factorization Approach | Li (2026), arXiv:2602.07974. >- The formal core of Xin Li's structural-learning programme. Width w(P; γ, δ) is the least number of open cells on each of which some predictor is γ-contractive with conditional risk ≤ δ. The paper proves that width is incomparable with the per-cell VC dimension (Thm 2.2). It shows that with K < w cells there is an n-independent error floor η(w, K), under a non-degeneracy assumption stated in the body (Thm 2.4, Assumption 2.3). Width is at least c·β₁·L/D₀ under a bounded-overlap assumption (Thm 2.5), and discovering all basins costs Ω(w log w) samples (Thm 2.6). The CS-Laplacian eigenvalue count converges uniformly to the predictor-relative count w_G(P) when n ≳ (H_G + d_X log(1/r_x) + log(1/δ′))/(r_x^{d_X} g_eff²) (Thm 3.5); no result shows that w_G equals width. There are no experiments. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-324](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-324.md)); >- Promising and unproven. It is the programme's fullest and most careful formal statement, and it carries the learning theory of [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md) §3 with the assumptions in the body. Its routine results hold, and its bouquet slip is repairable. What would settle it: - a theorem that the CS count for a learned predictor equals w(P; γ, δ), not w_G(P); - a K-sweep on benchmarks of known width, showing the floor at K < w for routed learners, and testing whether a capacity-matched monolithic model shows it; - the decoupled-versus-joint training comparison it motivates but does not run. |
| [LIT-376](../../record/literature.d/LIT-376.md) v2 | Local Urysohn Width: A Topological Complexity Measure for Classification | Li (2026), arXiv:2603.15412. >- It defines local Urysohn width uw_{D₀}(P, γ): the least number of connected sets of diameter ≤ D₀, each carrying a continuous classifier correct on the margin-γ safe region it meets, needed to cover that safe region. It proves: - uw = w on a bouquet of w circles with safe balls at the antipodes, when 3γ/2 ≤ D₀ < L/2 − 3γ/4 (Thm 4.1); - uw ≥ w·m, with m = Θ(L/D₀) safe balls per loop, so uw = Ω(w·L/D₀) (Cor 5.1); - two-way non-determination with VC dimension (Thm 6.1); - an Ω(w log w) sample lower bound via label-permuted coupon collection (Thm 7.1); - uw ≥ 2β₁/Δ₀ for good, convex, bounded-adjacency covers (Thm 8.5). The proofs are correct. The obstruction is metric, as the paper concedes in Remark 8.11: patches cannot reach across the wedge point. Because Urysohn's lemma makes local correctness automatic on any patch, the measure is a connected covering number of the safe region that does not depend on the labels. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-323](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-323.md)); >- Promising and unproven. It is the cleanest and most honest paper in Li's programme: definitions are precise, the main theorems are correctly proved, a dependency table separates proved from conjectural results, and earlier errors are corrected openly. It is also the consistent construction that [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md)'s and arXiv 2602.07974's bouquet lacked. What would settle it: a result showing that this width governs error or sample cost for learners not built from local experts (for example local-learning or nearest-neighbour rates), or a proof of Conj 8.9 under conditions its motivating examples actually satisfy. Without one of these, it is a covering number with a topological headline. |
| [LIT-377](../../record/literature.d/LIT-377.md) | On Context-Content Uncertainty Principle | Li (2025), arXiv:2506.20699 v1. It posits that structured content (priors, schemas) has far lower entropy than context (sensory input), and concludes that inference should run from content to context, minimizing H(context \| content). On that it builds four layers of principles. The first is structure before specificity and its equivalents. The second covers precision-weighted attention, slower learning for content than for context, and memory as low-entropy attractors. The third is staged structure-first learning, and the fourth hierarchical composition. It then reads predictive coding, the free energy principle, active inference and attractor dynamics as instances. Everything is asserted or argued informally. There are no experiments, and several proofs are wrong or missing. | Rejected — by [LIT-374](../../record/literature.d/LIT-374.md); read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-325](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-325.md)); not worth a reader's time as a source of results. Its foundation is the entropy chain rule plus an assumption, H(content) ≪ H(context), and from these it reads off a direction of inference. Of its eight theorems, one is proved only in sketch, one equivalence proposition is marked "proof skipped" and argued only in words (App. H), and the proofs of Theorems 6–8 swap the roles of content and context relative to the statements. The proof of Theorem 1 states the inequality backwards. Theorem 3 rests on D_KL(q‖p) ≥ H(q) − H(p), which is false in general. The abstract's "computational simulations" are not in the paper. The author's own v2 (June 2026) replaced the whole framework under a new title. |
| [LIT-378](../../record/literature.d/LIT-378.md) | 'What is a Thing?': Topos Theory in the Foundations of Physics | Döring & Isham (2008), arXiv:0803.0417. A 212-page restatement of the topos programme of papers I–IV. A theory of a system S is a representation of a typed language L(S) in a topos. In quantum theory the topos is presheaves on the poset V(H) of abelian von Neumann subalgebras, propositions are clopen sub-objects of the spectral presheaf Σ via daseinisation, a pure state gives sieve-valued truth values ν(A ε Δ; ψ)_V = {V′ ⊆ V \| ⟨ψ\|δ(Ê[A∈Δ])_{V′}\|ψ⟩ = 1}, and each bounded self-adjoint Â becomes an arrow Σ → ℝ^↔ whose values are monotone intervals over subcontexts. Classical physics fits its axioms for composite systems exactly; quantum theory fits the disjoint sum but not the tensor-product composite. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-326](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-326.md)); worth reading as the one-volume statement of the Döring–Isham topos programme. It is, by the authors' own account (p. 14), "partly an amalgam" of papers I–IV ([LIT-343](../../record/literature.d/LIT-343.md), [LIT-335](../../record/literature.d/LIT-335.md), [LIT-299](../../record/literature.d/LIT-299.md), [LIT-310](../../record/literature.d/LIT-310.md)), with corrections and some new material. The new material is the value of a quantity in a pseudo-state and the inverse-image link between L(S) and PL(S) (§§8.5–8.8, Thm 8.6), the algebra of the candidate quantity-value objects (§9.3), the Boolean-subalgebra base category (§5.5.3), Kochen–Specker as a lifting obstruction (§6.6), and a long speculative §14–15. It adds no new no-go result. Its proofs are as sketchy as the series' at the same places: Sub_cl(Σ) is Heyting (Thm 16.1) with ⇒ left unwritten, and ι : O → P_clΣ natural and monic is left to the reader. A reader who has read I–IV gains only §§8.5–8.8, 9.3 and 14; a reader new to the programme should start here. |
| [LIT-379](../../record/literature.d/LIT-379.md) | Anger makes fake news viral online | Chuai & Zhao (2020), arXiv:2004.10399. On Weibo, fake news carries more anger and less joy than real news, and in observational regressions with controls anger raises retweets while joy lowers them; questionnaires tie anger-dominated news to anxiety-management and information-sharing motives. Unread closely: registered from a triage reading. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from the reading feed, where it was read on three separate days (the anthology's issue 180, https://github.com/dmarx/anthology-of-the-sota/issues/180); read for triage, not yet closely enough to place, and no NOTE is filed |
| [LIT-380](../../record/literature.d/LIT-380.md) v2 | A Topos Perspective on the Kochen-Specker Theorem: III. Von Neumann Algebras as the Base Category | Hamilton et al. (1999), arXiv:quant-ph/9911020. Hamilton, Isham & Butterfield move the series' base category from operators (O) and Boolean subalgebras (W) to the poset V of commutative von Neumann subalgebras of B(H). There they restate Kochen–Specker as "the spectral presheaf Σ(V) = Gelfand spectrum of V has no global elements" (dim H > 2, cited, §2.2). They identify the coarse-graining presheaf G(V) = L(V), G(i)(P̂) = inf{Q̂ ∈ L(V₂) \| P̂ ≤ Q̂} (Def. 3.1), with the presheaf CloΣ of clopen subsets of the spectra; the isomorphism is asserted, and part IV proves it. A state ρ yields the sieve-valued valuation ν^ρ_V(P̂) = {V′ ⊆ V \| ρ(G(P̂)) = 1} (Def. 3.4) and the support projectors inf{P̂ ∈ L(V) \| ρ(P̂) = 1}. These form a global element of G and hence an "interval valuation", a subobject of Σ (§§4.2–4.4). The probability-r valuations give subobjects of G but not global elements. | Superseded — by [LIT-382](../../record/literature.d/LIT-382.md); read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-327](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-327.md)); covered better by part IV (Butterfield & Isham 2002, read alongside), which restates this paper's V-framework definitions nearly verbatim, proves the G ≅ CloΣ isomorphism that this paper only asserts and develops its interval valuations into theorems, and by Döring–Isham II ([LIT-335](../../record/literature.d/LIT-335.md)), whose spectral presheaf over V(H) and clopen propositions are the mature form; it remains the citation for introducing V as the base category (as [LIT-335](../../record/literature.d/LIT-335.md) credits it). |
| [LIT-381](../../record/literature.d/LIT-381.md) v2 | A Topos Perspective on the Kochen-Specker Theorem: II. Conceptual Aspects, and Classical Analogues | Butterfield & Isham (1998), arXiv:quant-ph/9808067. Butterfield & Isham argue that the sieve-valued "generalised valuations" of part I are not a quantum peculiarity but the natural valuations for any presheaf of propositions on any small category. Three supports are offered. (1) Classical physics: the value presheaf Υ(Ā) = Ā(S) on the category M of measurable functions has global sections (one per microstate, eq. 3.7), yet a macrostate R ⊆ S yields a sieve-valued valuation ν^R(A ∈ Δ) = {f_M \| B̄(R) ⊆ f(Δ)} (eq. 3.10) with the same FUNC property as the quantum ν^ρ. (2) A proof that a sieve-valued valuation on any presheaf G obeys generalised FUNC, ν(B, G(f)(d)) = f*(ν(A, d)), iff it is a natural transformation G → Ω, i.e. a subobject of G (Theorem 4.1). (3) An informal argument, explicitly "not a genuine deduction" (§5.2), that if partial truth is fixed by which weakenings of a proposition are totally true, the truth value is a sieve. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-329](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-329.md)); worth reading as the series' conceptual companion and the only place it states the classical analogue and the partial-truth motivation; the mathematics is light (one short theorem), and the sieve semantics it motivates is carried on in part III, part IV and Döring–Isham ([LIT-343](../../record/literature.d/LIT-343.md), [LIT-335](../../record/literature.d/LIT-335.md)). |
| [LIT-382](../../record/literature.d/LIT-382.md) v2 | A Topos Perspective on the Kochen-Specker Theorem: IV. Interval Valuations | Butterfield & Isham (2001), arXiv:quant-ph/0107123. Butterfield & Isham show, over the poset V of commutative von Neumann subalgebras, that sieve-valued valuations and "interval valuations" (assignments of support projectors, or of subsets of Gelfand spectra) determine each other under stated conditions. If supports s(α, V) = inf{P̂ \| α_V(P̂) = true_V} form a global element of the coarse-graining presheaf G, then α_{V₁}(P̂) = {V₂ ⊆ V₁ \| G(s(α, V₁)) ≤ G(P̂)} is a sieve-valued valuation obeying FUNC (Theorem 3.1). There is an analogue for "tight" subobjects of Σ (Theorem 3.2). Both are satisfied by every state's valuation ν^ρ. The ordering ≤ (or ⊆) is shown to be the natural relation securing sievehood, null, monotonicity, exclusivity and unit (§4). The review §2.4 also supplies the proof, via state extension, that G ≅ CloΣ: restricting the clopen set of P̂ to V₂ gives the clopen set of its coarse-graining. Part III had only asserted this. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-328](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-328.md)); worth reading as the finished statement of the series' von Neumann-algebra framework. It proves the G ≅ CloΣ isomorphism (coarse-graining = direct image of clopen regions) that Döring–Isham's daseinisation relies on, and it shows that a state's sieve-valued truth values are fixed by its support, a global element of G. Its two theorems are correct but close to definitional, so its value lies in the framework rather than in depth. |
| [LIT-383](../../record/literature.d/LIT-383.md) | Deciding Advantageously Before Knowing the Advantageous Strategy | Bechara, H. Damasio, Tranel & A. R. Damasio (1997), DOI-10.1126/science.275.5304.1293. In the four-deck gambling task, 10 controls and 6 ventromedial prefrontal patients were tracked on choices, anticipatory skin-conductance responses and verbal reports. All controls and no patients developed anticipatory responses; controls came to prefer the good decks, significantly from the point they reported a hunch; three patients who could state the correct strategy kept choosing the bad decks. The authors conclude that nonconscious biases guide behaviour before conscious knowledge does. The statistics support the group difference more firmly than the "before knowing" timing. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-331](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-331.md)); worth reading as the experiment the somatic marker hypothesis is usually cited for, and worth reading with its notes, which are weaker than its abstract. With 10 controls and 6 ventromedial patients, the robust results are that every control and no patient developed anticipatory skin-conductance responses, and that three patients who could state the right strategy still chose badly. The headline that controls chose well and responded to risky decks "before they knew" is only partly carried by the statistics: in the pre-hunch period the controls' preference for the good decks was not significant (note 9), and the reported SCR effect there is a group-by-period interaction, not a bad-versus-good-deck difference (note 8). Controls chose the good decks significantly only once they reported a hunch. Whether the account is true is not this note's question. |
| [LIT-384](../../record/literature.d/LIT-384.md) | The somatic marker hypothesis and the possible functions of the prefrontal cortex | Damasio (1996), DOI-10.1098/rstb.1996.0125. States the somatic marker hypothesis: ventromedial prefrontal cortex holds learned links between classes of situation and the body states (emotions) that went with them; when a situation recurs, those states are re-enacted, through the body or through an "as if" loop in somatosensory cortex, and mark imagined option-outcome pairs as good or bad, overtly as a feeling or covertly as a bias, so that reasoning works over a pruned space. Evidence summarised: ventromedial patients with intact intellect decide badly in life, lack skin-conductance responses to emotive pictures, and in the gambling task keep choosing the losing decks and show no anticipatory responses. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-332](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-332.md)); worth reading as the shortest statement of the somatic marker hypothesis by its author, with the evidence it then rested on. It is a hypothesis paper: the mechanism (ventromedial prefrontal linkages that re-enact body states, by a "body loop" or an "as if body loop", which then mark options good or bad) is stated, not shown, and the evidence it reports is lesion and skin-conductance work summarised from other papers, without numbers. Its strongest piece, that ventromedial patients lack anticipatory skin-conductance responses in the gambling task while responding normally to the losses themselves, is reported here in one paragraph; the 1997 Science report filed alongside it is the place to see that result. Whether the account is true is not this note's question. |
| [LIT-385](../../record/literature.d/LIT-385.md) | Time-locked multiregional retroactivation: A systems-level proposal for the neural substrates of recall and recognition | Damasio (1989), Cognition 33:25–62. The convergence-zone proposal: memory is not held in one store but as feature fragments in early sensory and motor cortices, with amodal records of their co-occurrence held in local and non-local convergence zones downstream; recall is the time-locked reactivation of the fragments, directed from convergence zones through feedback projections. Unread: registered from the abstract. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-01 at the owner's request for Antonio Damasio's work; no full text could be reached (the Elsevier article is closed, and no repository, author or course copy was found), so it is registered from its PubMed abstract only, unread, and no NOTE is filed |
| [LIT-386](../../record/literature.d/LIT-386.md) | Homeostasis and soft robotics in the design of feeling machines | Man & Damasio (2019), Nature Machine Intelligence 1:446–452. A perspective proposing machines organized around homeostasis: a body that must be kept within viable states gives the machine a goal of its own, and something like feeling as a source of motivation and of evaluation. Soft robotics and multisensory abstraction are named as the means. The claimed payoffs are equivalents of feeling, better function across environments, and a platform for studying consciousness. Unread: placed from the abstract. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-01 at the owner's request; no full text could be reached, so it is placed from the abstract and figure captions only and no NOTE is filed. Nature's page is paywalled, there is no arXiv version, Unpaywall, OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar list no open copy, and the ResearchGate, Scribd and ProQuest copies did not serve the text. By its abstract it is a design proposal for machines built around homeostasis, not a reported system or result. Flagged anthology-candidate because the anthology's agents-and-environments topic could hold it; a reading should settle whether it carries an instruction for ML practice. |
| [LIT-387](../../record/literature.d/LIT-387.md) | Feelings Are the Source of Consciousness | Damasio & Damasio (2023), Neural Computation 35(3):277–286. A "view" piece. Consciousness is the spontaneous identification of mind contents as belonging to an organism, and a continuous flow of homeostatic feelings provides it. Those feelings were the first phenomena of consciousness in evolution. They arise from a two-way interaction between the interoceptive nervous system and the viscera and their chemistry, so they are hybrid, at once neural and bodily, which the authors offer as a solution to the mind-body problem. Unread: placed from the abstract. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-01 at the owner's request; the full text exists as a free (bronze) PDF on MIT Press, but the site refused every automated request with a bot challenge (HTTP 403), and no repository, PubMed Central or preprint copy exists, so it is placed from the abstract only and no NOTE is filed. It is not the "Damasio & Damasio 2023" that [NOTE-120](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-120.md) reports as a critic of plant sentience: that is a different work, an Animal Sentience commentary. |
| [LIT-388](../../record/literature.d/LIT-388.md) | The nature of feelings: evolutionary and neurobiological origins | Damasio & Carvalho (2013), Nature Reviews Neuroscience 14:143–152. Feelings are mental experiences of body states, and they report homeostatic value: a valenced read-out of interoceptive maps that lets an organism learn and anticipate what restores or threatens its physiology. The first integrated whole-body maps are in the upper brainstem (parabrachial nucleus, periaqueductal grey, deep superior colliculus); the insula refines feeling but is not needed for it, since a patient with both insulae destroyed still feels pain, pleasure, hunger and compassion. The authors propose, without evidence of their own, that feeling starts in unmyelinated interoceptive fibres open to ions and ligands along their length. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-330](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-330.md)); worth reading as the shortest statement of Damasio's account of feeling as the felt read-out of homeostatic body maps, with its two empirical planks: patients whose insulae are destroyed still feel, and the upper brainstem is where whole-body interoceptive maps first assemble. It is an Opinion piece with no new data. The systems-level case rests on lesion and stimulation evidence it cites; the cellular proposal (unmyelinated interoceptive fibres and ephaptic coupling as the root of sentience) is offered as a hypothesis and stays one. |
| [LIT-389](../../record/literature.d/LIT-389.md) v2 | Some Possible Roles for Topos Theory in Quantum Theory and Quantum Gravity | Isham & Butterfield (1999), arXiv:gr-qc/9910005. Isham & Butterfield argue, without proving anything new, that presheaf toposes over a base of "contexts" suit physics because the subobject classifier Ω is fixed by the base. They say this answers the objection that many-valued logics are arbitrary (§2.2), and that it keeps the logic distributive (§1.1). They then sketch seven applications. Five are speculative one-paragraph proposals (frames, observers, causal sets, TQFT, synthetic-differential-geometry time in continuous histories). One (§3.7) recaps their Kochen–Specker results: KS ⇔ the dual presheaf D(Â) = Hom(W_A, {0,1}) on the operator category O has no global sections (dim H > 2), and every state ψ gives a FUNC-obeying sieve-valued valuation ν^ψ(A ∈ Δ) = {f_O : Â → B̂ \| Ê[B ∈ f(Δ)]ψ = ψ}. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-334](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-334.md)); worth reading only as the programme's short, informal manifesto as of 1999, written before parts III–IV appeared. It joins the Kochen–Specker valuations to the continuum scepticism (real-valued quantities, space and probabilities) that the Döring–Isham programme later develops, and the Literalism/Extra-Values framing appears nowhere else in the record. Its one technical section is a recap of KS I–II, and a reader of [LIT-325](../../record/literature.d/LIT-325.md), [LIT-381](../../record/literature.d/LIT-381.md) and [LIT-378](../../record/literature.d/LIT-378.md) loses little by skipping it. |
| [LIT-390](../../record/literature.d/LIT-390.md) v2 | Topos Theory and Consistent Histories: The Internal Logic of the Set of All Consistent Sets | Isham (1996), arXiv:gr-qc/9607069. Isham takes the poset B of all Boolean subalgebras ("windows") of the history orthoalgebra UP, ordered by coarse-graining, and shows that the presheaf A_d(W) = {α \| some d-consistent W′ ⊇ W contains α} is a subobject of the constant presheaf ΔUP, whereas the naive "realisable in W" assignment is not a presheaf at all (§4.1–4.2). The probability claim ⟨α, p⟩ then gets the sieve-valued semantic value {W ⊆ W₀ \| ∃ d-consistent W′ ⊇ W with α ∈ W′} if d(α, α) = p and ∅ otherwise (4.16), a global element 1 → Ω in Set^B, so the logic of "all consistent sets at once" is the distributive but non-Boolean Heyting algebra of sieves on B. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-333](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-333.md)); worth reading as the origin of the Isham school's sieve-valued, coarse-graining truth values, which the Kochen–Specker series names as its motivation (KS I, ref. [13]); it is the only place the construction is applied to consistent histories, it is short and elementary, and its constructions are correct as stated, but read it knowing that the Heyting values measure only whether α can be embedded in a consistent set (the probability d(α, α) is the same in every window), so it says nothing about the conflicts between consistent sets that motivate it. |
| [LIT-391](../../record/literature.d/LIT-391.md) | Group Agency: The Possibility, Design, and Status of Corporate Agents | List & Pettit (2011), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199591565.001.0001. Per its publisher, the book argues that "there really are group or corporate agents, over and above the individual agents who compose them", and that social science, law, morality and politics must take account of this. The account is said to be "entirely unmysterious" and grounded in social choice theory, economics and philosophy. Three parts: the logical possibility of group agents (conditions of agency, aggregation of attitudes, structure), their organisational design (epistemic, incentive-compatibility and control desiderata), and their normative status (responsibility, personhood, identification). Unread: filed from the description and contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (Oxford Academic is paywalled, and neither author has posted the book or draft chapters), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the book-length case that organised groups are agents over and above their members, which is the composition side's premise in the Minsky–Schwitzgebel bridge; whether it says anything about group consciousness is unknown from what was seen. |
| [LIT-392](../../record/literature.d/LIT-392.md) | Absent Qualia, Fading Qualia, Dancing Qualia | Chalmers (1995), in T. Metzinger (ed.), Conscious Experience (Imprint Academic, co-published in Paderborn). Principle: any system with the same fine-grained functional organisation as a conscious system has qualitatively identical experiences. Replace neurons gradually with silicon chips that keep the organisation. If the end point lacked experience, a midpoint would be a rational being wrong about all its experience ("fading qualia"). If it differed in experience, a switch between two circuits would flip experience with no possible noticing ("dancing qualia"). Both are judged implausible, so absent and inverted qualia are nomologically impossible. The argument extends explicitly to a population of homunculi. The result is "nonreductive functionalism": organisation fixes experience with natural, not metaphysical, necessity. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-347](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-347.md)); worth reading as the full statement of the principle of organizational invariance and the fading- and dancing-qualia arguments for it. Cite it carefully: the principle is conditional on fine-grained functional isomorphism to a system already conscious. It covers silicon replacement and, by the paper's own §3 extension, a population of homunculi that implements a human's neural organisation. It does not cover a system whose organisation is merely similar in kind to a conscious one, so it does not license Schwitzgebel's step to the United States ([LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md)). The conclusion is nomological, not metaphysical ("nonreductive functionalism"), and it is a plausibility argument by the author's own account. |
| [LIT-393](../../record/literature.d/LIT-393.md) | Artificial Intelligence as Philosophy and as Psychology | Dennett (1978), ch. 7 of Brainstorms; also in Ringle (ed.) 1979. Unread here. By Stich's 1980 summary, which Dennett accepted: the worry that internal representations need a homunculus to read them ("Hume's Problem") is unfounded, because homunculi are harmless if they are stupider than the whole. A "team or committee of relatively ignorant, narrow-minded, blind homunculi" can produce intelligent behaviour, each explained by stupider ones, until they can be "replaced by a machine"; AI organises "armies of such idiots". | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from catalogue records and a contemporary summary, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. Brainstorms (1978) is lending-only at the Internet Archive, the MIT Press and Project MUSE chapters are paywalled (MUSE: "Open Access: No"), the Tufts Digital Library holds Dennett's papers but not this essay, and PhilPapers was blocked. What the essay says about homunculi is known here only through Stich's 1980 critical notice of Brainstorms, which quotes it with page numbers and which Dennett, replying in the same issue, said he had "no quarrel with". It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-394](../../record/literature.d/LIT-394.md) | Computing Machinery and Intelligence | Turing (1950), Mind 59(236):433–460. "Can machines think?" is replaced by whether a digital computer can play the imitation game so that an interrogator, questioning by teleprinter, identifies it no more reliably than he tells a man from a woman. Universality reduces the question to one computer suitably programmed. Turing predicts that by about 2000 a machine with 10^9 bits of storage will leave an average interrogator no more than a 70 per cent chance of a right identification after five minutes. He answers nine objections, the argument from consciousness by its collapse into solipsism, and proposes reaching the goal by educating a child-machine. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-355](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-355.md)); worth reading as the source of the imitation game and as the first catalogue of objections to machine thought, nine of them, each still argued. Read it for what it actually proposes: not a definition of thinking but the replacement of an ill-posed question by a test, a prediction about 2000 (70 per cent right identification after five minutes, at 10^9 bits), and a programme for reaching it by educating a child-machine. Its reply to the argument from consciousness is an other-minds argument, not a theory of consciousness, and Turing says outright that he does not think there is "no mystery about consciousness". |
| [LIT-395](../../record/literature.d/LIT-395.md) | The Combination Problem for Panpsychism | Chalmers (2016/2017), in Brüntrup & Jaskolla (eds), Panpsychism: Contemporary Perspectives, OUP, pp. 179–214. How could micro-experiences constitute a macro-subject? The problem splits into subject, quality and structure versions; each yields an argument against constitutive (Russellian) panpsychism, of which the subject-summing and structural mismatch arguments are the strongest. Emergent, autonomous and identity panpsychism avoid combination at a cost in mental causation or physics. Phenomenal bonding, quantum holism, small palettes and informational composition are judged most worth pursuing. No solution is offered. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-358](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-358.md)), from the author's posted draft, not the published chapter; worth reading as the map of the combination problem: it splits the problem into subject, quality and structure versions (plus unity, boundary, awareness and grain), turns each into an argument, and sorts every response into emergent, autonomous, identity or combinatorial panpsychism. It solves nothing and says so. Its conclusions are judgements of promise ("phenomenal bonding or quantum holism", "principles of informational composition"), not results. For this record its value is that the subject-summing argument is James's argument against group minds, and that its footnote 7 objects to IIT's exclusion postulate on grounds that have nothing to do with groups. |
| [LIT-396](../../record/literature.d/LIT-396.md) | Psychological Predicates | Putnam (1967), in Capitan & Merrill (eds.), Art, Mind, and Religion (Proceedings of the 1965 Oberlin Colloquium in Philosophy), University of Pittsburgh Press, pp. 37–48; reprinted as "The Nature of Mental States". It proposes, as an empirical hypothesis, that pain is a functional state of a whole organism, a state of a probabilistic automaton fixed by its machine table, rather than a brain state. Condition (3) of the hypothesis is the anti-nesting stipulation: no pain-feeler "possesses a decomposition into parts which separately possess Descriptions" of the pain-capable kind, introduced only "to rule out such 'organisms' (if they can count as such) as swarms of bees as single pain-feelers". | Deferred — not read in full: the original (Art, Mind, and Religion, pp. 37–48) is closed on JSTOR. The University of Pittsburgh's page images sit behind a bot challenge I could not pass, and the Internet Archive copy of the 1975 reprint is print-disabled only. What was read is MIT's 24.09x course excerpt of the 1975 reprint ("The Nature of Mental States"), recorded as a Skimmed reading in [NOTE-335](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-335.md). That excerpt carries the four-clause hypothesis verbatim, including the anti-nesting condition (3) and its one-sentence motive. |
| [LIT-397](../../record/literature.d/LIT-397.md) | Facing Backwards on the Problem of Consciousness | Dennett (1996), Journal of Consciousness Studies 3(1):4–6, a reply to Chalmers's "Facing Up". The easy/hard split is "a major misdirector of attention, an illusion-generator". A vitalist could pose the same "hard problem" of life, and a neuroscientist who set perception aside as hard would have misdescribed vision. Chalmers's reason why consciousness is different repeats the claim. Subtract every function from one's own consciousness and nothing is left but a conviction. Positing experience as fundamental, like mass and charge, needs independent evidence, which is not given ("cutism"). | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-346](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-346.md)); worth reading as the three-page statement of the deflationary reply to the hard problem, and as the source of the vitalism, "Crock" and "cutism" analogies. Its one argued point is a challenge, not a refutation: Chalmers owes independent evidence for experience as a fundamental property, the way physics has for mass and charge, and without it the posit does no explanatory work. Its central premise, that once every function is subtracted from one's own consciousness "there is nothing left", is asserted from the author's introspective catalogue. |
| [LIT-398](../../record/literature.d/LIT-398.md) | True Believers: The Intentional Strategy and Why It Works | Dennett (1981; revised 1987, read in Mind Design II, 1997). Belief is "a perfectly objective phenomenon" that can be discerned only by one who adopts the intentional strategy. To be a true believer is to be a system "whose behavior is reliably and voluminously predictable" by it. The choice of stance is free, but its success is objective: Laplacean Martians who skipped it would miss real patterns. Thermostat and person differ only by degree. Why the strategy works is answered easily by evolution, and not yet at all by mechanism. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-353](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-353.md)), in the revised text reprinted in Mind Design II (1997), not the 1981 printing; worth reading as Dennett's statement that belief is objective although only visible through a strategy: anything whose behaviour is "reliably and voluminously predictable" by the intentional strategy is a believer "in the fullest sense of the word". It turns the 1971 paper's observer-relativity ([LIT-440](../../record/literature.d/LIT-440.md)) into "the facts about the success or failure of the stance … are perfectly objective", introduces the Martian thought experiment and the intentional "real patterns" later worked out in [LIT-220](../../record/literature.d/LIT-220.md), and runs the thermostat-to-person continuum ("no magic moment"). Its criterion admits any system on which the strategy pays, groups included, but the essay never applies it to a group. |
| [LIT-399](../../record/literature.d/LIT-399.md) | Plain Talk About Neurodevelopmental Epistemology | Minsky (1977), MIT AI Memo 430, also IJCAI-77 pp. 1083–1092. Takes the mind as an organised society of simple "agents", a theory credited to joint work with Papert, and asks how such agents could communicate without language. The proposal: no messages at all, since each argument lives at a fixed location on shared channels ("c-lines") that the agents themselves constitute as short-term memory. A specificity gradient confines low-level agents to local sub-societies, and a case-shift moves an object of attention into a better-described slot. The paper conjectures that these internal cases precede, and shape, the cases of natural language. Openly speculative. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-344](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-344.md)); worth reading as the earliest of Minsky's own papers in this record to state the society-of-agents view, then called "The Society of Minds" (Note 1), and as its first worked mechanism: agents communicate by fixed-location channels ("c-lines") instead of messages. It is openly speculative ("a model of scientific irresponsibility", p. 1085), with no data and no program. Read it for the communication scheme, the "specificity gradient", the case-shift mechanism and the conjecture that cognitive cases precede and shape linguistic ones; do not read it as evidence for any of them. That it is the first published statement of the idea is the usual attribution, and this reading could not confirm it (see [NOTE-344](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-344.md)). |
| [LIT-400](../../record/literature.d/LIT-400.md) | Minds, brains, and programs | Searle (1980), Behavioral and Brain Sciences 3(3):417–424. A man who knows no Chinese, locked in a room with rules in English for manipulating Chinese symbols, can answer Chinese questions as well as a native speaker yet understands nothing; so instantiating a program is never by itself sufficient for intentionality ("strong AI" is false). Six replies are answered, the systems reply by letting the man internalise the whole room. Only something with causal powers equivalent to a brain's could think; programs have syntax but no semantics. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-349](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-349.md)); worth reading as the source of the Chinese room and of the six replies (systems, robot, brain simulator, combination, other minds, many mansions) the debate still uses, in Searle's own words. Read it for its scope: the target is "strong AI", the claim that instantiating the right program is by itself sufficient for understanding, and Searle grants that machines, including artifacts with the brain's causal powers, could think. The argument is about understanding and intentionality; consciousness is mentioned, not argued. Its answer to the systems reply (internalise the room) is the part that bears on minds of parts, and it is asserted rather than shown: Searle says the subsystem is "simply a part of" the man, which is what the reply denies. |
| [LIT-401](../../record/literature.d/LIT-401.md) | What is it like to be a group agent? | List (2016), DOI-10.1111/nous.12162. Group agents (firms, courts, states) are real agents in a thin functionalist sense, and they can have consciousness as awareness. Whether they have phenomenal consciousness depends on which psycho-physical bridge principle is true. Under IIT, List argues informally that a group agent's Φ is low: groups decompose into self-contained units, their steering bodies keep working when partly randomised, and most of their processing happens inside individuals. The exclusion postulate would deny group consciousness even if group Φ were high. So group agents are (close to) not conscious, which List uses to ground a normative asymmetry: groups have only derivative rights. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-340](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-340.md)); the standard reply to [LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md) from the group-agency side: it grants that organised groups are agents with functional awareness and argues, conditionally on IIT, that they have little or no phenomenal consciousness. Worth reading for its separation of agency from experience and for its three reasons why a group agent's Φ should be low. The low-Φ verdict is argued informally, with no Φ computed for any group, and the paper never answers the reply Schwitzgebel had already made to the objection its third reason restates. |
| [LIT-402](../../record/literature.d/LIT-402.md) | K-Lines: A Theory of Memory | Minsky (1979/1980), MIT AI Memo 516; Cognitive Science 4(2):117–133. "The function of a memory is to re-create a state of mind." On a memorable event a K-node is made, and its K-line attaches to the agents then active. Reactivated, it re-imposes that partial mental state, but by the level-band principle only on an intermediate band of levels, so the present is seen as an instance of the past without hallucinating the old answer. K-lines attach mainly to earlier K-nodes, forming a K-pyramid against the perceptual P-pyramid, and cross-exclusion lets conflicting details cancel into abstraction. Read from the memo; a programme statement, with no simulation. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 from the MIT AI Memo 516 text ([NOTE-352](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-352.md)), not the Cognitive Science version, which was not reachable; worth reading as the society-of-mind programme's theory of memory and the first place Minsky's own text uses the phrase "Society of Mind". The idea to take away is that a memory re-creates a partial mental state rather than retrieving a stored description, and its level-band principle: re-enact only an intermediate band of the original state, so the present problem is seen as an instance of the remembered one. The second half is, by its author's account, not constructive ("from this point, the reader can assume that difficulties in understanding are my fault"), and nothing is simulated or measured. |
| [LIT-403](../../record/literature.d/LIT-403.md) | Intrinsic content, active memory and the extended mind | Clark (2005), DOI-10.1093/analys/65.1.1. Against Adams & Aizawa: the intrinsic-content objection to [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md) fails, because the notion is unclear, silicon parts could carry it, and a conventional encoding linked at run-time to intrinsically contentful states can be a belief's vehicle, inside or outside the head. Against the active-memory objection, the notebook's passivity is a difference below the functional level that matters. The extended mind is a thesis about vehicles. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-363](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-363.md)); worth reading as Clark's first reply to the critics of [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md), and short. It answers Adams and Aizawa's intrinsic-content objection three ways (the notion is unclear, silicon could carry it, and it would not matter), turning their own concession that not every cognitive state needs non-derived content against them; and it answers the "active memory" objection by conceding the notebook is unlike biological memory and arguing that the differences sit below the level a functionalist should attend to. Its replies are parity arguments with imagined cases; it adds no new evidence. It does not engage Rupert ([LIT-422](../../record/literature.d/LIT-422.md)). |
| [LIT-404](../../record/literature.d/LIT-404.md) | Does a rock implement every finite-state automaton? | Chalmers (1996), Synthese 108(3):309–333. Putnam's proof that every open system implements every finite-state automaton fails, because implementation needs reliable, counterfactual-supporting transitions for every formal state. Repaired, it survives only for weak formalisms: a clock and a dial implement every inputless FSA, and an input memory and a dial upgrade any system with the right input–output dependencies to every FSA with them. Combinatorial-state automata, whose states are vectors of independently varying components in distinct physical regions, put a strong constraint on implementation. That keeps computation available as a foundation for the theory of mind. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-342](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-342.md)), from the author's HTML copy, not the Synthese typeset text; worth reading as the standard reply to Putnam's and Searle's triviality arguments and as the source of the combinatorial-state automaton (CSA). Its result is real but partial: implementation needs counterfactual-supporting transitions over all formal states, inputless FSAs are trivially implemented by any clock plus dial, FSAs with input are implemented by any system with the right input–output dependencies plus an input memory and a dial, and CSAs escape only because a blind implementation must grow by a factor of n per step. Chalmers leaves two open problems (logically possible false implementations, and an overstrict one-region-per-component condition). For this record it decides which formalism a China-brain argument has to be stated in, and it lets one physical system host two minds. |
| [LIT-405](../../record/literature.d/LIT-405.md) | Where Am I? | Dennett (1978), the closing essay of Brainstorms. A narrator's brain ("Yorick") is kept in a vat in Houston and radio-linked to his body ("Hamlet") under Tulsa. Where is he? Not simply where the body is, and not simply where the brain is: he cannot think "here" of the vat. He favours being wherever his point of view is, finds that unclear, and when the links fail finds himself "in Houston". A computer duplicate ("Hubert") runs in step with Yorick, and either can drive a new body with no detectable switch.
