The essay ends when the two drift out of step and a second person speaks through the body. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-338](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-338.md)), in a retyped course copy, not the printed book; worth reading as the founding thought experiment on where a person is when brain and body are apart, and the place the record's threads on extended vehicles, scattered individuals and functional duplicates meet. It is fiction with a philosophical argument inside it. It tries three answers to "where am I?" (where the body is, where the brain is, where the point of view is), favours the third, admits it is unclear, and ends by showing that two synchronised brains can drift into two persons. It argues by intuition pump and settles nothing; the narrator calls one of its answers a truth "of underwhelming significance". |
| [LIT-406](../../record/literature.d/LIT-406.md) | Facing Up to the Problem of Consciousness | Chalmers (1995), Journal of Consciousness Studies 2(3):200–219. The "easy" problems of consciousness (discrimination, integration, report, access, attention, control, wakefulness) are problems of explaining functions, and a mechanism that performs a function explains it. The hard problem is that after every such function is explained, "why is the performance of these functions accompanied by experience?" stays open, so reductive methods cannot answer it. Experience should be taken as fundamental, linked to the physical by psychophysical laws. Two nonbasic candidates are offered, structural coherence (the structure of experience mirrors the structure of awareness) and organizational invariance (fine-grained functional isomorphs have the same experiences), with a speculative double-aspect theory of information as the basic law. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-336](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-336.md)); worth reading as the source of the easy/hard distinction, and short. Cite it for three separable things: the hard problem as the claim that explaining every function near experience leaves "why is this accompanied by experience?" open (§§2–3); the outline of a nonreductive theory built from two nonbasic principles, structural coherence and organizational invariance (§7); and the double-aspect theory of information, which the author calls "more of an idea than a theory" (§8). The organizational-invariance argument here is a one-page sketch; the full version is [LIT-392](../../record/literature.d/LIT-392.md). |
| [LIT-407](../../record/literature.d/LIT-407.md) | Troubles with Functionalism | Block (1978), Minnesota Studies in the Philosophy of Science 9:261–325. Functionalism ties mental states to the world only through inputs and outputs, so it is "liberal": a body run by a billion people with radios, each implementing one line of your machine table (the nation-of-China system), would be functionally equivalent to you, yet there is prima facie doubt that it has qualia. Putnam's stipulation that no pain-feeler has parts with pain-feeler organisation is an ad hoc patch that fails on its own terms. Psychofunctionalism escapes the doubt for beliefs but perhaps not for qualia, and no specification of inputs and outputs avoids both liberalism and chauvinism. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-366](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-366.md)); worth reading as the source of the homunculi-headed robot and the nation-of-China system, and of the first published objection to Putnam's anti-nesting stipulation. Cite it for two things that are easy to run together: the Absent Qualia Argument (a Functional simulation built from people is prima facie without qualia), and the case that Putnam's condition is ad hoc (§1.5). Block grounds the doubt about the China system in its being unlike us psychologically and neurally and designed to mimic us, not in its parts having minds (n. 19). He also grants that a Psychofunctional homunculi-head could have beliefs (§2.1). So it is not a source for "a whole made of minded parts cannot be conscious". |
| [LIT-408](../../record/literature.d/LIT-408.md) | Jokes and the Logic of the Cognitive Unconscious | Minsky (1980), MIT AI Memo 603; in Vaina & Hintikka (eds.), Cognitive Constraints on Communication (Reidel, 1984), pp. 175–200. Commonsense logic cannot be made reliable, so each mind accumulates "censors" that learn the precursors of known-bad thought (paradox, circling, false analogy) and suppress it before it occurs. On that view, humour is censor-learning. Its common element is an "unexpected frame-substitution", and laughter both stops the absurd line of thought and holds it in focus. Logical nonsense is then as "naughty" as Freud's forbidden wishes, which removes the gap in Freud's theory. An essay with no data or program. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 from the MIT AI Memo 603 text ([NOTE-361](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-361.md)), not the 1984 book chapter, which was not reachable; worth reading as the society-of-mind programme's account of humour and of "negative meta-knowledge". Its argument is that commonsense reasoning cannot be made reliable, so minds learn "censors" that suppress known-bad lines of thought, and that humour and laughter are how those censors are taught and engaged, which dissolves Freud's puzzle about nonsense jokes. It is an essay: no data, no program, and its evolutionary story is offered as "no plea for this particular scenario". |
| [LIT-409](../../record/literature.d/LIT-409.md) | How a materialist can deny that the United States is probably conscious – response to Schwitzgebel | Kammerer (2015), DOI-10.1007/s11406-015-9653-z. A short reply to [LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md) proposing the Sophisticated Anti-Nesting Principle (SAP). A whole W that has a functional property P normally sufficient for a conscious state S lacks S when some part of W plays a role that (A) requires the part to have conscious states representing W and (B) is indispensable: without some part playing such a role, W would lack P. The SAP keeps Schwitzgebel's antheads and Block's tiny-people neurons conscious, admits some group consciousness, and denies it to the US, whose citizens represent the US and could not be replaced by parts that do not. It is justified by an Occam-style rule against ascribing consciousness where other subjects' states explain the behaviour. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-362](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-362.md)); the most careful anti-nesting principle in the record, and the one aimed directly at [LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md). Worth reading because it draws the line that matters for the society-of-mind bridge: a whole is denied a conscious state only when the functional organisation that would ground it runs through parts that consciously represent the whole and could not be replaced by parts that do not. Its application to the United States rests on an asserted counterfactual, and its justification is an Occam-style principle the author says needs further work. Schwitzgebel replied in Philosophia 44 (2016). |
| [LIT-410](../../record/literature.d/LIT-410.md) | A Framework for Representing Knowledge | Minsky (1974), MIT AI Memo 306. A self-declared partial theory of thinking: on meeting a situation one retrieves a frame, a stereotyped structure whose terminals carry conditions and weakly bound default assignments, and adapts it by replacing details. Frames that share terminals form frame-systems whose transformations stand for moves, actions or changes of viewpoint, and failed matches are routed through a learned similarity network. The memo applies this to vision, imagery, language, memory and problem solving, and ends by arguing that consistency-seeking logical systems cannot represent commonsense knowledge, because they are monotonic. Nothing is implemented or tested. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-337](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-337.md)); worth reading as the source of the frame idea and of the critique of "logistic" representation that later motivated non-monotonic reasoning. It is a proposal, not a result: no program is described as built, nothing is measured, and the author says so ("Apology!", p. 2). The parts to take away are the frame with weakly bound default assignments (§1.11), frame-systems sharing terminals across viewpoints (§1), terminals read as questions (§2.8), the similarity network with cluster "capitols" as an account of family resemblance (§§3.4–3.5), and the appendix on monotonicity and consistency (§6). It contains no society-of-agents idea; that arrives in Plain Talk ([LIT-399](../../record/literature.d/LIT-399.md)), which calls itself in part a sequel to this memo. |
| [LIT-411](../../record/literature.d/LIT-411.md) | Could a Large Language Model be Conscious? | Chalmers (2023), arXiv:2303.07103; Boston Review, 9 August 2023; from a NeurIPS 2022 invited talk. Asks for a feature X that LLMs have (or lack) and that indicates consciousness (or its absence). Self-report, seeming-conscious, conversation and general intelligence give at most weak evidence for it. Biology, senses and embodiment, world and self models, recurrent processing, a global workspace and unified agency are the evidence against. All but biology are temporary. On mainstream assumptions: under 10% credence for current LLMs, 25% or more for conscious LLM+ systems within a decade. Twelve challenges are offered as roadmap or red flags. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-345](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-345.md)), from arXiv v3 (the Boston Review text), with v1's conclusions compared; worth reading as the clearest short statement of the case for and against consciousness in LLMs, and as the origin of the "theory-balanced" credence approach that Butlin et al. built on. Its method is a regimented request for a feature X, and its verdicts are credences offered "for illustrative purposes": under 10% for current LLMs, 25% or more for conscious LLM+ systems within a decade, on "mainstream assumptions". It does not break the deadlock [THEORY-023](../../record/theory.d/THEORY-023.md) describes. It is an instance of it: mimicry undercuts self-report, the main obstacles are theory-derived, and biology is set aside rather than answered. |
| [LIT-412](../../record/literature.d/LIT-412.md) | Supersizing the Mind: Embodiment, Action, and Cognitive Extension | Clark (2008), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780195333213.001.0001. Clark's book-length defence of the extended mind: a tour of embodied, situated and extended cognition, a reply to its critics, and a case for treating "some coalitions of biological and non-biological resources as the extended cognitive circuitry of individual minds". Unread: filed from the publisher's description and contents. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's abstract, its chapter list and its two front- and back-matter pieces that this record holds separately; the book's full text is not lawfully readable online, so it was not read and no NOTE is filed |
| [LIT-413](../../record/literature.d/LIT-413.md) | The Mind's I: Fantasies and Reflections on Self and Soul | Hofstadter & Dennett (eds.) (1981), Basic Books. An anthology of 27 selections, fiction and philosophy, on self, soul and consciousness, each followed by a "Reflections" by the editors. Among them are Turing's imitation-game paper ([LIT-394](../../record/literature.d/LIT-394.md)), Searle's Chinese room ([LIT-400](../../record/literature.d/LIT-400.md)), Nagel's bat ([LIT-096](../../record/literature.d/LIT-096.md)), Dennett's "Where Am I?" and Hofstadter's "Prelude ... Ant Fugue" from GEB ([LIT-437](../../record/literature.d/LIT-437.md)). Unread: registered from the catalogue record and a contents note; the Reflections, which are the editors' own contribution, were not seen. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the Library of Congress catalogue record, the publisher's page and a library contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scans of the 1981 Basic Books, Harvester, Bantam and Penguin printings are all lending-only, HathiTrust has no record for the LCCN, and no publisher, editor or Tufts-archive copy is posted. The editors' Reflections are unread. Two of the selections were read in full from lawful copies of their first publications and filed on their own: Turing ([LIT-394](../../record/literature.d/LIT-394.md)) and Searle ([LIT-400](../../record/literature.d/LIT-400.md)). |
| [LIT-414](../../record/literature.d/LIT-414.md) | High-level perception, representation, and analogy: A critique of artificial intelligence methodology | Chalmers, French & Hofstadter (1992), DOI-10.1080/09528139208953747. Traditional AI hands its programs representations built by people who already know the answer, so models like BACON (Kepler's third law) and the Structure-Mapping Engine (atom and solar system) skip the hard part, high-level perception: deciding what is relevant and how to organise it. Nor can the gap be filled later by a separate "representation module", because analogy shapes perception and the task decides which representation is right. Copycat, in a microdomain of letter strings, is offered as an architecture that builds representations and mappings together. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-359](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-359.md)), from the authors' preprint (CRCC Technical Report 49, March 1991) that Chalmers posts; the published text was not seen. Worth reading as the clearest short statement of the Fluid Analogies Research Group's case against hand-coded representations, and as the most accessible description of Copycat. It is an argument, not a result: its two arguments against a separate "representation module" are informal and good, its case studies (BACON, SME) are fair as far as they go, and the evidence it offers for Copycat is a description of the architecture and one worked problem, with nothing measured in the paper itself. |
| [LIT-415](../../record/literature.d/LIT-415.md) | Extended Cognition and Extended Consciousness | Chalmers (2019), DOI-10.1093/oso/9780190662813.003.0002. The 1998 extended-mind thesis as stated is too weak, since opponents accept extension in wired-in "extended circuit" cases. The interesting thesis is that mental states can be partly constituted by external entities in virtue of the subject's sensorimotor interaction with them. Consciousness does not extend this way, on the proposal that it requires information directly available for global control, and perceptually mediated availability is indirect. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-356](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-356.md)); worth reading as Chalmers's own later statement of what the extended-mind thesis of [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md) is and where it stops. It does two things. It replaces the 1998 paper's implicit thesis, which it concedes is too weak because even the critics accept it for wired-in "extended circuit" cases, with one that requires constitution "in virtue of the subject's sensorimotor interaction" (§1). And it argues that consciousness does not extend, because consciousness needs information directly available for global control and perception makes availability indirect (§3). Both are proposals, argued in a short chapter: the thesis is offered as "extensionally correct", and the directness account is offered as an explanation of why there are no plausible cases, not as a knockdown. |
| [LIT-416](../../record/literature.d/LIT-416.md) | Is there an 'I' in AI? | Hofstadter (2026), DOI-10.1098/rsta.2024.0527. The lead essay of the Phil. Trans. A theme issue on world models whose editorial is [LIT-195](../../record/literature.d/LIT-195.md). Unread: what is known of it here comes from the editorial as read in [NOTE-136](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-136.md), which quotes Hofstadter that a system passing a Turing test of consistent understanding should be credited with thinking, and that conscious AI may come "perhaps even by 2033". Whether the essay argues "nearly the opposite" of the editors' convergence verdict cannot be checked until the essay itself is read. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from Crossref, PubMed and the record's reading of the theme-issue editorial ([LIT-195](../../record/literature.d/LIT-195.md), [NOTE-136](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-136.md)), not read: royalsocietypublishing.org returned 403 to the DOI, article and PDF URLs, the article is not in PubMed Central or Europe PMC full text, and no repository or author copy was found. No NOTE is filed. |
| [LIT-417](../../record/literature.d/LIT-417.md) | Epilogue: Analogy as the Core of Cognition | Hofstadter (2001), DOI-10.7551/mitpress/1251.003.0020. The epilogue to Gentner, Holyoak & Kokinov's The Analogical Mind (MIT Press), pp. 499–538, whose title states the Fluid Analogies Research Group's thesis that analogy-making is not one cognitive tool among others but the core of cognition. Unread: no lawful full text was reached. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from Crossref and the MIT Press chapter record, not read: MIT Press Direct returned 403 to the chapter PDF and book page, OpenAlex lists the chapter as closed access, and no author or institutional copy was found. Third-party copies exist (a personal reference collection and an Internet Archive upload) but neither is the author's or the publisher's, so neither was used. No NOTE is filed. |
| [LIT-418](../../record/literature.d/LIT-418.md) | Society of mind: A response to four reviews | Minsky (1991), Artificial Intelligence 48(3):371–396. Minsky's reply, closing a review symposium, to four reviews of The Society of Mind in the same issue: by Michael G. Dyer, Matthew Ginsberg, George N. Reeke and Stephen W. Smoliar. Unread: the article is closed access and no copy was reached. Singh (2003) quotes it on why Minsky kept the words "agent" and "agency", and draws on it for a mechanism by which agents with different representations could communicate. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from the Crossref and Elsevier metadata of the article and of the issue it closes, not read: the article is closed access (Unpaywall and OpenAlex find no open copy, Semantic Scholar marks it closed, ScienceDirect refused the request), and no author copy was found. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. The four reviews it answers are listed below from the issue's contents, and are not filed. |
| [LIT-419](../../record/literature.d/LIT-419.md) | Form, Function, and Feel | Lycan (1981), The Journal of Philosophy 78(1):24–50, DOI-10.2307/2025395. Filed as a statement of homuncular functionalism, the view Lycan's Wikipedia entry says he advocates, and as the reply Schwitzgebel points to against Block's nation-of-China intuition. Unread: no lawful full text was reachable, so its arguments are not stated here. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-02 from the bibliographic records (Crossref, the Philosophy Documentation Center), not read: no lawful full text could be reached. JSTOR and PDC are paywalled, Unpaywall and Semantic Scholar list no open copy, and no author copy was found. No NOTE is filed. What it argues is known here only from how Schwitzgebel and Block's acknowledgements place it, which is not a reading. |
| [LIT-420](../../record/literature.d/LIT-420.md) | Surfaces and Essences: Analogy as the Fuel and Fire of Thinking | Hofstadter & Sander (2013), Basic Books. The book defends "the simple but unorthodox premise" that "analogy is the core of all thinking": analogy operates at every moment, from the choice of a word to the insights of physics, and is not a rare tool for hard problems. Its ten contents entries run from the evocation of words and phrases, through abstraction, "inter-category sliding" and naïve analogies, to "Analogies that shook the world" and a closing debate on the core of cognition. Unread: registered from the publisher's description and the catalogue's contents note. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-01 from the publisher's description and the Library of Congress catalogue record with its contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scan is lending-only, and the full texts circulating online are not the publisher's or the authors'. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-421](../../record/literature.d/LIT-421.md) | Foreword | Chalmers (2008), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780195333213.002.0007. The co-author's retrospective on the extended mind: his iPhone is "part of my mind already". The one principled objection is that perception and action bound the mental. His reply is revisionist and contextualist: whether Otto's belief counts as extended flips with explanatory purpose. No Otto-style twin case works for consciousness, so he tentatively keeps an internal conscious core. The thesis needs only a weak, parity-style functionalism and is compatible with dualism. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-369](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-369.md)); worth reading as the shortest statement of where Chalmers stands on the thesis he co-authored, ten years on. Three things in eight pages: the iPhone as a present-day Otto's notebook; the perception-action boundary as the one principled objection, answered by explanatory revisionism and a "Necker cube" view on which whether Otto's belief is extended depends on explanatory purpose; and a tentative internal "conscious core", resting on the failure of any Otto-style twin case for consciousness. It is a foreword: every position is stated briefly and several are offered as "perhaps". |
| [LIT-422](../../record/literature.d/LIT-422.md) | Challenges to the Hypothesis of Extended Cognition | Rupert (2004), DOI-10.5840/jphil2004101826. The embedded hypothesis (HEMC: cognition leans heavily on external props, but is the organism's) competes with the extended one (HEC), and conservatism favours HEMC unless HEC buys explanatory power. It does not. [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md)'s criteria either make phone directories into beliefs or privilege the organism, and memory research (interference, the generation effect, conversation) shows internal and external storage are different kinds. A generic kind covering both does no work. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-343](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-343.md)), in the author's pre-publication version; worth reading as the case that a conservative "embedded" hypothesis explains everything the extended hypothesis does, and as the empirical version of the critique. Its two arguments: a dilemma on [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md)'s past-endorsement criterion (keep it and the organism is privileged after all; drop it and directory assistance gives Otto beliefs about every listed phone number), and memory research (interference, the generation effect, conversation) showing internal and external storage behave as different kinds, so that a kind covering both does no explanatory work. Its strength is that it argues from cognitive science rather than intuition; its limit is that the empirical differences are about fine-grained profiles, which a defender can accept and still claim a coarse kind. |
| [LIT-423](../../record/literature.d/LIT-423.md) | I Am a Strange Loop | Hofstadter (2007), Basic Books. Per its publisher, an "I" is "a strange loop where the brain's symbolic and physical levels feed back into each other and flip causality upside down", and the book asks whether that "I" is real or "merely a convenient fiction", and how selves "can exist in the minds of others". Its 26 contents entries run from souls and the causal potency of patterns, through Gödel's "quintessential strange loop" and downward causality, to "How we live in each other", "Consciousness = thinking" and zombies and dualism. Unread: registered from the description and the catalogue's contents note. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-01 from the publisher's description and the Library of Congress catalogue record with its contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scans are lending-only, and no publisher or author copy is posted. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-424](../../record/literature.d/LIT-424.md) | From Bacteria to Bach and Back: The Evolution of Minds | Dennett (2017), W. W. Norton. Per its publisher, the book shows "step by step, how a comprehending mind could in fact have arisen from a mindless process of natural selection", with the decisive shift the sharing of memes, which produced "thinking tools" powerful enough that minds "create and comprehend". Its contents run from the origin of reasons and "two strange inversions of reasoning", through information, "brains made of brains", words, memes and the origins of language, to consciousness as "an evolved user-illusion" and "the age of post-intelligent design". Unread: registered from the description and the catalogue's contents note. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the catalogue record's contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scan is lending-only, Norton posts no excerpt beyond its description, and Dennett posted no draft chapters that I could find. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. The claim the record wants from it, competence without comprehension, is not stated in the description or the contents, so nothing here places it. |
| [LIT-425](../../record/literature.d/LIT-425.md) | The Meta-Problem of Consciousness | Chalmers (2018), Journal of Consciousness Studies 25(9–10):6–61. The meta-problem is to explain, in terms that do not mention consciousness, why we judge that consciousness poses a hard problem. It is an "easy" problem with an empirical programme. Of thirteen candidate mechanisms, the most promising is an introspective model that attributes primitive relations of awareness to primitive qualities, with a sense of acquaintance. A realist theory should explain how its basis of consciousness produces these judgments, which IIT and biological theories struggle to do. Debunking arguments for illusionism can be blocked, but a residual sense of coincidence remains. Only strong illusionism dissolves the hard problem, and it is false because people feel pain. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-354](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-354.md)); worth reading as the statement of the meta-problem, explaining in topic-neutral terms why we judge that consciousness poses a hard problem, and as a survey of thirteen candidate mechanisms. Cite it for four things: the meta-problem as an "easy" problem with a research programme; the meta-problem challenge to theories of consciousness, which tells against IIT and biological theories; the analysis of debunking and coincidence arguments for illusionism; and the argument that only strong illusionism dissolves the hard problem, with a Moorean rejection of strong illusionism. Its own preferred solution is offered as "closer to a fleshed-out explanandum" than an explanation. |
| [LIT-426](../../record/literature.d/LIT-426.md) | Macrocognition: A Theory of Distributed Minds and Collective Intentionality | Huebner (2014), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199926275.001.0001. Per its publisher, the book argues that genuine cognition requires flexible goal-directed behaviour, which requires specialised representational systems integrated so as to cope fluidly with the environment, and so that collective mentality "should be posited where and only where specialized subroutines are integrated to yield goal-directed behavior that is sensitive to the concerns that are relevant to a group as such". On this view there are many kinds of collective minds, some more like honeybees or cats than people, and groups are unlikely to be full "believers". Unread: filed from the description and contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (Oxford Academic is paywalled, the Internet Archive copy is lending-only, and no author-posted draft was found), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. Of the four works filed for the Minsky–Schwitzgebel bridge it is the one whose criterion for a collective mind is itself a decomposition criterion: mentality wherever specialised subroutines are integrated into goal-directed behaviour sensitive to the group's concerns. A close reading is the most likely of the four to change the bridge. |
| [LIT-427](../../record/literature.d/LIT-427.md) | Pandemonium: A Paradigm for Learning | Selfridge (1959), Mechanisation of Thought Processes Vol. I (HMSO), pp. 513–526 plus discussion to p. 531. Pattern recognition by a "pandemonium": data demons hold the input, computational subdemons compute features, each cognitive demon "shrieks" a weighted sum of them, and a decision demon picks the loudest. The system learns by hill-climbing on the weights and by "natural selection" on the subdemons, culling those of low worth and breeding new ones by mutation and by "conjugation" of pairs. It is applied to telling dots from dashes in hand-keyed Morse. In the discussion McCarthy proposes reading the demons' private computation as unconscious and their public shouting as conscious thought. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-360](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-360.md)); worth reading as the first published architecture of a mind-like process built from many quasi-independent "demons", each doing one narrow job, with no part holding the whole's competence. That is the decomposition template that Minsky's agents inherit. Cite it as an engineering proposal with a preliminary result reported only in discussion, not as a theory of mind. Its one remark on consciousness is McCarthy's, in the discussion, and it is an aside. |
| [LIT-428](../../record/literature.d/LIT-428.md) | Fluid Concepts and Creative Analogies: Computer Models of the Fundamental Mechanisms of Thought | Hofstadter & the Fluid Analogies Research Group (1995), Basic Books. The group's programme gathered in one volume: models of perception-driven analogy-making in microdomains (Seek-Whence, Jumbo, Numbo, Copycat, Tabletop, Letter Spirit, and the proposed Metacat), each chapter with a preface by Hofstadter, plus a critique of AI methodology and of how AI research is evaluated. Its contemporary reviewer in AI Magazine names two ideas under it: cognition as high-level perception, and the parallel terraced scan, by which coherent structure emerges from many probabilistic codelets without top-level control. Unread: registered from the description, the catalogue and that review. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-01 from the publisher's description, the Library of Congress catalogue record and a catalogue contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scan is lending-only, HathiTrust is search-only, and no publisher or author copy of the book is posted. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-429](../../record/literature.d/LIT-429.md) | The Society of Mind | Minsky (1986), Simon & Schuster. The book-length statement of the Society of Mind programme, begun with Seymour Papert in the early 1970s. A mind is a society of many small agents, each mindless by itself, and intelligence comes from their diversity and organisation, not from one principle. The publisher presents it as a puzzle of self-contained one-page pieces that assemble into a theory. Unread: no lawful full text was reachable, so it is registered from the publisher's description and the contents list. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from the publisher's description, the Library of Congress catalogue record and the Google Books contents list, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive copies are lending-only, HathiTrust is search-only, and Minsky's web page did not post the book. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. Its programme is read here at second hand through Singh's exposition ([LIT-444](../../record/literature.d/LIT-444.md)) and at first hand through the draft of its sequel ([LIT-432](../../record/literature.d/LIT-432.md)). |
| [LIT-430](../../record/literature.d/LIT-430.md) | The Intentional Stance | Dennett (1987), MIT Press. Per its publisher, the "first full-scale presentation of a theory of intentionality" Dennett had been developing for almost twenty years: we understand each other by adopting a stance, "a predictive strategy of interpretation that presupposes the rationality" of whatever we predict. Six chapters reprint papers of the 1980s, each followed by a new "Reflections" essay; the first and the last three chapters are new. Chapter 2 is True Believers ([LIT-398](../../record/literature.d/LIT-398.md)). Unread: registered from the description and a catalogue's contents note. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and a library catalogue's contents note, not read: no lawful full text of the book could be reached. The Internet Archive scans are lending-only (access-restricted, printdisabled), the MIT Press page and loc.gov were blocked, and the Tufts Digital Library holds earlier versions of several chapters as papers but not the book. Its chapter 2 is True Believers ([LIT-398](../../record/literature.d/LIT-398.md)), read in full in its 1997 reprint, which is the revised book text. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-431](../../record/literature.d/LIT-431.md) | Time and the Observer: The Where and When of Consciousness in the Brain | Dennett & Kinsbourne (1992), Behavioral and Brain Sciences 15(2):183–201, with open peer commentary. There is no place in the brain where "it all comes together" for consciousness. Discriminations are made once, locally, and revised in parallel "drafts", none canonical. The brain represents temporal order by content, not by the timing of its own vehicles, as a dated letter does. Colour phi, the cutaneous rabbit and Libet's timing results stop looking anomalous. Whether a revision was pre-experiential (Stalinesque) or post-experiential (Orwellian) has no answer at that scale. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-367](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-367.md)), the target article only; worth reading as the shortest complete statement of the Multiple Drafts model and of the case against "Cartesian materialism", worked out on four timing phenomena (colour phi, the cutaneous rabbit, Libet's backward referral and his delayed intention). Its sound core is the distinction between the time a content is represented and the time it represents. Its strong claim, that the Orwellian/Stalinesque distinction "need have no answer" below a few hundred milliseconds, is an argument from indistinguishability, not a demonstration. The open peer commentary (pp. 201–247) was not read. |
| [LIT-432](../../record/literature.d/LIT-432.md) | The Emotion Machine: Commonsense Thinking, Artificial Intelligence, and the Future of the Human Mind | Minsky (2006), Simon & Schuster, read in the author's 2005 draft. Emotions are Ways to Think, not a separate faculty. Each emotional state turns some mental resources on and others off, by Selectors that Critics trigger when they recognise a type of problem. The same machinery serves ordinary thinking. The book adds six levels of mental activity, from instinctive reaction to self-conscious reflection. It treats "consciousness", "emotion" and "pain" as suitcase words for many processes, and the Self as a network of partial self-models. It is a design argued from examples. Against Damasio, it treats the body as one more resource the brain uses, not the stuff of emotion. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 in the author's posted draft of 2005 ([NOTE-348](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-348.md)), not in the published book; worth reading as the late, fullest statement of the Society of Mind programme. It is the one where emotion is put inside thinking: an emotional state is a selection of mental resources switched on and off, made by Selectors that Critics trigger, and so it is a Way to Think. Read it as a design proposal argued by examples, not as evidence. It reports no experiment, Minsky labels several of its central ideas his own conjectures, and it sets the body to the side of emotion, against the bodily account of Damasio ([LIT-384](../../record/literature.d/LIT-384.md), [LIT-388](../../record/literature.d/LIT-388.md)) that this record holds. |
| [LIT-433](../../record/literature.d/LIT-433.md) | Quining Qualia | Dennett (1988), in Marcel & Bisiach (eds.), Consciousness in Contemporary Science, OUP, pp. 42–77. Qualia as tradition has them are ineffable, intrinsic, private and directly apprehensible, and fifteen intuition pumps argue that nothing has all four. Two neurosurgical routes to an apparent colour inversion, one changing qualia and one only memory-linked reactions, feel the same from the inside. So the subject cannot know his own qualia better than an outside tester can. What is real is practical ineffability and idiosyncrasy: we refer to information-rich public properties through our own detectors of them. Hence "there simply are no qualia at all". | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-357](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-357.md)); worth reading as the founding statement of qualia eliminativism and the source of the post-Cartesian side in the record's consciousness readings. It argues by fifteen intuition pumps, not by proof, and says so. Its lasting results are two: the alternative-neurosurgery and Chase-and-Sanborn cases, which show that neither the subject nor the physiologist can separate a change in qualia from a change in reactions to them; and a positive account of practical ineffability and privacy as idiosyncratic, information-rich property detection. Its conclusion, "there simply are no qualia at all", is a tactical choice of words by the author's own endnote 2, not a stronger thesis than that. |
| [LIT-434](../../record/literature.d/LIT-434.md) | Illusionism as the Obvious Default Theory of Consciousness | Dennett (2016), Journal of Consciousness Studies 23(11–12):65–72, in the issue built around Frankish's target article "Illusionism as a theory of consciousness". By its search-engine abstract, it uses a parallel with stage magic to argue that illusionism should be treated as the front runner, a conservative theory to be developed in detail. Unread: registered from bibliographic records only. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from bibliographic records, not read: no readable full text could be reached. Ingenta Connect (the publisher's host) and ResearchGate returned HTTP 403, PhilPapers and Semantic Scholar refused automated access (403 and 429), the Tufts Digital Library holds no copy, and the Wayback Machine was unreachable from here, so the archived copy of Dennett's Tufts page could not be tried. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-435](../../record/literature.d/LIT-435.md) | The emergence of understanding in a computer model of concepts and analogy-making | Mitchell & Hofstadter (1990), DOI-10.1016/0167-2789(90)90086-5. The Physica D statement of Copycat, the Fluid Analogies Research Group's model of analogy-making in a microworld of letter strings, in which understanding is meant to emerge from many small, parallel, nondeterministic agents (codelets) interacting with a network of concepts (the Slipnet) under a computational temperature. Unread: what is said here comes from its co-authored companion paper [LIT-414](../../record/literature.d/LIT-414.md), which cites it for the details of Copycat's "xyz" run. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-01 from Crossref, the Elsevier metadata API and the account of Copycat in Chalmers, French & Hofstadter ([LIT-414](../../record/literature.d/LIT-414.md), read in full), not read: the article is closed access at Elsevier, ScienceDirect returned 403, and no author or repository copy was found. No NOTE is filed. |
| [LIT-436](../../record/literature.d/LIT-436.md) | Intelligence without representation | Brooks (1991), Artificial Intelligence 47:139–159, written in 1987. Argues that AI should be built incrementally from complete autonomous "Creatures" tested in the real world, and that the system should be decomposed by activity into parallel layers, each connecting sensing to action, not by function into perception, a central reasoner and action. His robots have no central representation and no central control: each layer is a fixed network of simple finite-state machines, and a higher layer subsumes a lower one by suppressing or inhibiting its wires. Coherent behaviour emerges, "in the eye of an observer", from competing behaviours. Hypothesis: representation is the wrong unit of abstraction for the bulk of intelligence. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-351](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-351.md)) from the copy Brooks posts; worth reading as the decomposition side built and run: a robot whose purposive behaviour comes from parallel layers of simple finite-state machines with no central representation and no central control, which Brooks likens to Minsky's account of human behaviour. The coherence of the whole, he says, exists "in the eye of an observer". The evidence is three layers on one physical robot, described rather than measured. It makes no claim about minds or experience and disclaims interest in the philosophy. |
| [LIT-437](../../record/literature.d/LIT-437.md) | Gödel, Escher, Bach: an Eternal Golden Braid | Hofstadter (1979), Basic Books. The book that introduced the "strange loop": per its publisher, an account of the "I", self and consciousness built on the self-referential construction Gödel used to make a sentence assert its own unprovability. Chapters alternate with contrapuntal dialogues, chiefly between Achilles and the Tortoise, each built to exhibit the idea it discusses. Unread: no lawful full text was reachable, so it is registered from the publisher's description and the catalogue record, and its year is the only date a source gives. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-01 from the publisher's description and the Library of Congress catalogue record, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scans of the 1979 Basic Books printing are lending-only, HathiTrust is search-only, and no publisher or author copy is posted. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-438](../../record/literature.d/LIT-438.md) | The bounds of cognition | Adams & Aizawa (2001), DOI-10.1080/09515080120033571. Against [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md), Dennett, Donald and Hutchins: cognition has two necessary marks, non-derived content and a kind of causal processing found in brains. Notebooks, screens and tools have only derived content and work by different processes, so actual cognition is brain-bound as a contingent fact. Causal coupling does not extend a process, and brain-plus-tool systems are too motley for a science. They grant the parity principle. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-368](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-368.md)); worth reading as the first sustained reply to [LIT-097](../../record/literature.d/LIT-097.md) and the source of the "mark of the cognitive" line of criticism. It grants parity and argues that cognition has two necessary marks, non-derived content and a distinctive kind of causal process, which notebooks, screens and tools lack as a matter of contingent fact; and that brain-plus-tool systems are too motley to support a science. Read it for that structure, not for proofs: both marks are assumed ("we assume without argument"), and the processing differences it lists are asserted from common knowledge, not measured. The phrase "coupling-constitution fallacy" is not in this paper; it comes from the authors' later work. |
| [LIT-439](../../record/literature.d/LIT-439.md) | The Computational Boundary of a "Self": Developmental Bioelectricity Drives Multicellularity and Scale-Free Cognition | Levin (2019), Frontiers in Psychology 10:2688. Proposes "Scale-Free Cognition": an Individual or Self is the set of parts that pursue a goal together, bounded by the farthest events in space and time it can measure and try to change (its "cognitive light cone"). Selves scale up from homeostatic loops through memory and anticipation, and cells merge into larger Selves by sharing bioelectric state through gap junctions; cancer is the boundary shrinking back to one cell. Because every level that pursues its own goal is a Self, most living systems hold many nested, coexisting Selves, "not one, as implied by Integrated Information Theory". Levin's "Self" is a goal-pursuing collective, an agent and an individual in this record's terms; the essay gives no account of a self as subject. A hypothesis essay: no model and no new data. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-341](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-341.md)); worth reading as the composition-side statement that agency is nested by default: a Self is whatever set of parts pursues a goal together, so cells, organs, organisms and swarms are Selves at once, each bounded by its "cognitive light cone". It is a hypothesis-and-theory essay with no model, no measurement procedure for the light cone and no data of its own. Its one sentence against IIT's single-Self picture is asserted in a bracket, not argued, and its consciousness claims are confined to a closing speculation. |
| [LIT-440](../../record/literature.d/LIT-440.md) | Intentional Systems | Dennett (1971), Journal of Philosophy 68(4):87–106. An Intentional system is one whose behaviour can be explained and predicted by ascribing beliefs and desires and assuming rationality, which is the Intentional stance beside the design and physical stances. Being one is relative to an observer's strategy, and the choice of stance is pragmatic. The stance is "unmetaphysical": it sets aside composition, consciousness and moral standing. As psychology it is vacuous, because it presupposes rationality. Any theory that gives content to a part takes out a "loan of intelligence", repayable only by analysing away the part's reader: "there a little man is concealed". | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-350](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-350.md)); worth reading as the first statement in print of the intentional stance and of the homunculus debt that Dennett later answers with "discharge". Its definition makes being an Intentional system relative to an observer's predictive strategy, and it says outright that the definition "does not say that Intentional systems really have beliefs and desires" (p. 91). True Believers ([LIT-398](../../record/literature.d/LIT-398.md)) later withdraws exactly that relativity. Its lasting tool for this record is the "loan of intelligence" (p. 96): any theory that gives a part content owes an account of that part's reader, and "wherever a theory relies on a formulation bearing the logical marks of Intentionality, there a little man is concealed". The paper does not treat a group as an Intentional system. |
| [LIT-441](../../record/literature.d/LIT-441.md) | The Conscious Mind: In Search of a Fundamental Theory | Chalmers (1996), Oxford University Press. Per its introduction, the book argues that consciousness cannot be reductively explained and that materialism is false, and then moves toward a nonreductive theory with fundamental psychophysical laws. Its contents run from supervenience and explanation through the irreducibility argument, naturalistic dualism and the paradox of phenomenal judgment, to coherence principles, absent, fading and dancing qualia, and information. It ends with a defence of strong AI and a no-collapse reading of quantum mechanics. Unread: registered from the author's posted contents, introduction and jacket copy. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the author's posted table of contents, introduction and jacket copy, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The author posts only those three, not draft chapters. Its two central arguments for this record are readable in their paper versions, [LIT-406](../../record/literature.d/LIT-406.md) (the hard problem and naturalistic dualism) and [LIT-392](../../record/literature.d/LIT-392.md) (chapter 7's organizational invariance). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-442](../../record/literature.d/LIT-442.md) | The Self as a Center of Narrative Gravity | Dennett (1992), in Kessel, Cole & Johnson (eds.), Self and Consciousness: Multiple Perspectives, Erlbaum. A self is an abstractum, "a theorist's fiction", like a centre of gravity: no part of the brain, but a well-behaved posit of interpretation. It is like a fictional character, indeterminate beyond what its "text" fixes, and a selfless process can produce one (a novel-writing robot whose story is true of it). Brains are bundles of partly autonomous systems that make themselves cohere into one autobiography; when the story coheres around two points, there are two selves. Read in a copy-edited transcription. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-364](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-364.md)), in a third party's copy-edited transcription of the text Dennett posted, not in the Erlbaum printing; worth reading as the short, canonical statement that a self is an abstractum, a "theorist's fiction" of the same kind as a centre of gravity, posited by interpretation around which a life's narrative coheres. Read it as an analogy and two thought experiments (the novel-writing robot "Gilbert", the Updike sequel), not as an argument from evidence. Its evolutionary story of self-talk is labelled speculation, and its empirical support (Gazzaniga's split-brain work, multiple personality cases) is cited, not shown. |
| [LIT-443](../../record/literature.d/LIT-443.md) | Consciousness Explained | Dennett (1991), Little, Brown. Per its publisher, a "full-scale exploration of human consciousness" that "refutes the traditional, commonsense theory of consciousness and presents a new model, based on a wealth of information from the fields of neuroscience, psychology, and artificial intelligence". Its fourteen contents entries run from "How are hallucinations possible?" and "A method for phenomenology", through "Multiple drafts versus the Cartesian Theater" and "The architecture of the human mind", to "Qualia disqualified" and "The reality of selves". Unread: registered from the description and the catalogue's contents note. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the catalogue record with its contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scans are lending-only (access-restricted, printdisabled), no publisher or author copy is posted, and the Tufts Digital Library holds Dennett's papers but not the book or draft chapters of it. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-444](../../record/literature.d/LIT-444.md) | Examining the Society of Mind | Singh (2003), Computing and Informatics 22(6):521–543. A sympathetic exposition of Minsky's Society of Mind by his student. It traces the theory's history: Minsky and Papert's 1970s copy-demo robot, the procedure-bearing frames of 1974, the abandoned 1976 book drafts, and the 1977 and 1980 papers. It catalogues the mechanisms and how agents communicate without shared symbols. It finds resemblances in Bayes nets, case-based reasoning, blackboards, Cyc, Soar and multiagent systems, none of which it calls a Society of Mind. Its verdict is that the theory has never been implemented. | Active — read in full 2026-10-01 ([NOTE-339](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-339.md)); worth reading as the shortest reliable map of The Society of Mind ([LIT-429](../../record/literature.d/LIT-429.md)), written by the student who worked with Minsky on its sequel. It gives the theory's history from the 1970s memos, a catalogue of its mechanisms (agents and agencies, K-lines, nemes and nomes, frames and transframes, difference-engines, censors, A- and B-brains, ways agents communicate and grow), and a survey of later AI it resembles. It is an exposition, not a test: it reports no implementation and quotes the theory more than it argues for it. Its history leans on unpublished 1976 drafts that only the author saw. |
| [LIT-445](../../record/literature.d/LIT-445.md) | From the Phenomenology to the Mechanisms of Consciousness: Integrated Information Theory 3.0 | Oizumi, Albantakis & Tononi (2014), PLoS Computational Biology 10(5):e1003588. IIT 3.0 states five axioms of experience (existence, composition, information, integration, exclusion), turns each into a postulate about mechanisms in a state, and identifies an experience with the maximally irreducible conceptual structure of a "complex". Exclusion makes a complex a local maximum of integrated conceptual information Φ over all overlapping sets of elements, so complexes cannot overlap and no subset or superset of a complex is conscious. Worked logic-gate examples show minor complexes beside a major one, an inactive network that is conscious, a minimally conscious photodiode, and a feed-forward "zombie" functionally equivalent to a conscious system. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-365](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-365.md)), equations excepted; worth reading as the formal statement of the exclusion postulate that Schwitzgebel's 2014 manuscript ([LIT-159](../../record/literature.d/LIT-159.md)) attacks: "of all overlapping sets of elements, only one set can be conscious", a complex being a local maximum of Φ against every overlapping set. It is IIT's anti-nesting principle, stated as a postulate and motivated only by the phenomenological exclusion axiom; the paper's Occam's-razor argument is given for exclusion within a mechanism, not between systems. It also says plainly that function does not fix consciousness, which is IIT's answer to whether agency built from parts brings experience with it. |
| [LIT-446](../../record/literature.d/LIT-446.md) | Survival and Identity | Lewis (1976), DOI-10.1525/9780520353060-002. What matters in survival is mental continuity and connectedness (the R-relation among person-stages), and it is also identity, because the two answers relate different things. Persons are maximal R-interrelated aggregates of stages, so the I-relation (being stages of one person) is the R-relation in every possible case. Fission and fusion are two persons sharing stages, counted as one by "tensed identity"; Methuselah is a continuum of overlapping 137-year persons; degrees of personal identity come from supervaluating over cut-offs for R. Against Perry: two persons, not three, took part in a fission. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-373](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-373.md)), from a scan of the 1976 printing posted on Andrew M. Bailey's "Papers by David Lewis" page, OCR'd here; worth reading as the four-dimensionalist reconciliation of Parfit with common sense, and the source of stage-sharing and counting by tensed identity. It agrees with Parfit that what matters is mental continuity and connectedness, the R-relation among person-stages, and denies that this conflicts with identity: persons are maximal R-interrelated aggregates of stages, so the relation of being stages of one person is the R-relation. Fission becomes two persons sharing their early stages, and degrees are handled by supervaluation. A reconciliation by redefinition of counting, which Lewis grants is "somewhat counterintuitive". |
| [LIT-447](../../record/literature.d/LIT-447.md) | Personal Identity | Parfit (1971), DOI-10.2307/2184309. Against two beliefs, that every question about personal identity must have an answer and that important questions turn on it. In Wiggins's division case, each half-brain recipient stands in the relation that would be survival if there were only one, so "how could a double success be a failure?". What matters is psychological continuity and connectedness, which can branch and come in degrees, while identity is one-one and all-or-nothing. Q-memory and q-intention show the relations need not presuppose identity. Speaking of past and future selves by degree of connectedness, the principle of self-interest has no independent force. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-378](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-378.md)), in a JSTOR copy of the published article posted on a University of Athens course page; worth reading as the paper that separated what matters in survival from identity, and the short original of Part III of Reasons and Persons. Its division case makes "I survive as neither" absurd and "I survive as both" incompatible with identity, so it concludes that what matters is psychological continuity and connectedness, which can branch and come in degrees, while identity cannot. It supplies q-memory, the non-branching criterion, and the vocabulary of successive selves. Its first conclusion is argued; its claim that connectedness matters more than continuity is left, in its own words, needing "more arguments than I have space to give". |
| [LIT-448](../../record/literature.d/LIT-448.md) | The Human Animal: Personal Identity Without Psychology | Olson (1997), Oxford University Press. The book-length case for animalism. Per its abstract, "our identity over time involves no psychological facts": psychological accounts "lead to grave metaphysical problems", the arguments for them are inconclusive, and since we are animals we have "the purely biological identity conditions of animals". Its chapters run from psychology and personal identity through persistence, the case against the psychological approach, whether I was ever a fetus and whether people are animals, to the biological approach and alternatives. Unread: registered from the abstract and the contents. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's abstract, the Library of Congress record and the chapter list on Crossref, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. Oxford Academic is subscription-only, the Internet Archive copy is lending-only, and no author copy is posted. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-449](../../record/literature.d/LIT-449.md) | What we talk to when we talk to language models | Chalmers (2026), Inquiry, DOI 10.1080/0020174X.2026.2727582; circulated earlier as PhilArchive preprint CHAWWT-8. Unread. By its abstract it asks who or what our interlocutor is when we talk to an LLM: how to characterise it in terms of mental states; what kind of system it is in the philosophy of computation ("a model, an instance, a thread, or a persona?"); what personal identity comes to for such interlocutors; and what follows for AI welfare and moral status. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-02 from Crossref, OpenAlex (which carries the abstract of the published version) and the author's papers page, not read: tandfonline.com, philpapers.org and philarchive.org returned 403 to every article and PDF URL, the Internet Archive was offline, and no other repository copy was found. No NOTE is filed. |
| [LIT-450](../../record/literature.d/LIT-450.md) | Self models | Metzinger (2007, rev. 2013), Scholarpedia 2(10):4174. There is no substantial self, only a phenomenal self-model (PSM): the conscious part of an organism's self-model, an instrument of global control. A phenomenal self arises "if and only if a system operates under a transparent PSM", one it cannot introspectively recognise as a model; a first-person perspective is a transparent model of the intentionality relation (PMIR). Self-models can be unconscious and artificial, as in Bongard, Zykov and Lipson's limping starfish robot; embodiment comes in three orders, the third conscious. Full-body illusions are offered as the experimental handle. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-372](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-372.md)); worth reading as Metzinger's own short, open statement of the self-model theory of subjectivity (SMT) behind Being No One ([LIT-467](../../record/literature.d/LIT-467.md)): there is no self, only a phenomenal self-model, and a system experiences itself as a self when that model is transparent, that is, when it cannot introspectively recognise it as a model. It is an encyclopedia article, not an argument: the central claim is stated as a definition with a prediction attached, its empirical examples are cited, and the online text is a revision later than the 2007 acceptance, with editing slips. |
| [LIT-451](../../record/literature.d/LIT-451.md) | Of Personal Identity | Hume (1739), Treatise 1.4.6, with the Appendix (1740). There is no impression of a self, "constant and invariable", so no idea of one: introspection finds only "some particular perception or other", and the mind is "a bundle or collection of different perceptions". The identity we ascribe to it is "fictitious", like that of plants, ships and churches: an easy transition of the imagination along resemblance and causation, with memory discovering rather than producing it, so that hard cases are "grammatical" rather than philosophical. The Appendix retracts the account of the connecting principle: two principles he cannot reconcile or renounce leave the matter "too hard for my understanding". | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-386](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-386.md)), in the Merivale–Millican critical text on davidhume.org, with the Appendix's second thoughts (App. 10–21); worth reading as the founding statement of the bundle view and the first published retraction of it. The section finds no impression of a self, calls the mind "a bundle or collection of different perceptions", and explains our ascription of identity to it as a fiction of the imagination run on resemblance and causation, the same fiction that makes a repaired ship or a rebuilt church "the same". The Appendix then confesses that it cannot explain what unites the perceptions and leaves the problem "too hard for my understanding". The section's case is introspective and associationist; its Appendix is the clearest statement of what any bundle view owes. |
| [LIT-452](../../record/literature.d/LIT-452.md) | Against Narrativity | Strawson (2004), Ratio 17(4):428–452. Rejects both the psychological Narrativity thesis (people do experience their lives as a story) and the ethical one (they should, to be full persons), naming Sacks, Bruner, Dennett, Taylor, MacIntyre and Schechtman. Distinguishes Diachronic self-experience (the self I am now was there in the past) from Episodic (it was not, though the human being was), and splits Narrativity into form-finding, story-telling and revision, with only story-telling sufficient. Episodics are normal and can live well; self-narration risks falsifying the self it describes. Argued from the author's own case. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-383](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-383.md)), in the published typeset text; worth reading as the standard objection to narrative accounts of the self, and for its distinctions: Episodic versus Diachronic self-experience, and form-finding, story-telling and revision as separable parts of "Narrativity". Read it as a report of a temperament argued from the author's own case and literary examples. Its empirical claims (a genetic "individual difference variable", the spread across cultures) are asserted, not shown, and its central metaphysical claim, that the remoter past did not happen to "me*", is stated rather than argued. |
| [LIT-453](../../record/literature.d/LIT-453.md) | Philosophical conceptions of the self: implications for cognitive science | Gallagher (2000), Trends in Cognitive Sciences 4(1):14–21. Divides philosophical accounts of the self into the "minimal self", accessible to present self-consciousness without temporal extension, and the "narrative self", extended in time through memory and story. The minimal self splits into a sense of ownership and a sense of agency, which come apart in involuntary movement and, after Frith, in schizophrenic thought insertion when a forward comparator fails. Neonatal imitation suggests an embodied minimal self; Strawson's and Tani's are momentary. Against Dennett's narrative centre, the self is "the sum total of its narratives". Read in the author's preprint. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-377](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-377.md)), in the author's preprint, not the typeset article; worth reading as the paper that set the minimal self against the narrative self for cognitive science, and for the split of the minimal self into a sense of ownership and a sense of agency, which it ties to Frith's comparator model of schizophrenic thought insertion. It is a short review: the empirical support is cited, not shown, and its own proposal, the self as "the sum total of its narratives", is offered in two paragraphs. |
| [LIT-454](../../record/literature.d/LIT-454.md) | Personal Identity | Olson (2002, rev. 2023), Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy. Separates seven questions (characterization, personhood, persistence, evidence, population, personal ontology, what matters) and then takes up persistence. Three answers: psychological continuity (the majority view, repaired from Locke's memory criterion via causal dependence), brute-physical continuity (animalism), and anticriterialism. Fission forces the psychological view into multiple occupancy or a non-branching clause, which makes identity not what matters. The too-many-thinkers objection says that if we are not our animals there are two thinkers in every chair. Temporal-parts ontology may leave it indeterminate what we are. Avoids the word "self". | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-374](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-374.md)); worth reading as the standard map of the analytic personal-identity debate, written by the leading animalist. It separates seven questions that are usually run together, then works the persistence question through psychological-continuity, brute-physical and anticriterialist answers, fission and the too-many-thinkers objection. It is a survey with a side: §§6–7 put the case for animalism more fully than the case against it, and the entry sets the word "self" aside, so the narrative and minimal selves are outside it. |
| [LIT-455](../../record/literature.d/LIT-455.md) | Defending the Bounds of Cognition | Adams & Aizawa (2010), DOI-10.7551/mitpress/9780262014038.003.0004, read from the pre-publication draft. A reply to a manuscript of Clark's "Memento's Revenge". It names the "coupling-constitution fallacy": that X is coupled to a cognitive agent does not make X part of its cognition. It restates the 2001 marks of the cognitive, non-derived content and a distinctive causal process, and defends them: a mental image of Euler circles need not get its content by convention, and a "brain-tool science" would have to cover an open-ended motley of artifacts. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-380](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-380.md)), from a pre-publication draft, not the published chapter; worth reading as the shortest statement of Adams and Aizawa's case after [LIT-438](../../record/literature.d/LIT-438.md), and as the earliest text the record holds that names the "coupling-constitution fallacy". It answers a manuscript of Clark's "Memento's Revenge" (the chapter before it in the same volume), restating the two marks of the cognitive and defending them against Clark's Euler-circle and "brain-tool science" objections. It adds no new argument for either mark: the fallacy is shown by one analogy (nerve and muscle), and the intracranial processing differences are asserted from consumer electronics, not measured. |
| [LIT-456](../../record/literature.d/LIT-456.md) | Reply to Professor Stich | Dennett (1980), Philosophical Books 21(2):73–76, replying to Stich's critical notice of Brainstorms, "Headaches" (pp. 65–73). Accepts Stich's summary. On rationality: the norm is grounded only minimally in evolution, and full rationality is an idealisation, so personhood rests on a concept we "only imperfectly achieve". On pain: there are no pains in a mature psychology's ontology, yet people feel pain; between biting the bullet, reform and common sense (pain is like holes, voices and haircuts), the choice is "a matter of policy". Philosophy supplies the medicine for the puzzles, science the rest. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-371](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-371.md)); worth reading as Dennett's shortest statement of two positions he kept: the intentional stance grounds belief in an idealisation of rationality, so persons are believers by a standard "we already know we only imperfectly achieve"; and pain is not a good theoretical entity, yet people feel pain, a choice among biting the bullet, reform and common sense that is "in some measure a matter of policy". It is also the text behind [LIT-393](../../record/literature.d/LIT-393.md)'s account of the homunculus essay, which it confirms: Stich's quotations stand as [LIT-393](../../record/literature.d/LIT-393.md) gives them, and Dennett's acceptance of Stich's summary is a blanket one. |
| [LIT-457](../../record/literature.d/LIT-457.md) | Fluid Concepts and Creative Analogies: A Review | Burns (1995), DOI-10.1609/aimag.v16i3.1155. A review of Hofstadter and FARG's Fluid Concepts and Creative Analogies ([LIT-428](../../record/literature.d/LIT-428.md)). It finds two ideas under the book: cognition modelled by a parallel terraced scan of probabilistic codelets, and cognition as high-level perception. It asks why the work has had little impact on AI, and answers: microdomains, and critiques of rival programs that rest on analogy and intuition, applied to others but not to Copycat. It recommends the book. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-375](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-375.md)); worth reading as the one contemporary account the record holds of what is in [LIT-428](../../record/literature.d/LIT-428.md), which is unread, and as a short, fair statement of the two standing complaints against the Fluid Analogies programme: every model lives in a microdomain, and rival programs are dismissed by analogy and intuition while the same tests are waved off for Copycat. It is a three-page book review. Its account of the parallel terraced scan is clear and checkable; its verdict is the reviewer's opinion, argued with one worked example (symbol grounding in SME versus Copycat). |
| [LIT-458](../../record/literature.d/LIT-458.md) | Illusionism as a Theory of Consciousness | Frankish (2016), Journal of Consciousness Studies 23(11–12):11–39, the target article of the issue later published as Illusionism as a Theory of Consciousness (2017). Names "illusionism": phenomenal properties are not instantiated, and the task is the "illusion problem" of explaining why experiences seem to have them. Strong illusionism (no phenomenal properties at all, only "quasi-phenomenal" physical ones introspection misrepresents) is separated from weak illusionism (real phenomenal properties that merely seem ineffable, intrinsic, private), which it argues collapses into the strong form or into radical realism. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-384](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-384.md)); worth reading as the founding statement of illusionism under that name, and the target article that Dennett's "Illusionism as the Obvious Default Theory of Consciousness" ([LIT-434](../../record/literature.d/LIT-434.md)) answers. It names the "illusion problem", draws the strong/weak illusionism line with "quasi-phenomenal" properties, and argues from conservatism, anomalousness and the debunking argument that strong illusionism should be the research programme. It states a programme, not a theory: by its own account it "will not propose a detailed illusionist theory" (p. 1 of the eprint), and its positive arguments are abductive and openly so. |
| [LIT-459](../../record/literature.d/LIT-459.md) | Is the United States Phenomenally Conscious? Reply to Kammerer | Schwitzgebel (2016), DOI-10.1007/s11406-016-9725-8. Reply to Kammerer's Sophisticated Anti-Nesting Principle ([LIT-409](../../record/literature.d/LIT-409.md)), which he calls the best defence of anti-nesting he has met. Three concerns: the principle allows group consciousness and may not even exclude the US, since citizens may not consciously represent the concrete entity at issue; condition A's "required" lets a member's change of motive switch group consciousness on and off with no functional change; and the Occam-style justification either leads into causal exclusion, threatening human consciousness, or is ad hoc, and group-level explanation is often the simpler one. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-381](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-381.md)); worth reading as the direct reply to Kammerer's anti-nesting principle ([LIT-409](../../record/literature.d/LIT-409.md)), and the sharpest test so far of the line [THEORY-043](../../record/theory.d/THEORY-043.md) calls the best candidate on offer. Its three concerns: the principle may not exclude actual groups, because it is unclear that citizens consciously represent the entity "the United States" names; its condition A, read through "required", yields flickering group consciousness with no group-level functional change; and its Occam-style justification either generalises into the causal-exclusion problem or is ad hoc. Read from the author's manuscript of 28 April 2016, not the Springer version. |
| [LIT-460](../../record/literature.d/LIT-460.md) | Role play with large language models | Shanahan, McDonell & Reynolds (2023), Nature 623:493–498. A dialogue agent is an LLM role-playing the character its prompt describes, or more exactly a simulator keeping a superposition of simulacra consistent with the conversation. Only simulacra can appear to have beliefs, goals or a self; the simulator has none, "not even simulated versions", and "it is role play all the way down". When an agent says it wants to survive there is "no-one at home". Each role-played character would carry its own theory of selfhood, which the conversation narrows; what an agent would try to preserve (hardware, process, instance) is left open. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-370](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-370.md)), in the Nature text with the arXiv v1 preprint compared; worth reading here as the source of the simulator/simulacra account that every LLM-selfhood paper in this record answers. On it nothing is a self: the simulator has no beliefs, goals or agency "of its own, not even simulated versions", and a simulacrum only role-plays a character that has them ("no-one at home"). Its one section on selfhood asks which self a role-played character would try to preserve, not what the system is, and leaves identity over time for a dialogue agent "far from clear". The denial of a self is asserted from the framing, not argued against a rival. |
| [LIT-461](../../record/literature.d/LIT-461.md) | Consciousness interpreted: an interpretation of Dennett's view of consciousness | Taylor (2024), DOI-10.1080/09515089.2024.2433526. Interprets Dennett's theory of consciousness as realism about a real pattern. Consciousness is a real pattern in the brain's content-bearing states (drafts), and a user-illusion: a pattern so coarse that describing the brain through it is metaphor. On that reading multiple drafts and global workspace theory are compatible, qualia eliminativism is peripheral, the Orwellian and Stalinesque readings of masking are equally good descriptions, and first-person reports are fallible as literal descriptions of the brain but authoritative as unwitting fictions. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-379](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-379.md)); worth reading as the short, charitable reconstruction of Dennett's theory of consciousness that ties it to "Real Patterns" ([LIT-220](../../record/literature.d/LIT-220.md)): consciousness is a real pattern in the brain's content-bearing states (drafts), of the coarse, metaphorical kind Dennett calls a user-illusion. It reconciles six claims, including multiple drafts with global workspace theory and fallibility with "dictatorial" authority. It is an interpretation offered as consistent, not as correct or as Dennett's own, and its key step on metacontrast masking is a plausibility argument. |
| [LIT-462](../../record/literature.d/LIT-462.md) | The Self and the Future | Williams (1970), DOI-10.2307/2183946. One experiment, told twice. A and B enter a machine that exchanges their memories and characters, and choose selfishly which emerging person gets $100,000 and which torture: the outcomes make "changing bodies" the natural description. Told as a series of things that will happen to me (amnesia, changed character, another's "memories", then torture), fear stays rational at every step, and the step that completes the swap changes only what happens to someone else. Undecidability and convention make the clash worse, not better. If forced to choose selfishly, pass the pain to the other body, and call it a "risk". | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-387](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-387.md)), in a JSTOR copy of the published article posted on a University of Vermont course page; worth reading as the strongest case that first-person concern tracks the body, and the paper that made the reduplication-free body swap a problem for the psychological view. It tells one body-exchange experiment twice. Told third-personally, with choices before and reactions after, it says A and B swap bodies. Told first-personally, as a series of things that will happen to me before I am tortured, it says I am the one tortured. It argues that neither indeterminacy nor convention can resolve the clash, and ends with a "rather shaky" preference for the bodily answer, as a "risk". An argument from what fear is rational, not a theory. |
| [LIT-463](../../record/literature.d/LIT-463.md) | Subjectivity and Selfhood: Investigating the First-Person Perspective | Zahavi (2005), MIT Press. Per its publisher, a defence of the self against neuroscientists and philosophers who doubt it, arguing that "the notion of self is crucial for a proper understanding of consciousness" and that experience, self-awareness and selfhood cannot be understood apart. It uses Husserl, Heidegger, Sartre and Merleau-Ponty to give an account of the "experiential givenness of the self" for philosophy of mind, cognitive science, developmental psychology and psychiatry. Its chapters run from self-awareness and phenomenal consciousness through time-consciousness and reflection to self and other, theory of mind, autism and embodiment. Unread: registered from the description and the contents. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list on Crossref, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The MIT Press Direct edition is not open access (Crossref carries no licence, OpenAlex reports it closed), and no author copy of the book is posted. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-464](../../record/literature.d/LIT-464.md) | Of Identity and Diversity | Locke (1694), Essay II.xxvii, added in the second edition. Identity is relative to the idea the name stands for: the same mass is the same particles, the same oak or horse or man is the same continued life, and the same person is the same consciousness. A person is "a thinking intelligent being" that "can consider itself as itself", and personal identity reaches "as far as this consciousness can be extended backwards to any past action or thought". It is independent of substance, material or immaterial: the prince's consciousness in the cobbler's body is the same person but not the same man.
