"Person" is "a forensic term", grounding reward and punishment. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-385](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-385.md)), in the Wikisource transcription of the 1853 Troutman & Hayes reprint of the sixth-edition text, compared word by word with Project Gutenberg's; worth reading as the founding text of the psychological account of personal identity and the source of the record's whole line on what a person is. It separates the same substance, the same man and the same person, puts the person in "consciousness" extended back to past actions, and calls "person" a "forensic term". Its cases, the prince and the cobbler, the day-man and the night-man, the severed little finger, are argued from intuition, and its key relation, "the same consciousness", is never defined. |
| [LIT-465](../../record/literature.d/LIT-465.md) | Simulacra as Conscious Exotica | Shanahan (2024), Inquiry 69(6):2982–3010, from arXiv:2402.12422. After Wittgenstein, consciousness words mean only what is public, so the question for an exotic entity is whether we could "engineer an encounter" with it in a shared world and what a society-wide conversation would then say. Disembodied chat agents are not even candidates; virtually embodied LLM agents could be. Tool use and memory make belief talk increasingly literal, but a chat agent has no "self worth preserving", and a role player has no "person behind the mask": its identity is a shifting distribution of roles. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-382](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-382.md)) in the arXiv v2 preprint (July 2024), not the Inquiry version of record, which returned 403; worth reading as the Wittgensteinian alternative to the evidential debate about AI consciousness. It replaces "is it conscious?" with "could we engineer an encounter with it, and how would our language adapt?", and so counts disembodied chat agents as not even candidates. On the self it is brief and pointed: a chat agent has "nothing that would qualify as a self worth preserving", lacking body, personal history and autobiographical memory, and a role-playing agent has no "person behind the mask"; its identity, "insofar as that notion even made sense", is the shifting distribution of roles. It argues by method and scenario, not evidence, and says so. |
| [LIT-466](../../record/literature.d/LIT-466.md) | Palatable Conceptions of Disembodied Being | Shanahan (2025), arXiv:2503.16348. Asks whether consciousness talk can be stretched to disembodied, 2024-vintage LLM agents. Their time would be discrete and discontinuous, a string of unlike beads. Their "I" has two palatable referents, the model across conversations and a self confined to one conversation that "flickers into being with each user interaction", and we may slip between them. Such selves can be edited, copied and merged, and smeared across branching simulacra. Every candidate site dissolves, which Shanahan reads as Buddhist emptiness, and turns back on human selfhood. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-376](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-376.md)) in arXiv v3 (July 2025), the only text there is; worth reading as the one paper here that argues directly about what an LLM's self is, rather than whether it has one. Its answer: two conceptions are in play, a self sited in the model and "fleeting, flickering" selves sited in single conversations, and we may slip between them; every candidate site "dissolves upon close examination", and Shanahan takes that to the Buddhist conclusion that there is no inherent self, here or in us. It is avowedly "poetic", not literal, and its examples are invented usages, not data. |
| [LIT-467](../../record/literature.d/LIT-467.md) | Being No One: The Self-Model Theory of Subjectivity | Metzinger (2003), MIT Press. Per its publisher, "no such things as selves exist in the world: nobody ever had or was a self. All that exists are phenomenal selves", and the phenomenal self is "the content of a 'transparent self-model'". A representationalist and functional analysis of the conscious first-person perspective, with conceptual "tools", constraints on consciousness and neurophenomenological case studies (agnosia, neglect, blindsight, hallucination), asking whether being someone can be analysed at subpersonal levels. Unread: registered from the description and the contents; the theory is read in [LIT-450](../../record/literature.d/LIT-450.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list on Crossref, not read: no lawful full text of the book could be reached. The MIT Press Direct edition is not open access and no author copy is posted. Its theory is read in Metzinger's own short statement, "Self models" ([LIT-450](../../record/literature.d/LIT-450.md), read in full), and more fully in his "Précis: Being No One" (PSYCHE 11(5), 2005; LIT-568, read in full 2026-10-03), the read substitute for the book, which sets out six of its ten constraints and the phenomenal model of the intentionality relation. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-468](../../record/literature.d/LIT-468.md) | The Constitution of Selves | Schechtman (1996), Cornell University Press. Separates the reidentification question (is this the same person?) from the characterization question (which actions, experiences and traits are this person's own?), and argues against analytic philosophy's attention to the first at the expense of the second. Chapters 1–3 take up reidentification; chapters 4–6 give the narrative self-constitution view, on which, as Strawson quotes it, a person "creates his identity [only] by forming an autobiographical narrative". The source of the term "characterization question" in the SEP map. Unread: registered from the description and the contents. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description, the Library of Congress record and the chapter list on Crossref, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Cornell/De Gruyter eBook is sold, not open, and the Internet Archive copy is for print-disabled users only. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-469](../../record/literature.d/LIT-469.md) | Reasons and Persons | Parfit (1984), Clarendon Press; DOI-10.1093/019824908X.001.0001 is the 1986 reprint online. Four loosely connected parts, per the author's abstract: self-defeating theories of rationality and morality; what one person can rationally want at different times, and attitudes to time; personal identity, where "most of us … have certain beliefs about this subject that are false, but hard to give up"; and obligations to future generations, with the non-identity problem and the Repugnant Conclusion. By the chapter abstracts, Part III defends a reductionist view on which identity is not what matters and draws consequences for self-interest and distributive justice. Unread. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description, its chapter abstracts and the table of contents; the book is in copyright and no lawful full text was reachable (the Internet Archive copies are lending-only and were not opened), so it is unread and no NOTE is filed. By its publisher's account, Part III argues that most of us hold false beliefs about personal identity, that psychological continuity and connectedness, not identity, is what matters, and that this should change our attitudes to our own future, to rationality and to morality. The record already leans on it at second hand: Birch's Relation R, Velleman's reading of Frankfurt and the identity-and-ethics survey all cite it. |
| [LIT-470](../../record/literature.d/LIT-470.md) | Foundations for a Social Ontology | Thomasson (2003), ProtoSociology 18–19:269–290. Per its abstract: the social world poses a metaphysical puzzle (how facts and objects created by human intentionality can be real) and an epistemological one (how such products can include objective facts for the social sciences to discover). Searle's account in The Construction of Social Reality is too narrow to solve either; rules for constructing social reality that parallel rules for make-believe give a more comprehensive ontology, but talk of social reality is not pretence. Unread: filed from the abstract. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's abstract; no lawful full text was reachable (the Philosophy Documentation Center sells it at $20, PhilPapers returned 403, and the Academia.edu copy sits behind a bot check), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. By its abstract it argues that Searle's account of social facts is too narrow and that rules modelled on the rules of make-believe give a fuller social ontology; how, is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-471](../../record/literature.d/LIT-471.md) | Shared Cooperative Activity | Bratman (1992), DOI-10.2307/2185537, The Philosophical Review 101(2): 327–341. Bratman analyses activities such as singing a duet or painting a house together. A shared cooperative activity is marked by mutual responsiveness, commitment to the joint activity, and commitment to mutual support, and it rests on a shared intention built from each participant's intention that "we J", with meshing subplans. The account is reductive: it uses only the intentions of individuals and their interrelations. Unread: filed from metadata and from Bratman's later précis. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's metadata; no lawful full text was reachable (JSTOR and PDC are paywalled, the CUP reprint is paywalled, and the one course copy found returned 401 Unauthorized), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the paper that set out Bratman's reductive account of acting together; its developed form is Shared Agency ([LIT-488](../../record/literature.d/LIT-488.md)), whose author's précis the record has read. |
| [LIT-472](../../record/literature.d/LIT-472.md) | Understanding Institutions: The Science and Philosophy of Living Together | Guala (2016), DOI-10.1515/9781400880911. Per its publisher, a unified theory of institutions combining three views (equilibria of strategic games, regulative rules, constitutive rules), applied to money, private property and marriage. It then addresses reflexivity, realism, Verstehen and fallibilism in the social sciences, criticises Hacking's looping effects and interactive kinds, and asks whether causal and ontological dependence can demarcate social from natural science. Only the sample chapter 1 was read: it restates Hindriks & Guala's case against a rules-only account. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description, table of contents, sample chapter and index, not read: the book is not lawfully readable in full online (De Gruyter and Princeton are paywalled, and the Internet Archive has no copy). Only chapter 1, Rules, the publisher's free sample, was read; it is a lightly expanded version of the first section of Hindriks & Guala ([LIT-481](../../record/literature.d/LIT-481.md)). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-473](../../record/literature.d/LIT-473.md) | Convention: A Philosophical Study | Lewis (1969), DOI-10.1002/9780470693711 (the 2002 Blackwell reissue). The founding analysis of convention as a self-perpetuating solution to a recurring coordination problem with more than one equilibrium, sustained by a system of mutual expectations rather than by agreement, with conventions of language and communication as the main application. Unread: filed from catalogue records and chapter titles; the content summary is as Hindriks & Guala and the SEP entry state it. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from catalogue records and the chapter list, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. The Harvard 1969 edition is lending-only at the Internet Archive (access-restricted, printdisabled), and the Wiley-Blackwell reissue of 2002 is paywalled. What the book argues is known here only through readings that state it (Hindriks & Guala, [LIT-481](../../record/literature.d/LIT-481.md); the SEP entry, [LIT-487](../../record/literature.d/LIT-487.md)), and is labelled as theirs. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-474](../../record/literature.d/LIT-474.md) | The Social Construction of Reality: A Treatise in the Sociology of Knowledge | Berger & Luckmann (1966), Doubleday. A treatise in the sociology of knowledge. By its contents: the reality of everyday life and its language; society as objective reality (institutionalization, sedimentation and tradition, roles, legitimation, symbolic universes); society as subjective reality (primary and secondary socialization, identity). Unread: filed from catalogue records and the table of contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from library catalogue records and the 1966 edition's table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in copyright, the Internet Archive scans of the 1966 Doubleday edition and a 1967 printing are lending-only, and there is no author-posted text), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the sociology-of-knowledge treatise whose title phrase [ADR-017](../../record/decisions.d/ADR-017.md)'s topic blurb uses; what it claims, beyond its contents page, is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-475](../../record/literature.d/LIT-475.md) | The Social Construction of What? | Hacking (1999), Harvard University Press. A book on social-construction claims whose title asks what they are claims about. By its chapter titles: why ask what; too many metaphors; the natural sciences; madness; kind-making; weapons research; rocks; the end of Captain Cook. Unread: filed from bibliographic records and the table of contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the Crossref records for the book and its chapters and the Open Library records of its editions; no lawful full text was reachable (Harvard University Press and JSTOR pages did not load, the e-book is licensed through JSTOR, and there is no author-posted draft), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. Its title asks of social-construction claims what they are claims about; what its answer is, beyond its chapter titles, is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-476](../../record/literature.d/LIT-476.md) | We-Intentions | Tuomela & Miller (1988), DOI-10.1007/BF00353512, Philosophical Studies 53(3): 367–389. The paper introduces the we-intention: roughly, a group member's intention to do her part of a joint action, together with beliefs that the conditions for the joint action obtain and that this is mutually believed. The concept is applied to simple social practical reasoning and offered as basic to social theory. Unread: filed from metadata, with the content as search summaries report it. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's metadata and reference list; no lawful full text was reachable (Springer is paywalled, the University of Helsinki portal holds only the citation, and the 2020 De Gruyter reprint is closed), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the founding paper of Tuomela's we-intention account, the main analysis Searle's 1990 paper ([LIT-490](../../record/literature.d/LIT-490.md)) is reported to argue against. |
| [LIT-477](../../record/literature.d/LIT-477.md) | Walking Together: A Paradigmatic Social Phenomenon | Gilbert (1990), DOI-10.1111/j.1475-4975.1990.tb00202.x, Midwest Studies in Philosophy 15: 1–14. Gilbert takes going for a walk together as the model case of acting together and of a social group. Two analyses built from shared personal goals are rejected, because they cannot explain the walkers' obligations and rights to appropriate behaviour. Her alternative is that the walkers must constitute the "plural subject" of a goal. Unread: filed from metadata and the abstract as search indexes give it. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's metadata; no lawful full text was reachable (Wiley and PDC are paywalled, the author's SSRN posting and a public PDF at humanitiesmontana.org both sit behind bot challenges that returned 403, and the Wayback Machine copy of that PDF could not be fetched through this session's proxy), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the short canonical statement of Gilbert's plural-subject account, and the record's only reading of Gilbert is second-hand, through Tomasello's commentaries ([NOTE-185](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-185.md)). |
| [LIT-478](../../record/literature.d/LIT-478.md) | The Ant Trap: Rebuilding the Foundations of the Social Sciences | Epstein (2015), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199381104.001.0001. Social science works with an "anthropocentric" picture on which the social world is built out of people, as an ant colony is built out of ants. Part One separates the facts that ground a social fact from the facts that anchor the frame principle saying how such facts are grounded, and argues ontological individualism false. Part Two argues that even facts about groups, including group action and intention, are grounded in more than the members. Unread: filed from the introduction, the author's précis and the publisher's description. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the author's own posted introduction and table of contents, not read: the book is not lawfully readable in full online (Oxford Academic is paywalled; the author posts only the introduction). The introduction and the author's open-access précis were read. Its framework, grounding versus anchoring, is stated in the record from the précis and from the author's SEP entry ([LIT-487](../../record/literature.d/LIT-487.md), read). Part Two argues that facts about groups, including their actions and intentions, are grounded in more than their members, which bears directly on [THEORY-043](../../record/theory.d/THEORY-043.md). It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-479](../../record/literature.d/LIT-479.md) | What are groups? | Ritchie (2012 online; Philosophical Studies 166(2), 2013). Six criteria any theory of groups must meet: members can differ across times and worlds, groups exist contingently and temporarily, are located, and coincident groups of one kind can be distinct. Groups as non-singular pluralities, fusions, aggregates (Burge) and sets (Effingham) each fail at least one. Proposed instead: a group is a realization of a structure of functionally defined nodes, existing while every node is occupied; identity at a time needs the same members and the same relations. A sketch; it is silent on agency and minds. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-390](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-390.md)); worth reading as the cleanest statement of what a metaphysics of organised groups has to deliver and why pluralities, fusions, aggregates and sets fail to. Its six criteria (members can change across times and worlds, groups are contingent and located, and coincident groups of one kind can be distinct) are the useful part, and the case against each rival is short and checkable. Its own view, groups as realizations of structures whose nodes are functionally defined, is sketched in four pages; it says nothing about agency or minds, and the author amended its membership definition in her 2018 paper ([LIT-486](../../record/literature.d/LIT-486.md)). |
| [LIT-480](../../record/literature.d/LIT-480.md) | Gender and Race: (What) Are They? (What) Do We Want Them To Be? | Haslanger (2000), Noûs 34(1):31–55. Distinguishes conceptual, descriptive and analytical answers to "what is gender/race?" and takes the analytical one: define the terms for what a feminist antiracist theory needs. S is a woman iff S is regularly observed or imagined to have bodily features presumed to indicate a female's reproductive role, this marks S in the dominant ideology for subordinate positions, and that plays a role in S's subordination; men and racialized groups are defined in parallel, ethnicity and gender in general as the non-hierarchical genus. Stipulative by design; no evidence offered. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-394](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-394.md)); worth reading as the founding statement of ameliorative ("analytical") social ontology and of gender and race as hierarchical social positions. It is short and explicit about its method: it defines woman, man and racialized group for the purposes of a feminist and antiracist theory, says so, and accepts the consequences (non-oppressed females are not women in its sense, and the goal is a world with none). Read it for the three-way distinction between conceptual, descriptive and analytical projects and for the definitional schema (marked by assumed bodily features, positioned by a dominant ideology, subordinated or privileged as a result), not for evidence: its "matters of established fact" about race are asserted without citation, and its definitions are stipulated, not discovered. |
| [LIT-481](../../record/literature.d/LIT-481.md) | Institutions, rules, and equilibria: a unified theory | Hindriks & Guala (2015, online 2014), DOI-10.1017/S1744137414000496. Institutions are rules in equilibrium: correlated equilibria of coordination games with several equilibria, which the players represent to themselves as rules. Searle's constitutive rules ("X counts as Y in C") are obtained from a regulative rule by splitting it with a new term, a base rule (X is Y) and a status rule (if Y then Z). The new term is conservative and eliminable, so the constitutive-rules account reduces to the rules-in-equilibrium account, and Lewis's theory of theoretical terms says the term refers if the rules are true. Shown on one toy institution (first-occupancy property); general reduction asserted. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-395](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-395.md)), with the authors' reply to their six commentators in the same issue; worth reading as the shortest statement of the rules-in-equilibrium theory of institutions and of the claim that Searle's constitutive rules are regulative rules with a name added. The equilibrium half is a clear synthesis of Lewis, Aumann, Aoki and Greif. The reduction of constitutive rules is shown on one toy institution and asserted in general, and it leans on a reading of "collective acceptance" loose enough to make it hard to fail. |
| [LIT-482](../../record/literature.d/LIT-482.md) | Social ontology: Some basic principles | Searle (2006), DOI-10.1177/1463499606061731. Institutional reality is built from three primitives: collective intentionality, the assignment of function, and constitutive rules of the form "X counts as Y in C". The Y status is held only by collective acceptance, and it carries deontic powers, which are desire-independent reasons for action. Institutional facts need language because they exist only as represented. Two revisions of the 1995 book: status indicators (after De Soto), and "free-standing Y terms" (after Barry Smith), such as corporations and money without currency. In those cases there is no X, and "We accept (S has power (S does A))", a formula from later in the book, is ranked as the more general form. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-388](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-388.md)), in its parallel English printing in Papers: Revista de Sociologia 80 (2006), pp. 51–71, not in the Anthropological Theory typesetting the DOI names; worth reading as Searle's own short restatement of The Construction of Social Reality ([LIT-485](../../record/literature.d/LIT-485.md)), and as the place where he concedes that "X counts as Y in C" is not the general form. Status functions imposed by collective acceptance carry deontic powers, and deontic powers are desire-independent reasons for action. Read it for the structure, not for an argument about collective intentionality: that is taken as a primitive and referred to his 1990 paper ([LIT-490](../../record/literature.d/LIT-490.md)), not defended here. |
| [LIT-483](../../record/literature.d/LIT-483.md) | Groups as Agents | Tollefsen (2015), Polity. Per its publisher, a survey of whether groups can literally intend, whether there is a collective mind, and whether groups should be held morally responsible, arguing "the unorthodox view that certain groups should, indeed, be treated as agents and deserve to be held morally accountable". Chapters on group belief, intention, agency, cognition, interpreting groups and the moral responsibility of groups. Unread: filed from the description and contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in print with Polity, licensed as an e-book, and no author-posted draft was found), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is a short introduction to group belief, intention, agency, cognition and responsibility that, per its publisher, argues "certain groups should, indeed, be treated as agents and deserve to be held morally accountable"; which groups, and on what view of agency, is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-484](../../record/literature.d/LIT-484.md) | Qu'est-ce qu'un fait social ? | Durkheim (1894; book 1895), ch. I of Les Règles de la méthode sociologique. A social fact is "toute manière de faire, fixée ou non, susceptible d'exercer sur l'individu une contrainte extérieure", or equivalently one general in a society while existing independently of its individual manifestations. Such facts are neither organic nor individual-psychic, so their substrate is society. They are general because collective, not collective because general: "il est dans chaque partie parce qu'il est dans le tout". Ways of being (morphology) are consolidated ways of doing. A definition argued from examples, not proved. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-391](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-391.md)); worth reading as the founding definition of a social fact, and as a short text later social ontology still cites (Ritchie 2018 does, for the group as more than a sum). Fifteen pages define the object of sociology by two marks, exteriority to individual consciousness and external constraint, with diffusion plus independent existence as an equivalent second test. It is a definition, not an argument: Durkheim calls it "un simple résumé des données immédiates de lobservation" (n. 3), and its two load-bearing steps (that what is neither organic nor individual-psychic must have society as its substrate, and that constraint is present even where nobody feels it) are asserted from examples. Read for the vocabulary and the part-whole thesis ("il est dans chaque partie parce qu'il est dans le tout"), not for a proof. |
| [LIT-485](../../record/literature.d/LIT-485.md) | The Construction of Social Reality | Searle (1995), Free Press, New York (xiii + 241 pp.). The founding book of Searle's social ontology. Per its catalogue description, it argues that some facts are independent of human observers and some require human agreement. Its chapters move from the building blocks of social reality and the creation of institutional facts through language and a general theory of institutional facts, to a defence of external realism and the correspondence theory of truth. Unread: filed from the catalogue record and contents; the record reads the theory in Searle's 2006 restatement. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from catalogue records and a library scan of the table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in print with Free Press / Simon & Schuster, the Internet Archive copies are either lending-only or unauthorised uploads, and Searle posted no draft), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. The record reads the theory through Searle's own 2006 restatement ([LIT-482](../../record/literature.d/LIT-482.md), read in full), which is the entry to start from. |
| [LIT-486](../../record/literature.d/LIT-486.md) | Social Structures and the Ontology of Social Groups | Ritchie (2018 online; PPR 100(2), 2020). Social structures are networks of relations constitutively, not merely causally, dependent on social factors, and can be overt or covert, intentionally or unintentionally created, internal or external to what realizes them. Organized social groups (teams, courts, families) are structured wholes with internal social structures whose nodes people occupy; feature social groups (women, racial groups, classes) are social kinds, nodes in external social structures. Both meet a "Goldilocks Constraint": intuitive and explanatory groups in, arbitrary collections out. Silent on agency. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-389](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-389.md)); worth reading as the one framework in the record that covers both kinds of social group, the organised ones the group-agency literature is about and the race, gender and class groups the social-construction literature is about, and says why they differ. Organized social groups (teams, courts, firms) are structured wholes with internal social structures; feature social groups (women, African Americans, economic classes) are social kinds, nodes in external social structures. It is a framework offered to "motivate", not a proof, and its anti-overgeneration work is done by a restriction to structures that "intuitively exist" or "figure in explanations", imported rather than derived. It says nothing about agency or minds. |
| [LIT-487](../../record/literature.d/LIT-487.md) | Social Ontology | Epstein (2018; rev. 2024), <https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/social-ontology/>. A survey that splits social ontology into two inquiries: what determines (grounds) a social fact, and what constructs or "anchors" the category it falls under, likened to semantics and metasemantics. §3 surveys determination bases (psychologism, atomism, bodies and practices, physicalism, holism, flat ontologies) and reports that ontological individualism is widely assumed and recently contested. §4 surveys anchoring theories (agreement, Lewis's convention, Hart and Searle's collective acceptance, patterns, practices, language). §5 covers groups, group minds and agency, institutions, race and gender, artifacts, money, law and language. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-393](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-393.md)); worth reading as a survey: the best single map of social ontology in the record, organised around its author's distinction between what grounds a social fact (§3) and what anchors, or sets up, the category it falls under (§4). It is also the record's readable source for what The Ant Trap's grounding–anchoring framework is ([LIT-478](../../record/literature.d/LIT-478.md) is not lawfully readable). Read it as a survey written by a partisan: Epstein's own positions organise the entry, and the critics of anchoring are named but given a sentence each. |
| [LIT-488](../../record/literature.d/LIT-488.md) | Shared Agency: A Planning Theory of Acting Together | Bratman (2014), DOI-10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199897933.001.0001. The planning structures that organise an individual's agency over time are also at the core of small-scale shared agency. A shared intention is constructed from each participant's intention that "we J", by way of each other's intentions and meshing subplans, with persistence interdependence and common knowledge. These conditions are offered as sufficient, not necessary. No group subject and no new primitive is needed: the account is reductive "in spirit", against Gilbert and Searle. Unread: filed from the chapter list and the author's précis. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's metadata, the chapter list and the author's open-access précis; no lawful full text of the book was reachable (Oxford Academic is paywalled, and Bratman has not posted the book or draft chapters), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the developed statement of the reductive, planning-theory account of shared agency, and the précis, read in full, says plainly where it parts from Gilbert and Searle. |
| [LIT-489](../../record/literature.d/LIT-489.md) | Institutions | North (1991), DOI-10.1257/jep.5.1.97. Institutions are "the humanly devised constraints that structure political, economic and social interaction": informal constraints (taboos, customs, codes of conduct) and formal rules (constitutions, laws, property rights). With technology they set transaction and production costs, and so the incentive structure of an economy. Tribal exchange, the Suq and the caravan trade did not evolve from within; early modern European trade did, through merchant law, bills of exchange, insurance and a state bound not to confiscate. Institutional change is path dependent because the institutional matrix has increasing returns. A narrative essay; the definition is stipulated. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-392](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-392.md)); worth reading as the economist's canonical one-paragraph definition of institutions ("the humanly devised constraints that structure political, economic and social interaction") and as a compact statement of the transaction-cost and path-dependence programme. It is an essay, not an argument: the game-theoretic motivation is one paragraph, the history is narrative, and the definition is stipulated, not defended. For what an institution is, read it beside Hindriks & Guala ([LIT-481](../../record/literature.d/LIT-481.md)), who take it as the exemplar of the rules account and say what it leaves out. |
| [LIT-490](../../record/literature.d/LIT-490.md) | Collective Intentions and Actions | Searle (1990), DOI-10.7551/mitpress/3839.003.0021, in Cohen, Morgan & Pollack (eds.), Intentions in Communication (MIT Press), pp. 401–416; reprinted in Consciousness and Language (2002). The paper in which Searle introduces collective intentionality, intentions of the form "we intend", as a phenomenon of individual minds that is not a sum of individual "I intend" states plus mutual belief. Unread: filed from metadata, and from how Searle's own later paper uses it. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's metadata; no lawful full text was reachable (the MIT Press chapter is paywalled and returned 403, the CUP reprint is paywalled, the Internet Archive copy of the volume is lending-only, and no author or course copy was found), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the paper that put "collective intentionality" into analytic philosophy and the source Searle's later social ontology ([LIT-482](../../record/literature.d/LIT-482.md)) sends readers to. |
| [LIT-491](../../record/literature.d/LIT-491.md) | Categories We Live By: The Construction of Sex, Gender, Race, and Other Social Categories | Ásta (2018), OUP. Per its publisher, a theory of social categories built on the question "what is it for a feature of an individual to be socially meaningful?"; its main idea is that "social categories are conferred upon people", and its "conferralist" framework is applied to sex, gender, race, disability and other categories, and to their tendency to oppress. Unread: filed from the description and contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and table of contents; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is not open access, Oxford Academic is paywalled, and the author's site posts replies and related essays but no chapters or drafts), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the book-length statement of conferralism, the main rival to Haslanger's account of social categories ([LIT-480](../../record/literature.d/LIT-480.md)); what it argues beyond the description is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-492](../../record/literature.d/LIT-492.md) | On Social Facts | Gilbert (1989), Routledge (reissued by Princeton University Press, 1992; DOI-10.1515/9780691214627). The book asks whether social groups are real over and above individuals' thoughts. It analyses everyday concepts of social action, social groups, collective belief and social convention, in dialogue with Weber, Simmel, Durkheim and David Lewis, and proposes that core social phenomena are "plural subject" phenomena. Unread: filed from the publishers' descriptions and the chapter list. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publishers' records and the chapter list; no lawful full text was reachable (the Princeton reissue is sold as an e-book on De Gruyter, and the Internet Archive's copy of the Routledge edition is lending-only), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is Gilbert's book-length treatment of plural subjects, collective belief and social convention; "Walking Together" ([LIT-477](../../record/literature.d/LIT-477.md)) is the short version. |
| [LIT-493](../../record/literature.d/LIT-493.md) | The Grammar of Society: The Nature and Dynamics of Social Norms | Bicchieri (2006; print December 2005), DOI-10.1017/CBO9780511616037. Per its publisher, an integrated account, drawing on social psychology, experimental economics and evolutionary game theory, of how social norms such as fairness, cooperation and reciprocity emerge, why and when people follow them, when they attend to the relevant norm, why inefficient norms survive, and how psychological dispositions can lead to fairness norms like ours. Unread: filed from the description and contents only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and table of contents, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. Cambridge Core is paywalled. The author's own copy of chapter 1 on her University of Pennsylvania pages and the publisher's excerpt at assets.cambridge.org both failed (HTTP 403; connection reset), and the Internet Archive has no copy. It is the standard equilibrium-and-expectations account of social norms; what its definition of a norm says was not seen. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-494](../../record/literature.d/LIT-494.md) | The Theory of Dyadic Morality: Reinventing Moral Judgment by Redefining Harm | Schein & Gray (2018), DOI-10.1177/1088868317698288. By its abstract: harm is an intuitively perceived continuum, not an objective matter, and acts are condemned in proportion to norm violation, negative affect and perceived harm, where harm is dyadic (an intentional agent damaging a vulnerable patient). Harm leads to condemnation and condemnation to perceived harm (the "dyadic loop"). The theory argues against intuitively harmless wrongs and modular foundations. Unread: the argument and evidence are not known here. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the PubMed abstract and Crossref record, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. The SAGE PDF, listed as free to read, sits behind a bot challenge (HTTP 403); the PubMed record has no PMC copy; a University of North Carolina repository file returned by a search for the paper (cdr.lib.unc.edu/downloads/w9505f334) reset every connection, so what it holds is unknown; Semantic Scholar points only to the SAGE PDF, and CORE, OpenAlex and the Wayback Machine had no copy. It is the main harm-based rival to morality-as-cooperation, and what it argues is known here only from its abstract and from how other readings in this batch describe it. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-495](../../record/literature.d/LIT-495.md) | The Biology of Moral Systems | Alexander (1987), DOI-10.4324/9780203700976 (the 2017 Routledge reissue). By the publisher's account, moral questions arise from conflicts of interest, and moral systems use confluences of interest at lower levels of organization to manage conflicts at higher ones; they are "systems of indirect reciprocity", run on reputation, and morality arose within groups for unity in intergroup competition. Unread: filed from the publisher's description and chapter titles. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. The 1987 Aldine de Gruyter edition is lending-only at the Internet Archive (access-restricted, printdisabled), and the 2017 Routledge reissue is paywalled. A complete scan posted on a course blog was found and not used, since posting a whole in-copyright book is not a lawful copy. What the book argues is known here only from the publisher's description and from Nowak 2006 ([LIT-501](../../record/literature.d/LIT-501.md)), which cites it for the claim that indirect reciprocity leads to morality; both are labelled as theirs. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-496](../../record/literature.d/LIT-496.md) | Mapping morality with a compass: Testing the theory of ‘morality-as-cooperation’ with a new questionnaire | Curry, Jones Chesters & Van Lissa (2019), DOI-10.1016/j.jrp.2018.10.008. The Morality-as-Cooperation Questionnaire, 21 Relevance and 21 Judgement items for seven domains, tested in UK (n = 1,392) and US (n = 469) online samples against the MFQ (UK n = 1,042). Seven factors fit the Relevance items (CFI .98) but not the Judgement items (CFI .87), and the combined seven-domain model is rejected (CFI .73). The MFQ's five factors fit worse. MFQ purity items are rated about as morally relevant as its loyalty and authority items, which the authors concede contradicts the theory. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-412](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-412.md)), from the PsyArXiv manuscript; worth reading as the programme's psychometric test and as the place its authors concede that purity items are rated morally relevant, which "appear[s] to contradict" the theory. Its seven-factor result is weaker than the abstract says. The items were written by the authors, three per domain, from the theory itself, and selected by fit within each domain. Seven factors then fit the Relevance items but not the Judgement items, and the combined seven-domain model is rejected (CFI .73), so the best model has fourteen correlated factors. No non-cooperative item is in the MAC-Q, so it cannot test whether morality is only about cooperation. It would be settled by an item pool written independently of the theory, with harm, purity and other non-cooperative content in it, factored blind, on data that can be shared. |
| [LIT-497](../../record/literature.d/LIT-497.md) | The Genetical Evolution of Social Behaviour. I | Hamilton (1964), J. Theor. Biol. 7:1–16. A one-locus model in which an individual's genotype affects relatives' fitness. Weighting each effect by Wright's coefficient of relationship r gives "inclusive fitness", which tends to be maximized as classical fitness is. An action that costs the actor is favoured only if the benefit to a relative exceeds 1/r times the cost: more than two brothers, four half-brothers or eight first cousins (§5). The increase of mean inclusive fitness is proved only for giving traits; the model is not about morality. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-401](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-401.md)); worth reading as the source of inclusive fitness and of the rule the moral-psychology literature calls Hamilton's rule. It is a population-genetic model with non-overlapping generations, random mating and weak selection, and its rule appears as −k > 1/r (§5), not in the later rb > c form. It proves that mean inclusive fitness increases only for genes whose total effect on others is non-negative (giving traits); for harming traits it shows only that selection points uphill. It says nothing about morality: it is the kin domain of morality-as-cooperation in its biological form, read as biology. |
| [LIT-498](../../record/literature.d/LIT-498.md) | The Evolution of Cooperation | Axelrod & Hamilton (1981), DOI-10.1126/science.7466396. In an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with probability w of meeting again, TIT FOR TAT won two computer tournaments (14 and 62 entries) and an ecological simulation, and no strategy can do better against it when w ≥ max((T−R)/(T−P), (T−R)/(R−S)). ALL D is always stable; reciprocity can still enter through kinship or a cluster, after which a nice stable strategy resists clusters. Applications range from cleaner fish to disease and nondisjunction. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-411](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-411.md)); worth reading as the formal statement of how reciprocity can start, spread and hold: tit-for-tat is robust in Axelrod's tournaments, cannot be bettered by any mutant when the continuation probability w is high enough, and can enter an all-defect population through kinship or clustering. "Evolutionarily stable" is stronger than what is proved: the proof shows no strategy does better than TIT FOR TAT, and always-cooperate does as well, which Boyd & Lorberbaum (1987) later turned into a proof that no pure strategy is evolutionarily stable in the repeated game. It grounds the exchange domain of morality-as-cooperation; it is not about morality. |
| [LIT-499](../../record/literature.d/LIT-499.md) | Moral Foundations Theory: The Pragmatic Validity of Moral Pluralism | Graham et al. (2013), DOI-10.1016/B978-0-12-407236-7.00002-4. Moral Foundations Theory in full: a nativist, cultural-developmental, intuitionist and pluralist account with five foundations (Care, Fairness, Loyalty, Authority, Sanctity), each tied to an adaptive challenge and an existing evolutionary model. A decade of MFQ work finds liberals rely on Care and Fairness, conservatives on all five, across 12 world regions. Five criteria for "foundationhood" are proposed, and Liberty, Efficiency, Ownership and Honesty are named candidates. Harm monism (Gray) and fairness monism (Baumard) are rejected as Procrustean. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-396](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-396.md)); worth reading as the full statement of Moral Foundations Theory and as the text to check morality-as-cooperation's comparison against. It states the theory's four claims (nativism, cultural learning, intuitionism, pluralism), reviews a decade of MFQ findings, mostly on political ideology, and offers five criteria for what counts as a foundation. By its own table the Care foundation rests on kin selection and Fairness on Trivers' reciprocal altruism, and Ownership/theft is already a named candidate, so the claim that MFT omits kin altruism, reciprocal altruism and property does not survive this text. Sanctity, the foundation morality-as-cooperation excludes, is by the same table the one with the least evidence of innate preparedness. |
| [LIT-500](../../record/literature.d/LIT-500.md) | Morality beyond the WEIRD: How the nomological network of morality varies across cultures | Atari et al. (2023), DOI-10.1037/pspp0000470. The Moral Foundations Questionnaire-2: 36 items, six per foundation, for Care, Equality, Proportionality, Loyalty, Authority and Purity, built and tested on 25 populations (N ≈ 8,700) in local languages. Equality and Proportionality correlate weakly (mean r = .21). Purity is the foundation most related to cultural distance from the U.S. (r = .43) and to religiosity. The individualizing–binding split appears in only three of 19 national networks. The MFQ-2 explains more variance in 30 criterion scores than the MFQ-1 (mean adjusted R² .37 against .30). | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-398](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-398.md)), from the APA accepted manuscript; worth reading as the current Moral Foundations measure (the MFQ-2) and the theory's current list of six foundations, with Fairness split into Equality and Proportionality, and as the place MFT answers morality-as-cooperation directly: Curry et al. 2019 "left out Purity as an important and less-WEIRD moral foundation". Its strongest evidence for that, Purity's association with cultural distance from the U.S., rests on the one subscale that fails its own measurement-invariance threshold. Its samples are online panels in 25 populations, not small-scale societies. |
| [LIT-501](../../record/literature.d/LIT-501.md) | Five Rules for the Evolution of Cooperation | Nowak (2006), DOI-10.1126/science.1133755. Five mechanisms let natural selection favour cooperators who pay c to give b: kin selection (r > c/b), direct reciprocity (w > c/b), indirect reciprocity (q > c/b), network reciprocity (b/c > k) and group selection (b/c > 1 + n/m). Each is cast as a 2×2 game, from which conditions for cooperators to be evolutionarily stable, risk-dominant or advantageous follow. Punishment is said not to be a mechanism. Cooperation is proposed as a third principle of evolution. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-409](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-409.md)), main text only; the online supplement with the derivations was not read. Worth reading as the standard short map of mechanisms for the evolution of cooperation (kin selection, direct, indirect and network reciprocity, group selection), each reduced to a benefit-to-cost threshold from a 2×2 game. Every mechanism is modelled with the donation game, so it covers altruism only: mutualism, coordination and conflict resolution, four of morality-as-cooperation's seven domains, are outside it. Its one sentence on morality credits indirect reciprocity with "the evolution of morality", citing Alexander (1987). |
| [LIT-502](../../record/literature.d/LIT-502.md) | Intuitive ethics: how innately prepared intuitions generate culturally variable virtues | Haidt & Joseph (2004), DOI-10.1162/0011526042365555. Proposes that people are innately prepared to feel flashes of approval or disapproval at four patterns: suffering, hierarchy, reciprocity and purity, with ingroup the best candidate for a fifth (fn. 15). Each is a module with a proper domain it evolved for (one's own child in distress; cheating in joint ventures; disease and waste) and a culturally widened actual domain. Cultures build virtues on these intuitions and differ in which they use. The four were chosen by counting which concerns appeared in five surveyed works; the essay reports no data. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-400](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-400.md)); worth reading as the founding statement of Moral Foundations Theory, and as the text morality-as-cooperation quotes when it calls purity non-cooperative. It proposes four innately prepared intuition "modules" (suffering, hierarchy, reciprocity, purity), with ingroup as a likely fifth in a footnote, and argues that cultures build virtues on them. The list comes from tallying five books, not from data, and no study is reported. It already grounds the suffering module in care for one's own children and the reciprocity module in Trivers, so the later charge that the foundations omit kin and reciprocal altruism does not fit this text. |
| [LIT-503](../../record/literature.d/LIT-503.md) | Morality as Cooperation: A Problem-Centred Approach | Curry (2016), DOI-10.1007/978-3-319-19671-8_2. Morality is "a collection of biological and cultural solutions to the problems of cooperation and conflict recurrent in human social life". Nonzero-sum game theory yields seven problems (kin, coordination, exchange, hawkish and dovish contest displays, division, possession) and hence seven moral domains, illustrated but not tested. The chapter predicts that non-cooperative behaviour is morally neutral everywhere, and that MFT's purity domain has no connection to cooperation. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-408](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-408.md)); the founding statement of morality-as-cooperation and the place its predictions are stated most sharply, including the one its own tests have not run: that behaviour unrelated to cooperation will be morally neutral, and that no culture's morality will be about "aesthetics or nutrition" (p. 40). It reports no data. The seven domains are derived from game theory and then illustrated with quotations from philosophers and ethnographers, so the chapter shows the taxonomy is coherent, not that it is the right one. It would be settled by the test [LIT-047](../../record/literature.d/LIT-047.md) lacks: blind coding of all moralized content in one sample, asking whether non-cooperative domains are moralized. |
| [LIT-504](../../record/literature.d/LIT-504.md) | A Natural History of Human Morality | Tomasello (2016), DOI-10.4159/9780674915855. By the publisher's account, a reconstruction from experiments comparing great apes and children of how early humans became "an ultra-cooperative and, eventually, a moral species". The chapter titles give the steps: an interdependence hypothesis, a second-personal morality of collaborating partners, an "objective" morality of the cultural group, and human morality as "cooperation-plus". Unread: filed from the description and chapter list. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. The book is lending-only at the Internet Archive (access-restricted, printdisabled) and paywalled at Harvard University Press and De Gruyter. Its relation to Tomasello's later "The moral psychology of obligation" ([LIT-125](../../record/literature.d/LIT-125.md), read in [NOTE-185](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-185.md)) is inferred from the chapter titles and that reading, and is labelled as inference. An author's précis exists (Philosophical Psychology, 2018, DOI 10.1080/09515089.2018.1486605) and was not read; it would be the natural first step. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-505](../../record/literature.d/LIT-505.md) | The Logic of Animal Conflict | Maynard Smith & Price (1973), DOI-10.1038/246015a0. Why do animals with dangerous weapons fight conventionally? A simulation of five strategies (Mouse, Hawk, Bully, Retaliator, Prober-Retaliator) shows Hawk is not an evolutionarily stable strategy while Retaliator nearly is, so "limited war" can be favoured by individual selection. In a pure war of attrition no fixed persistence is stable; the ESS is an exponential mixture. There is no Hawk–Dove game and no ownership asymmetry here. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-404](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-404.md)); worth reading as the paper that introduced the evolutionarily stable strategy and showed that "limited war" in animal contests can be favoured by individual selection. It is not the source of the Hawk–Dove game or of owner–intruder conventions, though morality-as-cooperation cites it for both: its strategies are Mouse, Hawk, Bully, Retaliator and Prober-Retaliator, its contestants are symmetric, and its "Retaliator is an ESS" ties with Mouse, so it fails the paper's own second ESS condition. What it does ground for MAC's contest domains is restraint backed by retaliation, and, informally, retreat before a visibly superior opponent and honest signals of formidability. |
| [LIT-506](../../record/literature.d/LIT-506.md) | The Genetical Evolution of Social Behaviour. II | Hamilton (1964), J. Theor. Biol. 7:17–52. Part I's model is generalized, "unrigorously", into two principles: an animal values a neighbour's fitness against its own by their relationship, and its social discriminations evolve to make relationship more determinate. They are applied to alarm calls, clones and colonies, the haplodiploid hypothesis for hymenopteran sociality, multiple mating, fights between relatives, sibling competition and seed provisioning. Evidence is anecdotal, and the author reports where it runs against him. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-410](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-410.md)); worth reading as the place inclusive fitness is turned into two general principles (valuing neighbours by relatedness; evolving discrimination that makes relatedness determinate) and tried on warning behaviour, social insects, clones, fights and parental care. The principles are offered as an "unrigorous" generalization of Part I, and the evidence is anecdotal and, by the author's account, partly contrary. Its §6 on fights is the one place in Hamilton's pair that touches contests: relatives should fight less, a kin-weighted version of the restraint Maynard Smith and Price derived without kinship. |
| [LIT-507](../../record/literature.d/LIT-507.md) | Moral psychology is relationship regulation: Moral motives for unity, hierarchy, equality, and proportionality | Rai & Fiske (2011), DOI-10.1037/a0021867. Relationship regulation theory: moral judgments are not about features of acts (harm, unfairness, impurity) but about whether acts fit the relationship model in force. Fiske's four relational models each carry a moral motive: Unity (communal sharing), Hierarchy (authority ranking), Equality (equality matching) and Proportionality (market pricing). The motives are universal; cultures differ in where they apply them. So violence, inequality and "impure" acts can be morally required, and purity and ingroup loyalty are one motive. A review, with no new data. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-405](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-405.md)); worth reading as the relational alternative to both Moral Foundations Theory and morality-as-cooperation, and as the source of the Equality/Proportionality split the MFQ-2 later adopted. It is a theoretical review with no new data. It agrees with morality-as-cooperation that morality exists to sustain cooperative relationships, and differs from it on purity (a Unity motive, the same motive as ingroup loyalty), on harm (violence can be morally required) and on the units (four relational models, not seven game-theoretic problems). |
| [LIT-508](../../record/literature.d/LIT-508.md) | A mutualistic approach to morality: The evolution of fairness by partner choice | Baumard, André & Sperber (2013), DOI-10.1017/S0140525X11002202. When individuals can choose partners and have equal outside options, a split that gives one side less than it could get elsewhere is not stable, so partner choice selects for sharing the gains of cooperation in proportion to contribution. Competition to be chosen then favours a genuine, non-instrumental sense of fairness. Economic-game results (entitlement effects, merit-based division, need-based mutual aid, proportionate punishment) are reinterpreted as fairness, not altruism. Morality is defined as fairness; kin care and group loyalty are not intrinsically moral. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-407](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-407.md)), with all 28 open peer commentaries and the authors' Response; worth reading as the strongest single-mechanism cooperative theory of morality and as the parent of moral disciplining ([LIT-101](../../record/literature.d/LIT-101.md)). It is a cooperation theory that cuts the other way from morality-as-cooperation: partner choice selects one moral sense, fairness, and kin care and group loyalty are not intrinsically moral. Its evidence is a reinterpretation of others' economic-game results, and its central formal claim is cited from the authors' separate model. The commentaries (Graham, Ramlakhan & Brook, Machery & Stich, Sachdeva et al., Atran) press the purity, harm and scope objections, and the Response answers them two ways because the authors disagree among themselves. |
| [LIT-509](../../record/literature.d/LIT-509.md) | Moral Molecules: Morality as a Combinatorial System | Curry, Alfano, Brandt & Pelican (2021), DOI-10.1007/s13164-021-00540-x. The seven morality-as-cooperation domains are treated as elements that combine into "molecules". For each of the 21 pairs a moral concept is proposed and an example found in the literature: fraternity, blood revenge, filial piety, honour, queuing and so on. Counting combinations gives 127 positive molecules and 2,186 of mixed valence. The test is confirmatory: no search protocol, blind judges or failed searches are reported. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-399](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-399.md)); a clear statement of the programme's ambition, that seven cooperative elements generate "all moral ideas, possible and actual", but its test is the weakest in the programme. For each of the 21 pairs the authors proposed a concept and then searched the literature until they found it ("We succeeded in every case"). No search protocol, judges or failed attempts are reported, and the theory "doesn't predict which of these many possibilities will be actualities". Two molecules (mendicance, common ownership) combine an element with the negation of another, which the paper's own notation counts as mixed valence. It would be settled by blind judges classifying an independent list of moral concepts into the scheme, with a count of those that fit nothing. |
| [LIT-510](../../record/literature.d/LIT-510.md) | The Evolution of Reciprocal Altruism | Trivers (1971), DOI-10.1086/406755. Altruism between non-relatives can be selected if acts cost the giver less than they benefit the recipient, the same partners meet repeatedly and symmetrically, and altruists stop helping those who fail to reciprocate. Cleaner-fish hosts fit; bird alarm calls are explained instead by self-interest. In humans, friendship, moralistic aggression, gratitude, sympathy, guilt, suspicion and their sham versions are proposed as adaptations that regulate exchange, and rules of conduct as a multiparty extension. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-402](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-402.md)); worth reading as the source of reciprocal altruism and, for this record above all, of the first adaptive account of moral emotions: moralistic aggression, gratitude, sympathy, guilt and the detection of sham versions of each, with "norms of reciprocal conduct" as a later step. The formal part is slight (a verbal model and Hamilton's communicated Prisoner's Dilemma sketch); the human psychology is hypothesis supported by the social-psychology literature of the 1960s. It grounds the exchange domain of morality-as-cooperation and gives that domain its emotions. |
| [LIT-511](../../record/literature.d/LIT-511.md) | The emotional dog and its rational tail: A social intuitionist approach to moral judgment | Haidt (2001), DOI-10.1037/0033-295X.108.4.814. Moral judgment is usually caused by fast affective intuitions; moral reasoning mostly comes after, to justify the judgment to others, and changes minds chiefly by triggering new intuitions in listeners. Four reasons are offered: two processing systems, motivated reasoning, post-hoc confabulation, and the weak link between moral reasoning and moral action. Intuitions are partly innate and are tuned by culture through custom complexes and peers in late childhood. Harmless taboo violations are condemned anyway, and people are "dumbfounded" when their reasons run out. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-403](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-403.md)), from the author's final manuscript; worth reading as the standard statement of the social intuitionist model, the proximate account of moral judgment that morality-as-cooperation lacks, and as the source of the "harmless wrongs" (consensual incest, sex with a dead chicken) on which the purity and sex objections to cooperative theories rest. Most of its evidence comes from outside the moral domain, which the author says himself: the model is offered as "a plausible alternative", not an established fact. |
| [LIT-512](../../record/literature.d/LIT-512.md) | Moral universals: A machine-reading analysis of 256 societies | Alfano, Cheong & Curry (2024), DOI-10.1016/j.heliyon.2024.e25940. A word-count dictionary for the seven morality-as-cooperation domains (MAC-D, built by expert brainstorming and WordNet expansion) is run over 9,653 eHRAF Ethics paragraphs from 256 societies. Most morals are detected in most societies, with small but significant differences by region and subsistence. Against the earlier hand codes the dictionary agrees only slightly to fairly (κ .08–.25) and overstates prevalence. It cannot tell praise from condemnation, so it says nothing about valence. | Proposed — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-406](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-406.md)); worth reading for the Morality-as-Cooperation Dictionary and for what it shows about the sixty-society result, not as a replication of it. The dictionary counts words and, by the authors' own statement, "cannot be used to estimate the moral valence of cooperation", so it does not test the 961-of-962 finding at all. Its agreement with the earlier hand codes is slight to fair (κ .08–.25), and it inflates fairness from 15% to 58% of societies. It detects significant, though small, regional differences, against the earlier claim of equal frequency. A spot-check of its paragraphs found admired theft in four more societies. It would be settled by valence-aware coding validated against blind hand codes, run with dictionaries for non-cooperative domains on the same corpus. |
| [LIT-513](../../record/literature.d/LIT-513.md) | Validation of Morality as Cooperation Questionnaire in Turkey, and Its Relation to Prosociality, Ideology, and Resource Scarcity | Yilmaz, Harma & Doğruyol (2021), DOI-10.1027/1015-5759/a000627. In Turkish undergraduates (n = 445), the MAC-Q's seven-factor model fits the Relevance items (CFI .949, RMSEA .053) but not the Judgement items or the full form, as in the original. Deference and family track resistance to change, and fairness tracks lower opposition to equality. Resource-scarcity primes in Turkey (n = 576) and a preregistered US study (n = 921) change nothing. Relevance scores correlate modestly with prosocial intentions. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-397](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-397.md)), from the accepted manuscript; worth reading as the first independent test of the MAC-Q, by a team with no morality-as-cooperation author. It reproduces the questionnaire's main result in Turkish undergraduates (n = 445): seven factors fit the Relevance items (CFI .949) and fail on the Judgement items (CFI .828) and the full form (CFI .752). Its two resource-scarcity experiments are null. Its scope is narrow: there is no non-cooperative item, so it tests MAC's structure, not its claim that morality is only cooperation; and the latent correlations (fairness–property .87, family–group .80) and a second-order single factor fitting at CFI .906 limit how distinct the seven are. |
| [LIT-514](../../record/literature.d/LIT-514.md) | Moral Origins: The Evolution of Virtue, Altruism, and Shame | Boehm (2012), Basic Books. By the publisher's account, the moral sense is "a sophisticated defense mechanism": group punishment of bullies, thieves, free riders and psychopaths over six million years made social selection favour altruists and bred conscience, virtue and shame. The chapter list adds a "selection-by-reputation hypothesis" and its test. Unread: filed from the description and chapter list. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list, not read: no lawful full text was reachable. The book is lending-only at the Internet Archive (item moraloriginsevol0000boeh) and sold by Basic Books. What it argues is known here only from the publisher's description, labelled as such. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-515](../../record/literature.d/LIT-515.md) | Statistical physics of self-replication | England (2012; J. Chem. Phys. 2013), arXiv:1209.1179. For a system in a heat bath obeying detailed balance, a transition between coarse-grained ensembles I → II obeys β⟨ΔQ⟩ + ln π(II→I) + ΔS_int ≥ 0 (Eq. 6), a second law stated for macrostates. Applied to self-replication, the reverse probability is small when the copy is durable and made fast, so the heat released per copy is bounded below by about 2n ln(n τ_decay/τ_div) − ΔS_int (Eq. 8). The author's estimates put E. coli within a factor of three of this bound and a self-replicating RNA near it. The "self" in self-replication is an observer's classification of microstates, not a physical given. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-419](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-419.md)), from arXiv v1, the only arXiv version; the published J. Chem. Phys. text was not reached. Worth reading as the founding paper of the "dissipation-driven" programme and as a clean worked use of a macrostate fluctuation relation: the heat a replicator must release is bounded below by how fast it copies and how durable the copy is, not by the copying itself. The general bound (Eq. 6) is a coarse-grained second law and is sound; the numbers for E. coli and RNA rest on an argued, not derived, upper bound on the reverse probability (Eq. 7). It draws no line between living and non-living replicators, and says so: "self" is a classification an observer supplies. |
| [LIT-516](../../record/literature.d/LIT-516.md) | Time, Structure and Fluctuations | Prigogine (1977), Nobel Lecture, reprinted in Science 201:777 (1978). Near equilibrium the second-order excess entropy δ²S is a Lyapounov function, so fluctuations die out, and in the "strictly" linear regime the steady state minimizes entropy production. Far from equilibrium the sign of the excess entropy production ΣδJδX is no longer fixed. Given autocatalytic steps (necessary, not sufficient) the thermodynamic branch can go unstable, and fluctuations are amplified into "dissipative structures": Bénard convection, the Brusselator's limit cycle and "Turing bifurcation". A closing programme derives irreversibility from a non-unitary transformation of the Liouville dynamics. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-424](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-424.md)); worth reading as Prigogine's own short statement of the dissipative-structure programme, and as the place where its scope limits are stated in his words. A dissipative structure is a state beyond an instability of the "thermodynamic branch", "a giant fluctuation stabilized by exchanges of energy with the outside world". The minimum-entropy-production theorem is valid only in the "strictly" linear regime with constant phenomenological coefficients. Far from equilibrium the only general criterion offered is a sufficient one for stability (non-negative excess entropy production). Its §§6–7 programme of a non-unitary "entropy operator" is a sketch whose demonstrated link to macroscopic thermodynamics is, by its own account, "at least in the linear region". |
| [LIT-517](../../record/literature.d/LIT-517.md) | Nonequilibrium Equality for Free Energy Differences | Jarzynski (1997), DOI-10.1103/PhysRevLett.78.2690. For a classical system that starts in canonical equilibrium and is switched from Hamiltonian H_0 to H_1 at any finite rate, the average of e^{−βW} over repetitions equals e^{−βΔF} (Eq. 2), whatever the path and the switching time. The proof is exact for an isolated system and for Nosé–Hoover thermostats, and needs weak coupling for a physical reservoir. Thermodynamic integration and the Zwanzig perturbation formula are its slow and fast limits, and ⟨W⟩ ≥ ΔF follows by Jensen. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-417](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-417.md)); worth reading as the four-page origin of the nonequilibrium work relation ⟨e^{−βW}⟩ = e^{−βΔF}: an equality, independent of path and switching rate, that turns irreversible work measurements into an equilibrium free-energy difference and gives ⟨W⟩ ≥ ΔF as a corollary by Jensen. Read it knowing two things. The physical-reservoir proof rests on weak coupling (Eq. 9 → 10), which the author states; and its usefulness is limited by the author's own estimate to work fluctuations of order k_BT, so nanoscale systems or simulations. |
| [LIT-518](../../record/literature.d/LIT-518.md) | Statistical Physics of Adaptation | Perunov, Marsland & England (2014; Phys. Rev. X 2016), arXiv:1412.1875. Starting from a fluctuation relation between coarse-grained macrostates, the paper writes the log-odds of two outcomes of a driven system as an internal-entropy term, a reversal-probability term and Ψ − Φ, the mean heat minus a fluctuation correction (Eq. 8). Holding the first two fixed, the more likely outcome is the one whose formation absorbed and dissipated work from the drive more reliably. It calls such outcomes "adapted" and argues the tendency needs no self-replication. Two single-particle hopping models illustrate the dissipation and fluctuation terms (Eqs. 10–13); the many-body case is a plausibility argument. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-416](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-416.md)), from arXiv v1, the only arXiv version; the published Physical Review X text was not reached. Worth reading as the origin of "dissipative adaptation": the claim that driven matter tends towards states whose formation absorbed and dissipated work reliably, with no replication needed. Read it as a heuristic argument with two toy models, not a theorem. Its exact content is a rearranged macrostate fluctuation relation; the step to "adaptation" holds two terms fixed that it does not show can be held fixed in a many-body system, and the simulation it reports was not shown. It deliberately erases the line between living and non-living organization. |
| [LIT-519](../../record/literature.d/LIT-519.md) | Nonequilibrium thermodynamics and maximum entropy production in the Earth system | Kleidon (2009), DOI-10.1007/s00114-009-0509-x. A review of MEP in the Earth system. MEP is the proposed principle that a system with enough degrees of freedom adopts the steady state of maximum entropy production. It selects among steady states, where Prigogine's principle only describes relaxation toward one. Box models show the flux–force trade-off that gives an MEP state. The review estimates the Earth's entropy budget, which is dominated by absorption of sunlight. It surveys MEP fits for atmosphere, ocean, mantle, hydrology and biota, and proposes that life drives the planet toward MEP, a thermodynamic reading of Gaia. The author grants that MEP is not established. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-423](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-423.md)); worth reading as the clearest statement of the maximum-entropy-production (MEP) programme in Earth-system science by its main advocate: what MEP claims, how it differs from Prigogine's minimum principle (minimum entropy production describes relaxation toward a steady state; MEP selects among possible steady states), an order-of-magnitude entropy budget of the Earth, a survey of climate, ocean, mantle and biotic MEP applications, and the extension to life and Gaia. Read it as advocacy that is candid about its footing: the author calls MEP "proposed" and says "no one would argue that MEP is well established". Chosen over the Kleidon–Lorenz 2005 book, which has no lawful open text. |
| [LIT-520](../../record/literature.d/LIT-520.md) | Maximum entropy production principle in physics, chemistry and biology | Martyushev & Seleznev (2006), DOI-10.1016/j.physrep.2005.12.001. A 45-page Physics Reports review of the maximum entropy production principle (MEPP) across physics, chemistry and biology. Kleidon (2009) cites it as the review of MEP's physical foundation. Semantic Scholar's machine-generated summary says it analyses the principle's thermodynamic and statistical basis, including its relation to the second law and to Prigogine's principle. Unread: no text or abstract reached. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-02 from Crossref metadata and citing works, not read: no lawful full text or publisher abstract could be reached (Physics Reports, closed; Semantic Scholar and OpenAlex list no open copy, and the abstract is elided by the publisher). Filed because it is the main survey of the maximum-entropy-production principle and of its relation to Prigogine's minimum principle; it stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-521](../../record/literature.d/LIT-521.md) | Information theory explanation of the fluctuation theorem, maximum entropy production and self-organized criticality in non-equilibrium stationary states | Dewar (2003), DOI-10.1088/0305-4470/36/3/303. It applies Jaynes's maximum-entropy inference to the microscopic paths of an open system in a stationary state. Under fixed initial densities and boundary fluxes plus local conservation, the path distribution is p_Γ ∝ exp(A_Γ), with A_Γ carrying τσ_Γ/2k_B (Eq. 14). A fluctuation theorem and ⟨σ⟩ ≥ 0 follow (Eqs. 18–21). Then, in a mean-field approximation and assuming the number of paths increases with irreversible action, maximizing path entropy over the remaining multipliers is maximizing mean entropy production (MaxEP, Eq. 24). A Landau–Ginzburg argument claims self-organized criticality as slow driving goes to zero. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-426](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-426.md)); worth reading as the founding attempt to derive the maximum-entropy-production principle (MaxEP) from Jaynes's maximum-entropy inference over microscopic paths, and as the source most later MEP work, Kleidon's included, cites for MEP's theoretical foundation. Read it as a programme, not a proof. The path distribution p_Γ ∝ exp(A_Γ) and its fluctuation theorem follow from the stated constraints. The MaxEP step needs a mean-field approximation and an assumption that the number of paths grows with their irreversible action, which is stated, not argued. The self-organized-criticality corollary is a mean-field artefact by Grinstein and Linsker's analysis ([LIT-525](../../record/literature.d/LIT-525.md)), which this reading confirms. |
| [LIT-522](../../record/literature.d/LIT-522.md) | Entropy production fluctuation theorem and the nonequilibrium work relation for free energy differences | Crooks (1999), DOI-10.1103/PhysRevE.60.2721. For finite classical systems with stochastic, Markovian, microscopically reversible dynamics, the entropy production ω of a forward process and of its time reverse satisfy P_F(+ω)/P_R(−ω) = e^{ω} for any finite time, provided ω is odd under reversal. That holds for processes that start and end in equilibrium, where ω = β(W − ΔF), and for time-symmetric nonequilibrium steady states. The Jarzynski equality follows in one line, and for long times the theorem gives a heat fluctuation theorem and a Gaussian with variance twice the mean. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-413](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-413.md)); worth reading as the short paper that turned the entropy-production fluctuation theorem into a finite-time, trajectory-level statement for stochastic, microscopically reversible dynamics, P_F(+ω)/P_R(−ω) = e^{ω}, and derived the Jarzynski equality from it in a line. It also defines the trajectory entropy production ω = ln ρ(x_{−τ}) − ln ρ(x_{+τ}) − βQ that the record's stochastic-thermodynamics papers inherit. Read it knowing that its conditions are stated exactly (finite classical system, constant-intensive-parameter baths, Markovian microscopically reversible dynamics, ω odd under time reversal), and that the steady-state version is formally exact but, by the author's own account, of little practical use. |
| [LIT-523](../../record/literature.d/LIT-523.md) | Stochastic thermodynamics, fluctuation theorems and molecular machines | Seifert (2012), DOI-10.1088/0034-4885/75/12/126001. A 105-page review of stochastic thermodynamics. Work, heat and a stochastic system entropy s = −ln p(x(τ),τ) are defined along single trajectories of Markovian systems coupled to baths, and every known integral, detailed and Crooks fluctuation theorem is derived from one master theorem by choosing a conjugate dynamics. Further results: dissipation bounds forward–reverse distinguishability (⟨w⟩ − ΔF ≥ D[p‖p_eq]), Sagawa–Ueda feedback relations, a NESS fluctuation–dissipation theorem in terms of entropy production, and a cycle-based theory of efficiency at maximum power for molecular machines. | Active — read 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-420](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-420.md); §§5, 8.3–8.4, 9.2 and 11 skimmed); worth reading as the systematic reference for stochastic thermodynamics as of 2012: first law, heat and stochastic entropy along single trajectories of Markovian systems coupled to baths, every known fluctuation theorem derived from one master theorem with a conjugate dynamics, dissipation as distinguishability of forward and reverse paths, Sagawa–Ueda feedback, the NESS fluctuation–dissipation theorem and efficiency of molecular machines.
