It is the framework in which the record's thermodynamics-of-prediction and thermodynamics-of-learning results ([LIT-327](../../record/literature.d/LIT-327.md), [LIT-308](../../record/literature.d/LIT-308.md)) are stated. Read it as a review: the derivations are its own and concise, the case studies are reported. |
| [LIT-524](../../record/literature.d/LIT-524.md) | Self-Organization in Nonequilibrium Systems: From Dissipative Structures to Order through Fluctuations | Nicolis & Prigogine (1977), Wiley-Interscience. The Brussels school's monograph on self-organization far from equilibrium, from the thermodynamics and bifurcation theory of dissipative structures to their stochastic theory ("order through fluctuations"). The Nobel lecture cites its ch. VII for the Brusselator and the book for its catalogue of chemical and biological examples. Unread: filed from catalogue records only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from library catalogue records; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in copyright, the Internet Archive scan is a lending-only copy, HathiTrust is search-only, and no publisher description or table of contents was found), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the Brussels school's book-length treatment of dissipative structures and "order through fluctuations", and the source the Nobel lecture ([LIT-516](../../record/literature.d/LIT-516.md)) gives for the Brusselator; what it contains beyond that is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-525](../../record/literature.d/LIT-525.md) | Comments on a derivation and application of the ‘maximum entropy production’ principle | Grinstein & Linsker (2007), DOI-10.1088/1751-8113/40/31/N01. A Comment with two points. (1) Dewar's 2005 derivation of MaxEP needs a quadratic approximation to p(f) near f = F to hold for all f. With the fluctuation theorem, that forces A(F) to be constant, i.e. linear constitutive relations. So the "orthogonality condition" behind both maximum and minimum entropy production fails far from equilibrium, as an exact spin-chain model shows. (2) Dewar's 2003 SOC result is an artefact. Keeping the quartic term gives a finite flux variance at F_ext = 0. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-422](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-422.md)); worth reading as the short, exact critique of Dewar's information-theoretic derivation of maximum entropy production. It shows that the 2005 derivation's key step holds only where the constitutive relations are linear, so MaxEP far from equilibrium is not established by it, and that the 2003 claim that MaxEP yields self-organized criticality is an artefact of a mean-field expansion. Read from the authors' submitted manuscript (Zenodo); the version of record was not seen. Its first point is checked here only on its own worked example, since Dewar 2005 itself could not be read. |
| [LIT-526](../../record/literature.d/LIT-526.md) | Life as we know it | Friston (2013), J. R. Soc. Interface 10(86):20130475. Any ergodic random dynamical system whose states split into internal, sensory, active and external sets with a Markov blanket has internal and active states whose flow can be written as a gradient descent on a variational free energy of the blanket and internal states (Lemma 2.1, Eqs. 2.5–2.8). They therefore "appear" to do Bayesian inference about external states and to bound their own entropy. A simulated "primordial soup" of 128 coupled Lorenz subsystems forms a blanket-enclosed cluster whose internal states predict external motion (5 of 82 above a time-reversed null) and which disperses when blanket or internal states are lesioned. The paper says a candle flame cannot have a Markov blanket, and concedes that the universe holds countless blankets, conjecturing that minimum-entropy blankets mark life. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-421](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-421.md)), from the published text (the author's posted PDF of the journal version and the PMC full text). Worth reading as the founding statement of the Markov-blanket formulation of the free energy principle applied to life, and as the one text in the record that names a candle flame and says it is not lifelike. Read it as a redescription, not a derivation: its "heuristic proof" shows that the flow of an ergodic system with a Markov blanket can be written as a gradient flow on a variational free energy, by choosing the variational density to equal the posterior. The free energy is variational, not thermodynamic; no heat or entropy production is computed. The simulation is one run, analysed with a blanket chosen by spectral clustering. |
| [LIT-527](../../record/literature.d/LIT-527.md) | Pattern formation outside of equilibrium | Cross & Hohenberg (1993), DOI-10.1103/RevModPhys.65.851. A 262-page review classifying non-equilibrium patterns by the linear instability of a uniform state: type I_s (finite wave-number, stationary: convection rolls, Turing patterns), III_o (uniform oscillation: the stirred BZ reaction) and I_o (travelling waves). Near threshold, universal amplitude equations describe them, and phase equations describe them further above it. It finds no general variational principle for non-equilibrium steady states, only perturbative ones near threshold. | Active — skimmed 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-418](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-418.md)), about 30 of 262 pages read closely; worth reading as the physicists' standard account of pattern formation away from equilibrium, and as the sober counterweight to the dissipative-structure programme. It treats Rayleigh–Bénard convection, Turing patterns and the BZ reaction in one framework, classifying patterns by the linear instability of a uniform state and describing them near onset by universal amplitude equations. Its conclusion finds "no evidence for the existence of any global minimization principles controlling the structure, except as a perturbative statement near threshold", and quotes Glansdorff and Prigogine conceding as much. |
| [LIT-528](../../record/literature.d/LIT-528.md) | Energy flow and the organization of life | Morowitz & Smith (2006 working paper; Complexity 2007). Life may be forced into existence by abiotic free-energy sources, as lightning and hurricanes are channels that relieve electric and thermal potentials. Core anabolism, the reductive citric-acid cycle and the cascade built on it, is read as a relaxation channel for geochemical redox and phosphate energy. Jaynes-style entropy maximization with current-dependent entropies (not entropy-production principles) is offered as the framework, and the emergence of life as a "collapse", a phase transition of a metastable abiotic earth. Selection on metabolism comes before replication. No quantitative model is given; the authors say the claim is undemonstrated. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-425](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-425.md)), from the Santa Fe Institute working paper (2006-08-029, dated 7 August 2006), not the Complexity version of record. Filed in place of Morowitz's 1968 book Energy Flow in Biology, which could not be read: every Internet Archive scan of it is lending-only. Worth reading as a short statement of the "life was inevitable" view by Morowitz and Smith: core metabolism, the reductive citric-acid cycle, is a channel geochemistry was forced to open to relieve free-energy stresses, as lightning relieves a charge separation. It is an essay. It says itself that the claim "has not been quantitatively demonstrated", and its evidence is the structure of core metabolism read in that light. It puts life on a continuum with lightning and hurricanes and draws no principled line. |
| [LIT-529](../../record/literature.d/LIT-529.md) | Incomplete Nature: How Mind Emerged from Matter | Deacon (2011), W. W. Norton. An account of how life, mind and purpose ("ententional" phenomena) emerged from physics, built on constraint and absence rather than on added stuff. Its contents run from absence, homunculi and teleonomy through emergence and constraint to a hierarchy of dynamics (homeodynamics, morphodynamics, teleodynamics), a minimal self-producing system (autogenesis), and then work, information, significance, evolution, self, sentience and consciousness. Unread: registered from the publisher's description and the contents; the text was not reached. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and a library contents note, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The Internet Archive scan of the Norton printing is lending-only, and no author-posted draft was found. The companion chapter the record already cites, Deacon & Cashman (2013), "Teleology versus mechanism in biology: beyond self-organization", is identified below but was not reachable either and is not filed. |
| [LIT-530](../../record/literature.d/LIT-530.md) | What Is Life? The Physical Aspect of the Living Cell | Schrödinger (1944), Cambridge University Press. Lectures given at Trinity College Dublin in February 1943, published as a short book for the lay reader. It asks how events inside a living organism can be accounted for by physics and chemistry. It treats the gene as an "aperiodic crystal" carrying a code-script, defended through the Delbrück model, and in chapter 6 says an organism keeps its order by drawing "order from order" and by feeding on negative entropy from its surroundings. Unread: registered from the publisher's description and contents; the text itself was not reached. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description, the Cambridge Core contents and chapter excerpts, and library catalogue records, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. The book is in copyright with Cambridge University Press. Every Internet Archive scan of a Cambridge or Macmillan printing is lending-only, and the freely downloadable PDFs that turn up (course sites, personal uploads, Internet Archive "opensource" uploads) are unauthorized copies of the Canto edition, so I did not open them. Chapter 6, the negative-entropy chapter, is unread beyond the publisher's one-paragraph excerpt. |
| [LIT-531](../../record/literature.d/LIT-531.md) | Belousov-Zhabotinsky reaction | Zhabotinsky (2007), Scholarpedia 2(9):1435. BZ reactions are metal-ion-catalysed oxidations of organic reductants by bromate in acid. The core loop is autocatalytic oxidation of the catalyst via HBrO₂, switched off by bromide, which the oxidized catalyst regenerates from bromomalonic acid. Closed, stirred, it can run "up to several thousand" cycles; only in a continuous-flow reactor do oscillations continue indefinitely, and there bursting and chaos appear. Unstirred thin layers support target and spiral waves (Zaikin & Zhabotinsky 1970). The Oregonator is qualitatively right and quantitatively not. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-414](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-414.md)); worth reading as the short, authoritative account of the Belousov–Zhabotinsky reaction by the chemist who named half of it: discovery, the core mechanism (autocatalytic HBrO₂ inhibited by Br⁻), the Oregonator and its known defects, closed versus open-reactor dynamics, and target and spiral waves. Chosen over Zaikin & Zhabotinsky (1970) because it is openly readable and covers the 1970 waves paper; it is a short encyclopedia review with no new results. |
| [LIT-532](../../record/literature.d/LIT-532.md) | Inadequacy of entropy and entropy derivatives in characterizing the steady state | Landauer (1975), DOI-10.1103/PhysRevA.12.636. Per its abstract: in a bistable system the kinetics of transitions between the two locally stable states can be changed without changing the behaviour near either state, so no quantity that characterises only the vicinity of the states can determine which is more probable. Separately, even a monostable linear circuit's steady state need not be one of minimum entropy production. Unread: filed from the abstract. | Deferred — seeded 2026-10-02 from its abstract and Crossref metadata, not read: no lawful full text could be reached (Physical Review A, closed; Semantic Scholar and OpenAlex list no open copy). Filed because it is the classic objection to entropy-based characterisations of nonequilibrium steady states, Prigogine's minimum principle among them; it stays Deferred until a copy is supplied. |
| [LIT-533](../../record/literature.d/LIT-533.md) | Life as a manifestation of the second law of thermodynamics | Schneider & Kay (1994), Math. Comput. Modelling 19(6–8):25–48. A "restated second law": systems moved from equilibrium "utilize all avenues available to counter the applied gradients". Bénard convection and a vortex draining a bottle are read as structures that destroy gradients faster. Life is "another means of dissipating the solar induced gradient" (living systems as "sophisticated mini-tornados, with a memory"), and ecosystems develop to degrade more incoming exergy. A stressed marsh shows smaller flows and fewer cycles; airborne thermal scans show mature forests cooler (degrading 90% of net solar radiation against a quarry's 62%). The second law is "necessary but not sufficient" for life. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-415](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-415.md)), from the authors' own formatted text (copyright line "James J. Kay and Eric Schneider, 1992"), not the journal's typeset version. Worth reading as the canonical statement of the "gradient-dissipation" view of life: living systems and ecosystems are dissipative structures that develop so as to degrade the solar gradient more completely, and the second law is "a necessary but not sufficient cause for life". Read the thermodynamics as a programme, not a derivation. The "restated second law" is offered as a corollary of Kestin's stability result but not derived from it. The ecological evidence is one stressed-versus-control marsh pair and a few thermal-scanner and satellite comparisons, all correlational. It draws no line between living and non-living dissipative structures beyond "memory" (DNA). |
| [LIT-534](../../record/literature.d/LIT-534.md) | Order Out of Chaos: Man's New Dialogue with Nature | Prigogine & Stengers (1984), Bantam; based on their La nouvelle alliance. A book for general readers that sets the science of complexity and irreversibility against classical dynamics. By its contents: the rise and limits of Newtonian science and its philosophical reception (Diderot, Kant, Hegel, Bergson, Whitehead); thermodynamics from Carnot to Boltzmann's order principle; linear and far-from-equilibrium thermodynamics, bifurcations and order through fluctuations; and the rediscovery of time in physics. Unread: filed from catalogue records only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from library catalogue records and a partial contents note; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in copyright, the Internet Archive scans of the Bantam and Flamingo printings are lending-only, HathiTrust is search-only), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the popular and philosophical statement of the programme whose technical core is the Nobel lecture ([LIT-516](../../record/literature.d/LIT-516.md)); what it argues, beyond its contents, is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-535](../../record/literature.d/LIT-535.md) | The Chemical Basis of Morphogenesis | Turing (1952), DOI-10.1098/rstb.1952.0012. Chemicals ("morphogens") that react and diffuse through tissue can turn a stable homogeneous state unstable, with random disturbances setting which pattern grows. On a ring of cells the linearized onset takes six forms. The most important is stationary waves of finite "chemical wave-length", which (9.4a, b) allow for two morphogens only if they diffuse at different rates. A 20-cell example computed on the Manchester computer gives a three-lobed pattern. Hydra tentacles, whorled leaves, dappling and gastrulation are suggested applications, not tested ones. | Active — read in full 2026-10-02 ([NOTE-427](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-427.md)); worth reading as the founding paper of reaction–diffusion pattern formation and the origin of what the Brussels school later called the "Turing bifurcation". A homogeneous, stable steady state of reacting and diffusing chemicals can be destabilized by diffusion itself and settle into a stationary pattern with an intrinsic "chemical wave-length". The analysis is linear and near onset by design, every biological application is offered as suggestion, and the paper says outright that its patterns are "dynamic equilibria" fed by "a continual supply of free energy", which places them among dissipative structures before the term existed. |
| [LIT-536](../../record/literature.d/LIT-536.md) | Biological Autonomy: A Philosophical and Theoretical Enquiry | Moreno & Mossio (2015), Springer, History, Philosophy and Theory of the Life Sciences 12. The book argues that biology should revolve around autonomy, not only natural selection: organisms are organized systems that self-produce and self-maintain as integrated wholes, set their own goals and norms, and promote the conditions of their existence through interaction with their environment. Chapters treat constraints and organisational closure, emergence and inter-level causation, teleology and function, agency, evolution, organisms and levels of autonomy, and cognition. Unread: registered from the publisher's description and the chapter list. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-02 from the publisher's description and the chapter list, not read. The book is not open access at Springer, but the authors deposited a copy of it in HAL (hal-01151449, "Book_Autonomy_web.pdf", CC BY-NC 4.0, deposited 13 May 2015). HAL now serves files behind an anti-bot challenge that requires a browser running JavaScript, and it states that the challenge is there against automated scrapers, so I did not try to get past it. A human reader can open the copy lawfully, and a reading from it would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-537](../../record/literature.d/LIT-537.md) | Thermodynamic Theory of Structure, Stability and Fluctuations | Glansdorff & Prigogine (1971), Wiley-Interscience. The monograph of the Brussels thermodynamic stability theory. By the Nobel lecture's citations, it treats local equilibrium (ch. II), the second-order excess entropy δ²S as a Lyapounov function near equilibrium (ch. V), and the excess-entropy-production criterion far from equilibrium, including convection (ch. VII). Unread: filed from catalogue records only. | Deferred — filed 2026-10-02 from library catalogue records; no lawful full text was reachable (the book is in copyright, the Internet Archive scan is a lending-only copy, HathiTrust is search-only, and no publisher description or table of contents was found), so it is not read and no NOTE is filed. It is the monograph in which Glansdorff and Prigogine set out the thermodynamic stability theory that the Nobel lecture ([LIT-516](../../record/literature.d/LIT-516.md)) summarizes; what it contains beyond what the lecture and Cross & Hohenberg cite from it is not recorded here. |
| [LIT-538](../../record/literature.d/LIT-538.md) | How effective psychological treatments work: mechanisms of change in cognitive behavioural therapy and beyond | Salkovskis, Sighvatsson & Sigurdsson, Behavioural and Cognitive Psychotherapy 51(6):595–615, in the special issue "Disseminating CBT: 50 years and beyond" (issue dated November 2023, first published online 5 January 2024). A history of behaviour therapy and CBT as empirically grounded treatment development, an argument that common factors are not mechanisms of change, and a proposal: the mechanism of all effective therapies is a shift from a stuck, threat-laden meaning ("Theory A") to a collaboratively built and tested alternative ("Theory B"), which the authors call a type of psychological flexibility. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-428](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-428.md)), from the open-access (CC BY 4.0) publisher PDF; worth reading as a senior Beckian's statement of a single mechanism of change for all effective psychological therapy, and for its clear separation of "common factors" from "mechanisms of change". Its thesis is a proposal argued from the history of CBT and clinical examples, not a tested result: people are "stuck" in an exaggerated negative or threatening meaning (Theory A) kept alive by what it makes them do, and any effective therapy works by helping them build and test a less threatening alternative (Theory B). The paper calls this a particular type of psychological flexibility, without citing the ACT literature that uses that name. |
| [LIT-539](../../record/literature.d/LIT-539.md) | Explanatory Models for Psychiatric Illness | Kendler (2008), American Journal of Psychiatry 165(6):695–702. Psychiatry should explain by mechanisms, not laws. Psychiatric causes act at many levels, within and outside the person, and are non-aggregative and loop-ridden, as an alcohol-dependence sketch shows (gene–environment interaction, tolerance feedback, niche selection, aversive cultural transmission). So "hard reductionism" and "hard emergentism" both fail, but locally decomposable subsystems can still be studied and then reassembled. After Marr, biology will implement psychological functions, not replace them. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-432](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-432.md)), from the NIH author manuscript in PubMed Central; filed as the readable substitute for Kendler's 2005 "Toward a Philosophical Structure for Psychiatry" ([LIT-552](../../record/literature.d/LIT-552.md)), which has no lawful open copy. It opens by citing the 2005 paper for its premise that etiological models must be pluralistic, and develops two of the 2005 abstract's propositions at length: piecemeal integration becomes "decomposition and reassembly" of multilevel mechanisms, and the mind–brain question becomes Marr-style implementation, in which "biology will implement but not replace psychology". It does not restate the 2005 paper's arguments against dualism and epiphenomenalism, so it stands in for the pluralism, not for the whole of the earlier paper. |
| [LIT-540](../../record/literature.d/LIT-540.md) | Impulsive action: emotional impulses and their control | Frijda, Ridderinkhof & Rietveld (2014), Frontiers in Psychology 5:518. Impulsive action is non-deliberate but purposive. Automatic appraisal of an event's pertinence to the agent's concerns elicits a state of action readiness, a motive to establish, modify or end a relation to the object, with "control precedence" (urgency, persistence, attentional capture). Modes of action readiness are what distinguish one emotion from another. Events usually draw several appraisals and so several states of readiness, and their interaction regulates action: much emotion regulation is itself emotional, not reason overriding passion. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-433](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-433.md)); filed in place of Frijda's The Emotions ([LIT-546](../../record/literature.d/LIT-546.md)), which could not be read, as the shortest lawfully readable statement of his action-readiness theory in his own words. Appraisal of an event's pertinence to one's concerns elicits a state of action readiness, a motive to change one's relation to the object, which has "control precedence". Modes of action readiness correspond to emotions (hostility to anger, avoidance to fear). Much emotion regulation is itself one state of action readiness checking another, so "there is no clear distinction between processes of emotion and processes of emotion regulation". It is a theoretical paper with no data, written for impulsive action, so it states the theory's core and not its full range. |
| [LIT-541](../../record/literature.d/LIT-541.md) | Relational Frame Theory: Some Implications for Understanding and Treating Human Psychopathology | Barnes-Holmes, Barnes-Holmes, McHugh & Hayes (2004), International Journal of Psychology and Psychological Therapy 4(2):355–375. RFT stated briefly: humans relate events bidirectionally and arbitrarily, as a generalized operant under contextual control, so words take on the functions of what they are related to. Frames combine into networks, networks are related to networks (metaphor), rules are networks that regulate behaviour, and the self is content, process and context of verbal relations. Psychopathology is read as verbal behaviour, and ACT's acceptance, defusion and values are given RFT interpretations. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-430](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-430.md)), in place of the RFT book, [LIT-549](../../record/literature.d/LIT-549.md); worth reading as a short statement of Relational Frame Theory by two of the book's editors, and as the paper that shows how RFT is meant to become a theory of psychopathology. Humans, unlike the animals of conditioning research, relate events bidirectionally, so a word acquires the functions of what it names. Relating is a generalized operant learned across exemplars, and its frames share mutual entailment, combinatorial entailment and the transformation of function. Rules are relational networks, and the self is three verbal relations to one's own behaviour. ACT's acceptance, defusion and values are then reinterpreted in those terms. Its clinical examples are illustrations, not data, and it says its Part 3 is "no more than a heuristic". |
| [LIT-542](../../record/literature.d/LIT-542.md) | The theory of constructed emotion: an active inference account of interoception and categorization | Barrett (2017), SCAN 12(1):1–23. The brain runs an internal model of the body in the world for allostasis. Its predictions are "concepts" issued from agranular limbic cortex and refined by prediction error. Interoceptive predictions are felt as affect (valence, arousal). An emotion is an instance of categorizing sensation with an emotion concept, built the same way as any perception. Since instances of an emotion are "created by multiple spatiotemporal patterns" (degeneracy), the categories have no neural essences, and the classical view, basic-emotion and causal appraisal theories included, is rejected. Emotions are "constructions of the world, not reactions to it." | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-434](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-434.md)); worth reading as the shortest complete statement of the theory of constructed emotion and of its link to predictive processing. The brain's core task is allostasis, run through an internal model whose predictions are "concepts". An instance of emotion is what happens when the brain categorizes predicted interoceptive and exteroceptive sensation with an emotion concept. On this account emotion categories have no neural "essences", and causal appraisal theories are "highly doubtful". The paper is a theoretical synthesis: its own data are a few figures, and the rest is cited evidence and explicitly labelled hypotheses. |
| [LIT-543](../../record/literature.d/LIT-543.md) | Examining Domains of Psychological Flexibility and Inflexibility as Treatment Mechanisms in Acceptance and Commitment Therapy: A Comprehensive Systematic and Meta-Analytic Review | Macri & Rogge (2024), Clinical Psychology Review 110:102432. A systematic review and meta-analysis of 77 records (67 studies, N = 9,123). ACT reduced global inflexibility, lack of present-moment awareness, fusion, experiential avoidance, self-as-content and inaction, and increased committed action/values, flexibility/acceptance and defusion, against waitlist and active comparators. Those changes were linked to drops in distress. Unread: registered from the abstract. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the PubMed abstract and Crossref, not read. The article is closed access at Elsevier, it is not in PubMed Central, and Unpaywall and OpenAlex list no open copy. A web search for a PsyArXiv or OSF preprint found none, and the University of Rochester's repository (UR Research) holds two dissertations from Rogge's lab on the same question (Dubler 2018, Rolffs 2019) but not this review. ScienceDirect itself was not tried. A human reader with Elsevier access could promote this; it is the record's only quantitative test of the ACT process model. |
| [LIT-544](../../record/literature.d/LIT-544.md) | The Future of Intervention Science: Process-Based Therapy | Hofmann & Hayes (2019; online 2018), Clinical Psychological Science 7(1):37–50. The era of "protocols for syndromes" is over: thirty years of DSM-defined randomised trials never found the diseases the syndromes were meant to reveal. Evidence-based therapy should be redefined as process-based therapy, "evidence-based processes linked to evidence-based procedures", with mediation and moderation as the evidence, functional analysis and idiographic networks as diagnosis, and named therapies and broad schools in decline. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-437](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-437.md)), from the NIH author manuscript in PubMed Central; worth reading as the programme statement of process-based therapy by the leading figures of the two camps of CBT it reconciles. It replaces "protocols for syndromes" with one question: "What core biopsychosocial processes should be targeted with this client given this goal in this situation, and how can they most efficiently and effectively be changed?" It is a position paper with predictions, not a result. Its sharpest commitment is a falsification rule: an intervention whose specified process of change "cannot be shown to be consistently applicable" has an incorrect model, however well it works. |
| [LIT-545](../../record/literature.d/LIT-545.md) | Self-Efficacy: Toward a Unifying Theory of Behavioral Change | Bandura (1977), Psychological Review 84(2):191–215. The founding paper of self-efficacy theory: psychological treatments, whatever their form, are proposed to work by changing a person's expectation that they can perform the needed behaviour. Unread: registered from bibliographic metadata, with no lawful abstract reachable. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Crossref and PubMed, not read. The article is closed access at APA. Unpaywall and OpenAlex call it "green" open access, but their one location is a DOAJ record of an unrelated 2020 Jurnal Psikologi article on thalassemia screening that carries Bandura's DOI by mistake. The copy Stanford hosted with Bandura's papers (web.stanford.edu/dept/psychology/bandura/pajares/Bandura1977PR.pdf) now returns 403 Forbidden. Its archived snapshots are on the Wayback Machine, which this environment's egress policy blocks, and the University of Kentucky mirror of the same collection now redirects away. A PDF of the published article is posted by the Sustainable Sanitation Alliance's library (susana.org), a third party, so it was not read (only its first page was opened, to identify what it was). A human reader with APA access, or one who can open the archived Stanford copy, could promote this. |
| [LIT-546](../../record/literature.d/LIT-546.md) | The Emotions | Frijda (1986), The Emotions, Cambridge University Press, Studies in Emotion and Social Interaction (ISBN 0-521-30155-6 hardback, 0-521-31600-6 paperback), 544 pp. The canonical statement of Frijda's appraisal and action-readiness theory: emotions are changes in action readiness elicited by appraisal of events as relevant to the individual's concerns, with control precedence over other activity. Unread here; what is said of it comes from the works that cite it. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from bibliographic records, not read. No lawful open copy of the book (544 pages) was found: it has no Crossref DOI, and Open Library lists no open or lendable scan. Under the owner's book rule its reading is replaced by Frijda, Ridderinkhof and Rietveld (2014), [LIT-540](../../record/literature.d/LIT-540.md), read in full, which states the book's central constructs, action readiness, concern and control precedence, in Frijda's words. The closer substitute, Frijda (1988) "The laws of emotion" (American Psychologist 43(5):349–358, DOI 10.1037/0003-066X.43.5.349), was not lawfully reachable from here. A human with library access could read it, or the book, and either would let this entry say more. |
| [LIT-547](../../record/literature.d/LIT-547.md) | A Network Theory of Mental Disorders | Borsboom (2017), World Psychiatry 16(1):5–13. Mental disorders are not common causes of their symptoms but states of networks of symptoms that cause one another, through "myriads of biological, psychological and societal mechanisms". Five principles; the dynamic one is hysteresis: a strongly connected network, once activated by an external trigger, can sustain itself after the trigger is gone. Disorder is an alternative stable state of a strongly connected network, mental health the stable state of a weakly connected one. Comorbidity follows from bridge symptoms. Treatments are symptom, external-field or network interventions. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-435](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-435.md)); worth reading as the short, citable statement of the network theory of mental disorders by its main author. Five principles: disorders are complex systems of interacting components, the components are the symptoms of the diagnostic manuals, the network comes from direct causal connections between symptoms, disorders are the tightly connected clusters, and disorders arise through hysteresis, so a strongly connected network can stay "stuck" after its trigger is gone. Mental health, disorder, resilience and vulnerability are redefined as stable states and dispositions of weakly or strongly connected networks. Its evidence is indirect and it says so: the theory is offered as an "organizing framework" and a deliberate simplification, and the strict symptom-component correspondence of principle 2 is its own flagged weak point. |
| [LIT-548](../../record/literature.d/LIT-548.md) | The Need for a New Medical Model: A Challenge for Biomedicine | Engel (1977), Science 196(4286):129–136. The paper that named the biopsychosocial model. Its abstract says the dominant biomedical model of disease "leaves no room within its framework for the social, psychological, and behavioral dimensions of illness", and proposes a biopsychosocial model as "a blueprint for research, a framework for teaching, and a design for action in the real world of health care". Unread: registered from its PubMed abstract and Crossref; no lawful full text was reachable. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from its PubMed record and Crossref, not read. Not open access: Unpaywall lists no open copy of the Science article or of any of its five reprints (Dimensions of Behavior 1978, Holistic Medicine 1989, Family Systems Medicine 1992, The Health Psychology Reader 2002, Psychodynamic Psychiatry 2012), and Europe PMC holds the abstract only. science.org answered both the article page and the PDF with a Cloudflare "Just a moment" challenge, which I did not try to get past. The copies a web search turns up are on a private practitioner's site and on document-sharing sites, neither an author nor an institutional deposit, so I did not open them. A reader with Science or AAAS access, or a library copy of any reprint, can read it lawfully; a reading would move this to Active. No shorter open work by Engel stating the same model was found (his 1980 American Journal of Psychiatry follow-up, "The clinical application of the biopsychosocial model", DOI 10.1176/ajp.137.5.535, is closed in Unpaywall, and psychiatryonline.org answered it with HTTP 403 and a challenge page). |
| [LIT-549](../../record/literature.d/LIT-549.md) | Relational Frame Theory: A Post-Skinnerian Account of Human Language and Cognition | Hayes, Barnes-Holmes & Roche (eds.), Kluwer Academic/Plenum, 2001. The founding book of Relational Frame Theory: human language and cognition are learned, generalized operant behaviour, the arbitrarily applicable relating of events (mutually, in combination, and with transformation of their functions) under contextual control. Eight chapters state the theory, from derived relations to rules and the self; five apply it to development, education, social processes, psychopathology and religion. Unread: registered from Crossref's chapter records, with [LIT-541](../../record/literature.d/LIT-541.md) read in its place. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Crossref's book and chapter records, not read; [LIT-541](../../record/literature.d/LIT-541.md), a 21-page statement of the same theory by two of its editors, was read in its place. No lawful full text of the book was found. Springer's book page returned a "Client Challenge" bot page, Unpaywall and OpenAlex list no open copy, and Maynooth University's repository (where Barnes-Holmes and Roche deposited many RFT papers) holds no chapter of it. A copy on an Ethiopian national-library server was not used, because it is not an author, institutional or publisher deposit. The editors' own fourteen-page chapter 8, "Relational Frame Theory: A Précis" (pp. 141–154, DOI 10.1007/0-306-47638-x_8), is the part a human reader should take first; a reading of the whole book would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-550](../../record/literature.d/LIT-550.md) | Emotion and Adaptation | Lazarus (1991), Emotion and Adaptation, Oxford University Press, New York (ISBN 0-19-506994-3). The book-length statement of Lazarus's cognitive-motivational-relational appraisal theory; its chapter titles frame emotion through the person–environment relationship, motivation, coping and goal congruence. Eleven chapters run from definition and measurement through motivation and coping, cognition, causality, negative, positive and problematic emotions, development, social influence and health, to research, assessment, treatment and prevention. Unread. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Crossref's chapter records, not read, and no substitute was read either. The book (Oxford University Press, about 560 pages) is closed at the publisher. The shorter work by the same author that states the same theory, Lazarus (1991), "Progress on a cognitive-motivational-relational theory of emotion" (American Psychologist 46(8):819–834, DOI 10.1037/0003-066X.46.8.819), has no lawful open copy. APA does not make it free, and Unpaywall and Semantic Scholar list none. The only full text the web search found is in a bulk upload of Lazarus's journal articles to the Internet Archive by a private user, which is not a lawful source, so I did not open it. Other Lazarus emotion papers (American Psychologist 1991 46(4); Smith & Lazarus 1993; Psychological Inquiry 1995; Journal of Personality 2006) are also closed. His 1993 Annual Review of Psychology chapter is listed as free at the publisher, but annualreviews.org answered with a bot challenge. A human with library access could read the 1991 American Psychologist paper and file it as the read substitute. |
| [LIT-551](../../record/literature.d/LIT-551.md) | Experiential Avoidance and Behavioral Disorders: A Functional Dimensional Approach to Diagnosis and Treatment | Hayes, Wilson, Gifford, Follette & Strosahl (1996), Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology 64(6):1152–1168. Syndromal diagnosis has not found functional pathological processes, and functional analysis has not produced a replicable classification. The paper proposes functional diagnostic dimensions instead, and offers experiential avoidance as one: many disorders are unhealthy efforts to escape and avoid emotions, thoughts, memories and other private experiences. Unread: registered from the abstract. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the PubMed abstract and Crossref, not read. The article is closed access at APA, and Unpaywall and OpenAlex list no open copy. Copies found online were all third-party postings: a clinician's practice site (actmindfully.com.au), an Argentine ACT group's site (grupoact.com.ar), and a CiteSeerX cache whose source could not be traced because CiteSeerX now redirects to the Wayback Machine, which this environment's egress policy blocks. None is an author, institutional or publisher deposit, so none was used. The ACBS publication page (contextualscience.org) gives the citation and abstract but no file. A human reader with APA PsycArticles access, or the chapter of the same title in The Act in Context (Routledge, 2015, DOI 10.4324/9781315745138-20; Crossref lists it without authors, so that it is a reprint is an inference), could promote this. |
| [LIT-552](../../record/literature.d/LIT-552.md) | Toward a Philosophical Structure for Psychiatry | Kendler (2005), American Journal of Psychiatry 162(3):433–440. Eight propositions for a philosophical frame for psychiatry, per the abstract: psychiatry is grounded in first-person experience; Cartesian substance dualism and epiphenomenalism are false; brain→mind and mind→brain causation are both real; psychiatric disorders are etiologically complex, with no more "spirochete-like" discoveries to come; explanatory pluralism beats monism, especially biological reductionism; and the goal is "patchy reductionism" and "piecemeal integration". Unread: registered from its PubMed abstract. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from its PubMed abstract and Crossref, not read. Not open access: Unpaywall lists no open copy, Europe PMC and PubMed Central hold no full text, and psychiatryonline.org answered both the full-text page and the PDF with HTTP 403 and a Cloudflare "Just a moment" challenge, which I did not try to get past. A web search finds it only on a document-sharing site, which is not an author or institutional deposit. A reader with American Journal of Psychiatry access can read it lawfully; a reading would move this to Active. Its readable substitute is Kendler's "Explanatory models for psychiatric illness" (Am J Psychiatry 165(6):695–702, 2008), filed and read in full as [LIT-539](../../record/literature.d/LIT-539.md). That paper cites this one for its pluralist premise and develops its piecemeal integration and its mind–brain propositions, but not its arguments against dualism and epiphenomenalism. A later open paper, "The dappled nature of causes of psychiatric illness" (Mol Psychiatry 17(4):377–388, 2012, PMC3312951), was not read or filed. |
| [LIT-553](../../record/literature.d/LIT-553.md) | Psychological Flexibility as a Fundamental Aspect of Health | Kashdan & Rottenberg (2010), Clinical Psychology Review 30(7):865–878. Psychological health is not a ratio of positive to negative experience or a set of fixed strengths but flexibility: adapting regulation to the situation, balancing time perspectives and life domains, and acting on values while open to experience. Inflexibility marks depression (rumination, emotion context insensitivity) and anxiety (experiential avoidance), and cardiac vagal control is proposed as an integrating marker. Executive functioning, default states and personality underlie it, and dynamic measurement is needed. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-436](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-436.md)), in the NIH author manuscript on PubMed Central; worth reading as the review that took "psychological flexibility" out of the ACT literature and made it a general construct of health. Flexibility is adapting to situational demands, reconfiguring mental resources, shifting perspective and balancing competing desires and life domains. Its absence (rumination, emotion context insensitivity, experiential avoidance, low cardiac vagal control) runs through depression and anxiety. Executive functioning, default mental states and personality are its "building blocks". It is a narrative synthesis: no meta-analysis of its own, the causal direction between inflexibility and disorder is left open by its own account, and it asks for dynamic measures in place of the global self-reports the ACT line relies on. |
| [LIT-554](../../record/literature.d/LIT-554.md) | The Emerging Field of Emotion Regulation: An Integrative Review | Gross (1998), Review of General Psychology 2(3):271–299. Emotions are response tendencies (behavioural, experiential, physiological) called forth by evaluated situations and open to modulation. Emotion regulation is how people influence which emotions they have, when, and how they are experienced and expressed, and it is distinct from coping, mood regulation and defense. A process model places regulation at five points in emotion generation: situation selection, situation modification, attentional deployment, cognitive change (reappraisal), and response modulation (suppression). The first four are antecedent-focused, the last response-focused. Dysregulation is said to figure in over half of the DSM-IV Axis I disorders and all of Axis II. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-429](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-429.md)); worth reading as the founding statement of the process model of emotion regulation, the five-family scheme (situation selection, situation modification, attentional deployment, cognitive change, response modulation) that later regulation research and many therapies are organised around. It defines emotion regulation as "the processes by which individuals influence which emotions they have, when they have them, and how they experience and express these emotions" (p. 275) and separates it from coping, mood regulation and defense. It is a conceptual review: no data of its own, and it says the scheme is "a set of working distinctions" (p. 282). |
| [LIT-555](../../record/literature.d/LIT-555.md) | Acceptance and Commitment Therapy: Model, Processes and Outcomes | Hayes, Luoma, Bond, Masuda & Lillis (2006), Behaviour Research and Therapy 44(1):1–25. The ACT model: psychopathology is psychological inflexibility, produced when language (cognitive fusion) and experiential avoidance dominate direct contingencies, and ACT restores flexibility through six processes. A review of correlational, component, mediational and outcome studies finds the model "broadly supportive" but the literature immature, and the evidence suggests ACT works through different processes from traditional CBT. Read in an early author manuscript whose numbers differ from the published paper's. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-431](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-431.md)), but in an early manuscript, not the published text; worth reading as the canonical statement of the ACT model of psychopathology, "psychological inflexibility", and of the six processes ACT targets. Cognitive fusion and experiential avoidance (from [LIT-551](../../record/literature.d/LIT-551.md)) narrow behaviour, and acceptance, defusion, present-moment contact, self as context, values and committed action restore flexibility. The copy read is an author manuscript in Georgia State University's repository that still carries "xx" placeholders, and its numbers differ from the published paper's as Kashdan and Rottenberg report them (21 studies against 32; an AAQ correlation printed ".030" against ".42"). Use its model and argument, not its numbers. Its own verdict on the evidence is "broadly supportive" but "not mature". |
| [LIT-556](../../record/literature.d/LIT-556.md) | Reinforcement, Reward, and Intrinsic Motivation: A Meta-Analysis | Cameron & Pierce (1994), Review of Educational Research 64(3):363–423. A hierarchical meta-analysis of about 96 experiments concluding that rewards do not, overall, decrease intrinsic motivation; that verbal rewards increase it; and that the only reliable negative effect is on free-choice time when expected tangible rewards are given for merely doing a task. It called for "abandoning cognitive evaluation theory". Unread: registered from the account of it in [LIT-557](../../record/literature.d/LIT-557.md) and the abstract of [LIT-562](../../record/literature.d/LIT-562.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Crossref and from the two papers that analyse it, not read. The article is closed access at SAGE (Review of Educational Research), and Unpaywall finds no open copy. A web search found it only on Scribd, a third-party upload, which was not opened. Its authors' reply to Deci, Koestner and Ryan, Cameron, Banko and Pierce (2001, [LIT-562](../../record/literature.d/LIT-562.md)), is free in PubMed Central but could not be reached here either, so it is also Deferred. A reader with SAGE access could promote this. |
| [LIT-557](../../record/literature.d/LIT-557.md) | A meta-analytic review of experiments examining the effects of extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation | Deci, Koestner & Ryan (1999), Psychological Bulletin 125(6):627–668. A hierarchical meta-analysis of 128 experiments (101 with free-choice, 84 with self-report measures) on interesting tasks. Tangible rewards undermined free-choice intrinsic motivation (d = −0.34) and self-reported interest (−0.07); expected rewards did, by every task-related contingency; unexpected and task-noncontingent rewards did not; positive feedback enhanced both measures, but not children's free choice. Read as strong support for cognitive evaluation theory and against Eisenberger and Cameron's behavioural account. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-438](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-438.md)), from an OCR of the scanned published article the authors post on selfdeterminationtheory.org; worth reading as the largest meta-analysis of the reward-undermining effect and the evidential base SDT cites for it. Across 128 experiments on interesting tasks, expected tangible rewards undermined free-choice intrinsic motivation (d = −0.36), engagement-, completion- and performance-contingent rewards all did (−0.40, −0.36, −0.28), unexpected and task-noncontingent rewards did not, and positive feedback enhanced it (0.33), though not for children's free choice. It reanalyses Cameron and Pierce ([LIT-556](../../record/literature.d/LIT-556.md)) study by study. Its categories are CET's, and the rival authors reply, by the abstract of Cameron, Banko and Pierce ([LIT-562](../../record/literature.d/LIT-562.md), unread), that the negative effects are circumscribed, not pervasive. |
| [LIT-558](../../record/literature.d/LIT-558.md) | The "What" and "Why" of Goal Pursuits: Human Needs and the Self-Determination of Behavior | Deci & Ryan (2000), Psychological Inquiry 11(4):227–268, target article. SDT's case for innate psychological needs, for competence, relatedness and autonomy, defined as nutriments for growth, integrity and well-being and identified functionally, against Hull's drives and Murray's acquired needs. The "why" of a goal (autonomous or controlled regulation) and its "what" (intrinsic or extrinsic aspiration) each predict well-being, because each affects need satisfaction. Thwarted needs yield substitutes, controlled styles and rigid patterns. SDT grounds autonomy in the organism's tendency to self-organize, argued to be an evolved advantage. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-439](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-439.md)), from the authors' own deposit of the published target article on selfdeterminationtheory.org; worth reading as the full statement of what SDT means by a "need" and why it posits three. Needs are "innate psychological nutriments that are essential for ongoing psychological growth, integrity, and well-being" (p. 228), identified by what follows when they are satisfied or thwarted, not by how strongly people want them. Goals differ in their "why" (autonomous or controlled regulation) and their "what" (intrinsic or extrinsic content), and both matter because of need satisfaction. Thwarting produces need substitutes, controlled regulatory styles and rigid behaviour patterns. It is a target article: the commentaries and the authors' reply in the same issue were not read. |
| [LIT-559](../../record/literature.d/LIT-559.md) | Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation, social development, and well-being | Ryan & Deci (2000), American Psychologist 55(1):68–78. Self-determination theory in eleven pages. Three innate psychological needs, for competence, autonomy and relatedness, must be satisfied for growth, integration and well-being; their thwarting is "a principal source of human distress" (p. 74). Motivation differs in kind, not only amount: extrinsic motivation runs from external through introjected and identified to integrated regulation, and becomes more autonomous as a regulation is internalized and integrated with the self. Autonomy is volition, not independence. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-442](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-442.md)), from the authors' own deposit of the published article on selfdeterminationtheory.org; worth reading as the shortest statement of self-determination theory by its authors, and the one most cited. It gives the three basic psychological needs (competence, autonomy, relatedness), the continuum of regulation from amotivation through external, introjected, identified and integrated regulation to intrinsic motivation, and the claim that autonomy is volition, "not ... being independent, detached, or selfish" (p. 74). It is a review: every empirical claim is cited to other papers, nothing is tested here, and it states the reward-undermining result as settled by Deci, Koestner & Ryan ([LIT-557](../../record/literature.d/LIT-557.md)), which Cameron, Banko & Pierce dispute ([LIT-562](../../record/literature.d/LIT-562.md), unread; by its abstract). |
| [LIT-560](../../record/literature.d/LIT-560.md) | Self-Regulation and the Problem of Human Autonomy: Does Psychology Need Choice, Self-Determination, and Will? | Ryan & Deci (2006), Journal of Personality 74(6):1557–1586. SDT's defence of autonomy as a psychological construct. Philosophers (phenomenologists from Pfänder and Ricoeur; hierarchical accounts from Frankfurt and Dworkin) agree that autonomy is self-endorsement, not independence. On that definition, the critiques fail: behaviourists and Bandura define autonomy as independence from the environment; reductive neuroscience mistakes levels of explanation; nonconscious priming shows autonomy can be compromised, not that it is illusory; too many options is not self-determination; and autonomy matters across cultures. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-441](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-441.md)), from the authors' own deposit of the published article on selfdeterminationtheory.org; worth reading as the place where SDT states its concept of autonomy against both philosophy and psychology. Autonomy is "regulation by the self", its opposite "heteronomy", regulation "without self-endorsement" (abstract). It is "a matter of degree" (p. 1562), compatible with dependence and with having one option, and lies "not in being independent causes but in exercising their capacity to reflectively endorse or reject prompted actions" (p. 1573). It answers Skinner, Bandura, Pinker, Bargh, Wegner, Schwartz and Iyengar. Its engagement with Frankfurt is thin: two citations of the 1971 paper inside one paragraph on hierarchical accounts, with the analytic detail taken from Dworkin. |
| [LIT-561](../../record/literature.d/LIT-561.md) | Basic psychological need theory: Advancements, critical themes, and future directions | Vansteenkiste, Ryan & Soenens (2020), Motivation and Emotion 44(1):1–31. Two decades of basic psychological need theory. A basic need must be psychological, essential, inherent, distinct and universal, and its effects pervasive, content-specific, directional and explanatory. Need frustration is not merely low satisfaction: it predicts ill-being, compensatory behaviour and need substitutes, and may be transdiagnostic. Novelty, morality and beneficence are reviewed as candidate fourth needs; none is yet admitted. Universality holds for the effects of need satisfaction, not for how needs are supported. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-440](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-440.md)), from the publisher's typeset PDF that the authors post on selfdeterminationtheory.org; worth reading as the current statement of basic psychological need theory and of what would make something a basic need. It gives nine criteria (psychological, essential, inherent, distinct, universal; pervasive, content-specific, directional, explanatory), treats need frustration as distinct from low satisfaction and as a candidate transdiagnostic process in psychopathology, and restates universality as "universality without uniformity": the effects of need satisfaction are claimed invariant, the ways needs are supported are not. It is an editorial introduction to two special issues, by SDT's authors, and reviews selectively. |
| [LIT-562](../../record/literature.d/LIT-562.md) | Pervasive negative effects of rewards on intrinsic motivation: The myth continues | Cameron, Banko & Pierce (2001), The Behavior Analyst 24(1):1–44. A new meta-analysis meant "to resolve differences in previous meta-analytic findings", against Deci, Koestner and Ryan's "pervasive negative effects". Per its abstract: rewards are in general not harmful; rewards for low-interest tasks enhance free choice; verbal rewards are positive; negative effects appear on high-interest tasks only when rewards are tangible, expected and "loosely tied to level of performance". Unread: registered from the PubMed abstract. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Crossref and the PubMed abstract (PMID 22478353, fetched through NCBI E-utilities), not read. The article is free to read in PubMed Central (PMC2731358) as a scanned PDF, but PMC's article pages and both Europe PMC PDF routes answered this environment with bot challenges (reCAPTCHA, Cloudflare), which were not passed, and E-utilities says the publisher does not allow XML download. SpringerLink also served a challenge. A copy on a third-party site was found and is not the basis of anything here. A human can read the free PMC scan in a browser, and a reading would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-563](../../record/literature.d/LIT-563.md) | HDDM: Hierarchical Bayesian Estimation of the Drift-Diffusion Model in Python | Wiecki, Sofer & Frank (2013), Frontiers in Neuroinformatics 7:14. HDDM is an open-source Python package that estimates the drift diffusion model (and the linear ballistic accumulator) by hierarchical Bayesian MCMC: subject parameters drawn from group distributions with informative priors, full posteriors, outlier handling, and regression of parameters on trial-by-trial covariates. A tutorial reproduces a theta-power effect on decision threshold that reverses under subthalamic deep brain stimulation. In simulations hierarchical Bayes recovers parameters and detects effects better than χ²-quantile and maximum-likelihood fitting, most with few trials. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-466](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-466.md)); worth reading as the methods paper for HDDM, the Python package that fits the drift diffusion model by hierarchical Bayesian estimation (PyMC, MCMC), with subject parameters drawn from group distributions and trial-by-trial regressions of parameters on covariates such as EEG power. Its simulations show hierarchical Bayes recovering parameters with less error than per-subject χ²-quantile, maximum-likelihood or non-hierarchical Bayesian fits, most of all with few trials, and detecting drift-rate differences and covariate effects more often. The comparisons are the authors' own, on data simulated from the model being fitted, and its hypothesis tests on the group posterior differ from the t-tests used for the other methods. |
| [LIT-564](../../record/literature.d/LIT-564.md) | The Sources of Normativity | Korsgaard (1992), Tanner Lectures at Clare Hall, Cambridge. The "normative question" asks what justifies morality's claims on us. Voluntarism, realism and reflective endorsement each fail alone. The answer is autonomy: because human consciousness is reflective, we need reasons, and so need a "conception of your practical identity" that makes us "a law to yourself". Obligation is "the reflective rejection of a threat to your identity". Valuing any identity commits us to valuing our humanity, and reasons are public, so others can obligate us. "Autonomy is the source of obligation." | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-465](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-465.md)), as the read substitute for the book The Sources of Normativity (1996, [LIT-599](../../record/literature.d/LIT-599.md)), which is its "longer version" with commentaries and a reply. Worth reading as Korsgaard's own first statement of practical identity and of the claim that "autonomy is the source of obligation". The reflective structure of human consciousness means we must act for reasons and so must have "a conception of your practical identity", "a description under which you value yourself"; reasons express that identity and obligations "spring from what that identity forbids". Valuing any practical identity commits one to valuing one's humanity, and the publicity of reasons commits one to others. The lectures concede that the argument is "sketchy, and sketchily argued". |
| [LIT-565](../../record/literature.d/LIT-565.md) | The Time Course of Perceptual Choice: The Leaky, Competing Accumulator Model | Usher & McClelland (2001), Psychological Review 108(3):550–592. Perceptual choice as gradual, stochastic accumulation in one unit per alternative, with leakage and lateral inhibition between units and a threshold at zero. For two choices the linearised difference is an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process with net leak K = k − β; K = 0 recovers the classical diffusion model, and K ≠ 0 bounds asymptotic accuracy. The model fits time–accuracy curves slightly better than diffusion with drift variance, fits Vickers's latency–discriminability data, predicts individual primacy or recency biases confirmed in a new experiment, and yields Hick's law under a constant-accuracy criterion. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-449](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-449.md)); worth reading as the source of the leaky, competing accumulator (LCA): one leaky accumulator per alternative, lateral inhibition between them, a threshold-linear floor at zero, and an absolute response criterion. In the linear two-choice case the difference between accumulators is an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process with net leak K = k − β, so the classical diffusion model is the special case K = 0 and accuracy given unlimited time is bounded whenever K ≠ 0. Its evidence for leakage over drift variance is a modest likelihood advantage on 3 participants (combined ratio about 62 to 1, 7 of 9 curves), and the authors concede that a diffusion model with drift variance, starting-point variance and decision boundaries is hard to tell apart from it. |
| [LIT-566](../../record/literature.d/LIT-566.md) | Defining Agency: Individuality, Normativity, Asymmetry, and Spatio-temporality in Action | Barandiaran, Di Paolo & Rohde (2009), Adaptive Behavior 17(5):367–386. Three conditions are jointly necessary and sufficient for minimal agency: the system defines its own individuality, it is the active source of modulations of its coupling with the environment (interactional asymmetry), and it regulates that coupling by norms it generates (normativity). Generatively, an agent is an open autonomous system (a precarious network of mutually enabling processes) that adaptively modulates its coupling so as to maintain some of its constituent processes. Minimal life satisfies this; life is sufficient for agency but not necessary. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-458](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-458.md)), from the authors' own deposit of the accepted text. Worth reading as the standard organizational definition of minimal agency, and as the clearest statement that an agent is not the same thing as an individual. Individuality is one of three conditions, with interactional asymmetry and normativity; it is a precondition for the other two and is not sufficient for agency. The generative definition: an agent is an autonomous organization that adaptively regulates its coupling with its environment and contributes to sustaining itself as a consequence. The paper rejects statistical and energetic measures of asymmetry as sufficient, and says systems optimising an externally fixed function should not be treated as models of agency. |
| [LIT-567](../../record/literature.d/LIT-567.md) | A Planning Agent's Self-Governance over Time | Bratman (2018), essay 11 of Planning, Time, and Self-Governance (OUP). A planning agent's self-governance over time is self-governance at each time along a planned activity, plus cross-temporal links of intention that parallel the interlocking intentions of shared agency: in diachronic self-governance one acts "together" with oneself. Standpoints are "diachronicalized" by a conditional, reflexive end of one's own diachronic self-governance. That end can make willpower and resistance to "shuffling" self-governed, while anticipated regret at "plan's end" excludes drinking Kavka's toxin. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-464](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-464.md)), in the author's pre-publication draft of 10 July 2017, not the typeset chapter. Worth reading as the read substitute for Planning, Time, and Self-Governance (2018) and, through its summary of the planning theory, for Intention, Plans, and Practical Reason (1987, [LIT-593](../../record/literature.d/LIT-593.md)). A planning agent governs herself over time when she is self-governing at each time along a planned activity and her intentions at those times interlock like the intentions of people acting together, so that she acts "together" with herself. Her standpoints are "diachronicalized": they include a conditional, reflexive end of her own diachronic self-governance. That end explains how willpower and resisting "shuffling" between incomparable options can be self-governed, and anticipated regret at plan's end rules out drinking Kavka's toxin. A philosophical model argued by cases; nothing in it is tested. |
| [LIT-568](../../record/literature.d/LIT-568.md) | Précis: Being No One | Metzinger (2005), PSYCHE 11(5), the author's précis of Being No One. Minimal consciousness is a global world-model in a window of presence that cannot be recognised as a model (globality, presentationality, transparency). A phenomenal self is a transparent self-model: "no such things as selves exist in the world". Transparency comes in degrees, and the self-model's opaque partition is what lets humans doubt their own self-representation. The unity of consciousness is a single global functional cluster. A first-person perspective is a phenomenal model of the intentionality relation, and iterating it yields introspective and social intelligence. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-460](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-460.md)), as the read substitute for Being No One ([LIT-467](../../record/literature.d/LIT-467.md)), which it summarises in Metzinger's own words. Worth reading as the most complete short statement of the self-model theory of subjectivity (SMT): more of the machinery than "Self models" ([LIT-450](../../record/literature.d/LIT-450.md)), in particular the six constraints, the degrees of transparency, and the phenomenal model of the intentionality relation (PMIR). Phenomenal selfhood is a transparent self-model, "a function realized by a lack of information"; "no such things as selves exist in the world". The unity of a conscious world is a global functional cluster bound by "highest-order binding". It states the theory and its predictions; the evidence is cited, not shown, and the neural sections say how little is known. |
| [LIT-569](../../record/literature.d/LIT-569.md) | The Physics of Optimal Decision Making: A Formal Analysis of Models of Performance in Two-Alternative Forced-Choice Tasks | Bogacz, Brown, Moehlis, Holmes & Cohen (2006), Psychological Review 113(4):700–765. The pure drift diffusion model implements the SPRT and Neyman–Pearson test, so it is the optimal two-choice decision process. The O-U, mutual inhibition (Usher–McClelland), feedforward inhibition and pooled inhibition (Wang) models reduce to it for balanced parameters; the race model does not. For reward rate, Bayes risk and two accuracy-weighted criteria there is a unique optimal threshold, giving parameter-free optimal performance curves. With biased priors the optimal starting point is proportional to the log prior odds. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-461](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-461.md)), main text and Appendix B in full, Appendix A (the derivations) by section only; worth reading as the paper that puts the two-choice decision models in one frame and ties them to the sequential probability ratio test. The pure drift diffusion model is the continuum limit of the SPRT, so it is the fastest for a given accuracy and the most accurate for a given time. Of six models, all but the race model reduce to it in some parameter range: the Usher–McClelland mutual inhibition model when leak equals inhibition and both are large, the feedforward inhibition model when its inhibitory and excitatory weights are equal. Each reward criterion has a unique optimal threshold, which gives testable optimal performance curves. The optimality results hold for the pure model with stationary evidence and two alternatives. |
| [LIT-570](../../record/literature.d/LIT-570.md) | An Organizational Account of Biological Functions | Mossio, Saborido & Moreno (2009), BJPS 60(4):813–841. A trait has a function iff it contributes to the maintenance of a self-maintaining organization, is produced and maintained under that organization's constraints, and the organization is differentiated. Closure grounds teleology and normativity in the current system, unifying etiological and dispositional accounts. Flames, Bénard cells and hurricanes have closure but no functions, because each makes a single undifferentiated contribution to its own maintenance. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-444](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-444.md)), from the HAL deposit of the submitted version. Worth reading as the organizational account of function and as the place where the organizational school says plainly that a candle flame has closure. Functions are contributions to a differentiated self-maintaining organization by traits that organization produces and maintains. Closure alone grounds teleology and norms but is shared by flames, Bénard cells and hurricanes; organizational differentiation is what functions need. Reproduction is admitted to be a problem and left to a sketch. |
| [LIT-571](../../record/literature.d/LIT-571.md) | Participatory sense-making: An enactive approach to social cognition | De Jaegher & Di Paolo (2007), Phenomenology and the Cognitive Sciences 6(4):485–507. Coordination between coupled systems is cheap and common. When patterns of coordination and the course of an encounter shape each other, the interaction acquires its own operationally closed, precarious organization. Social interaction is then regulated coupling between autonomous agents that constitutes an autonomous relational organization without destroying the agents' autonomy. Because sense-making is embodied in movement, interactors can coordinate their sense-making, along a spectrum from modulated individual sense-making to jointly created meaning. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-456](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-456.md)), from the published version posted on De Jaegher's own site. Worth reading as the founding statement of participatory sense-making. A social interaction is defined as regulated coupling between autonomous agents that itself becomes an autonomous organization in the relational domain, without destroying the agents' autonomy; coordinating sense-making in interaction can generate meanings neither individual had. The argument is conceptual, illustrated by everyday cases and by the perceptual-crossing experiments; nothing is measured in the paper. |
| [LIT-572](../../record/literature.d/LIT-572.md) | Narrative Identity | McAdams & McLean (2013), Current Directions in Psychological Science 22(3):233–238. Per its abstract, narrative identity is "a person's internalized and evolving life story, integrating the reconstructed past and imagined future to provide life with some degree of unity and purpose". Narrators who find redemptive meanings in adversity and whose stories feature agency and exploration "tend to enjoy higher levels of mental health, well-being, and maturity". Unread: registered from the abstract; the construct is read in [LIT-595](../../record/literature.d/LIT-595.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref record and its abstract, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. SAGE does not make it open, Unpaywall and OpenAlex report it closed with no repository copy, Kate McLean's publications page lists it without a file, the APS journal page offers no PDF, and ResearchGate refused this session (403). Its definition and its role are read first-hand in McAdams's 2019 essay ([LIT-595](../../record/literature.d/LIT-595.md), read in full), which quotes the definition and builds on it. Filed because it is the canonical citation for narrative identity in psychology. A human reader with SAGE access could read its six pages and move it to Active. |
| [LIT-573](../../record/literature.d/LIT-573.md) | Self-Command in Practice, in Policy, and in a Theory of Rational Choice | Schelling (1984), American Economic Review 74(2):1-11, the Richard T. Ely Lecture, on self-command and what it means for the theory of rational choice. Unread: no lawful free copy. Schelling's 1980 essay "The Intimate Contest for Self-Command", [LIT-602](../../record/literature.d/LIT-602.md), is read in its place. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from bibliographic records, not read. No lawful free copy was found. The article is on JSTOR (AER 74(2), the May 1984 Papers and Proceedings issue), which returned a bot challenge and was not pursued. RePEc lists no downloadable version. It is reprinted in Schelling's Strategies of Commitment and Other Essays (Harvard University Press, 2006, pp. 63-81), which is not open access. The only copies found online were on a personal website that is not the author's and on course pages, which this record does not use. Schelling's earlier, freely published essay on the same subject was read instead and filed as [LIT-602](../../record/literature.d/LIT-602.md) ([NOTE-452](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-452.md)). A human with JSTOR access could read this lecture and move it to Active. |
| [LIT-574](../../record/literature.d/LIT-574.md) | A Multicomponent Conceptualization of Authenticity: Theory and Research | Kernis & Goldman (2006), Advances in Experimental Social Psychology 38:283–357. Unread. As two read papers report it: authenticity is "the unimpeded operation of one's true self in one's daily enterprise", it is measured by the 45-item Authenticity Inventory (AI-3), and dispositional authenticity correlates with self-esteem (r = .47), self-concept clarity and self-actualization and negatively with distress. It is commonly summarized as four components: awareness, unbiased processing, behaviour and relational orientation. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref record and from two read papers that report it, not read. No lawful full text was found: the chapter is paywalled at Elsevier (ScienceDirect), Unpaywall and Semantic Scholar list it as closed, and the copies a web search turns up are uploads to ResearchGate and academia.edu, neither an author or institutional deposit that could be reached (ResearchGate refuses automated requests). Michael Kernis died in 2009, so no author page holds it. It is a 75-page chapter; a human reader with library access to Advances in Experimental Social Psychology 38 can read it, and that would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-575](../../record/literature.d/LIT-575.md) | Identification and Wholeheartedness | Frankfurt (1987/88), in Schoeman (ed.), Responsibility, Character, and the Emotions (CUP), pp. 27–45; reprinted in The Importance of What We Care About (CUP 1988), pp. 159–176. Unread. By the record's second-hand reports it is Frankfurt's answer to the regress in the hierarchical account: identification ends in a "decisive commitment" made "without reservation", which is not arbitrary, and wholeheartedness is the integration that such a decision aims at, extruding the rival desire "as an outlaw". | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from Cambridge's chapter records and the record's own second-hand readings, not read. No lawful full text was found: the chapter is paywalled at Cambridge Core in both the Schoeman volume and the 1988 collection, Unpaywall lists no open copy, and the copies a web search turns up are uploads to academia.edu and lecture notes about the essay, not the essay. A human reader with library access to either volume (pp. 27–45 of the first, pp. 159–176 of the second) can read it in an hour; that reading would move this to Active and is the first-hand text [THEORY-029](../../record/theory.d/THEORY-029.md) needs for its middle case. |
| [LIT-576](../../record/literature.d/LIT-576.md) | The Diffusion Decision Model: Theory and Data for Two-Choice Decision Tasks | Ratcliff & McKoon (2008), Neural Computation 20(4):873–922. A review of the diffusion decision model for fast two-choice tasks, with three new motion-discrimination experiments. Noisy evidence accumulates from a starting point z to one of two boundaries (0, a) at drift v, plus non-decision time and across-trial variability in v, z and Ter. The model fits accuracy and full correct and error RT distributions, and maps difficulty to drift, speed–accuracy instructions to boundary separation and stimulus proportion mainly to starting point. Applications cover aging, aphasia and links to neural firing. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-445](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-445.md)), from the author manuscript in PubMed Central; worth reading as the canonical statement of the diffusion decision model and of what it is for. Two-choice RT and accuracy are decomposed into drift rate (quality of evidence), boundary separation (caution), starting point (bias) and non-decision time, plus across-trial variability in drift, starting point and non-decision time. Three new motion-discrimination experiments show each manipulation landing on its own parameter: difficulty on drift, speed–accuracy instructions on boundaries, stimulus proportion mainly on starting point. Its closing claim, that diffusion models have come "as near to provid[ing] a solution to simple decision making as is possible in behavioral science", is the authors' assessment, not a result. |
| [LIT-577](../../record/literature.d/LIT-577.md) | Sophisticated Inference | Friston, Da Costa, Hafner, Hesp & Parr (2021; arXiv 2020), Neural Computation 33(3):713–763. Ordinary active inference scores a fixed set of policies by expected free energy (risk plus ambiguity). Sophisticated inference instead evaluates the expected free energy of the next action recursively, averaging over the beliefs the agent would hold after each plausible outcome and action, which amounts to a tree search over belief states pruned below a probability of 1/16. In simulations it plans past short-term costs: a rat visits a mildly aversive cue, and a horizon of four escapes a local minimum in a maze. It recovers Bayes-adaptive RL in the zero-temperature limit. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-454](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-454.md)), from the arXiv preprint, whose displayed equations did not survive text extraction. Worth reading as the active-inference planning scheme the record's free-energy holdings otherwise lack: expected free energy evaluated recursively over future belief states, a deep tree search pruned by probability, with the Bellman equation as its formal analogue. The evidence is small simulations (a T-maze rat and an 8 × 8 grid).
