Flagged anthology-candidate: it is a planning algorithm, compared with reinforcement learning, and the anthology's agents-and-environments topic may hold it. |
| [LIT-578](../../record/literature.d/LIT-578.md) | Dynamic Patterns: The Self-Organization of Brain and Behavior | Kelso (1995), Dynamic Patterns: The Self-Organization of Brain and Behavior, MIT Press, xvii + 334 pp. The book-length statement of coordination dynamics. Unread: registered from catalogue records. What the record knows of its content comes from the works that cite it: the coordination potential of the HKB model (reproduced in Kelso 2021, Box 1), absolute versus relative coordination (De Jaegher & Di Paolo 2007, citing p. 98), a chapter on how HKB arose (ch. 3) and one on brain rhythms (ch. 8), per Kelso 2021. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 as a seed, not read. No lawful full text was found: the book has no DOI, is not listed as open access, and MIT Press's page refused an automated request. Under the owner's rule for books it is supplanted by a shorter work by the same author, Kelso's 2021 retrospective on the HKB model ([LIT-601](../../record/literature.d/LIT-601.md)), which was read. It is filed because it is the citation others reach for when they mean coordination dynamics, relative coordination or metastability; De Jaegher & Di Paolo ([LIT-571](../../record/literature.d/LIT-571.md)) and Raja et al. ([LIT-598](../../record/literature.d/LIT-598.md)) both cite it. |
| [LIT-579](../../record/literature.d/LIT-579.md) | Evolution of the Internal Family Systems Model | Schwartz (undated, c. 2000s; IFS Institute). IFS views a person as "an ecology of relatively discrete minds", each wanting a valuable role but forced into extreme ones by experience: managers keep control, exiles carry pain, and firefighters douse exiles' feelings by bingeing or numbing. Everyone has a Self that appears when parts "step back", and is not a passive witness but an active leader and healer. Its qualities are the "eight Cs". The goal is to differentiate the Self from the parts. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-453](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-453.md)); worth reading as Richard Schwartz's own short statement of the IFS model, freely published by the IFS Institute, and the read substitute (with chapter 1 of the book, [NOTE-457](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-457.md)) for Internal Family Systems Therapy ([LIT-591](../../record/literature.d/LIT-591.md)). IFS is "a new synthesis of two already-existing paradigms: systems thinking and the multiplicity of the mind". A person contains "an ecology of relatively discrete minds", forced into extreme roles (managers, firefighters, exiles) by experience. Everyone has a Self with "crucial leadership qualities", summed up as eight Cs: calmness, curiosity, clarity, compassion, confidence, creativity, courage and connectedness. A practitioner's essay: the evidence is clinical experience and one case. |
| [LIT-580](../../record/literature.d/LIT-580.md) | Uncertainty-based competition between prefrontal and dorsolateral striatal systems for behavioral control | Daw, Niv & Dayan (2005), Nature Neuroscience 8(12):1704-1711. Habitual and goal-directed control are model-free ("caching") and model-based ("tree-search") reinforcement learners in dorsolateral striatum and prefrontal cortex. Behaviour follows whichever controller's value estimate is less uncertain under approximate Bayesian versions of each. Simulations reproduce the devaluation data: over-training yields devaluation-insensitive habit, except for actions near reward or in tasks with more actions and outcomes. Both controllers serve the same ends; neither is the "irrational" one. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-459](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-459.md)), supplementary methods not read. Worth reading as the founding statement of model-based versus model-free control as two brain systems, and of arbitration between them by relative uncertainty. Goal-directed (devaluation-sensitive) behaviour is a prefrontal "tree-search" controller, statistically efficient but noisy to compute. Habit is a dorsolateral-striatal "caching" controller, cheap but data-hungry and blind to revaluation. Each controls behaviour when its value estimate is the more certain, which reproduces why over-training produces habits unless the task is complex or the action near the reward. Both controllers pursue "identical rational ends": a rejection of the impulsive-limbic-versus-rational-prefrontal picture. |
| [LIT-581](../../record/literature.d/LIT-581.md) | Hierarchically organized behavior and its neural foundations: A reinforcement learning perspective | Botvinick, Niv & Barto (2009), Cognition 113(3):262-280. Hierarchical RL groups actions into reusable "options" with their own policies, subgoals and pseudo-reward. That eases the scaling problem by cutting the number of decisions to explore and learn. The paper builds an actor-critic version and maps option identifiers to DLPFC, option-specific policies to dorsolateral striatum, and option-specific values and option-length prediction errors to OFC. Unsuitable options produce negative transfer, so how useful options are discovered is the central open problem. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-451](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-451.md)), from the PubMed Central author manuscript; the online appendix with the equations was not read. Worth reading as the bridge between hierarchical reinforcement learning (temporally abstract "options") and the psychology and neuroscience of hierarchically organized behaviour. Options ease the scaling problem of RL by shrinking search and learning to a few decision points. An actor-critic version maps option identifiers to dorsolateral PFC, option-specific policies to dorsolateral striatum, and option-specific values and extended prediction errors to orbitofrontal cortex. The problem it puts first is option discovery: the wrong options cause negative transfer, as in human problem solving. |
| [LIT-582](../../record/literature.d/LIT-582.md) | Biological organisation as closure of constraints | Montévil & Mossio (2015), J. Theor. Biol. 372:179–191. Two causal regimes act in organisms: processes (thermodynamic changes) and constraints, which act on processes while being conserved at the relevant time scale. Closure is mutual dependence among constraints: each is produced by a process another constraint constrains, and each helps produce another. Closure mixes slow and fast dependence, grounds functions, and is proposed as a boundary criterion. A volume-sweeping count of dependencies, the tendency to closure, separates symbionts that share an encompassing closure, and separated nested closures mark levels of organisation. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-468](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-468.md)), from the authors' HAL deposit of the accepted text. Worth reading as the paper statement of the central claim of Biological Autonomy ([LIT-536](../../record/literature.d/LIT-536.md)): biological organisation is closure of constraints. A constraint is defined by a symmetry: it acts on a process at a time scale at which it is itself conserved. Closure is a set of constraints each of which depends directly on another in the set and generates another, and cannot be split into two closed sets. The paper offers closure as a boundary criterion and gives a preliminary measure, the tendency to closure, for drawing boundaries between interacting organisms. It does not claim closure defines an organism. |
| [LIT-583](../../record/literature.d/LIT-583.md) | Breakdown of Will | Ainslie (2001), Cambridge University Press. The book-length statement of picoeconomics: hyperbolic discounting makes a person a population of bargaining interests, and will is the recursive bundling of choices into precedents. Unread: registered from the publisher's description and the précis, [LIT-586](../../record/literature.d/LIT-586.md), which stands in for it. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the publisher's description and from the outline of the book given in its précis, not read. Not open access: Unpaywall reports no open copy, and no author deposit of the book was found (the author's site, picoeconomics.org, carries the précis and articles, not the book). The owner suggested the précis instead, and it is read and filed as [LIT-586](../../record/literature.d/LIT-586.md) ([NOTE-443](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-443.md)), which follows the book's three sections and twelve chapters. The book is kept as the citation others reach for. A human with library access could read chapters 5-8 (the bargaining model and its evidence) against the précis. |
| [LIT-584](../../record/literature.d/LIT-584.md) | Model-based and model-free Pavlovian reward learning: Revaluation, revision, and revelation | Dayan & Berridge (2014), Cognitive, Affective, & Behavioral Neuroscience 14(2):473-492. Pavlovian prediction is not only model-free. A cue that had predicted disgusting salt became instantly "wanted" when a never-experienced salt appetite was induced, before the salt was retasted, which needs a prediction of the outcome's identity revalued against the current bodily state. Over-training may "defocus" outcome representations rather than hand control to a model-free system. Dopamine may carry model-based evaluations. No algorithm for the revaluation is given. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-463](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-463.md)), from the PubMed Central author manuscript. Worth reading as the argument that Pavlovian prediction, usually modelled as purely model-free, is also model-based. In the "Dead Sea salt" experiment a lever that had predicted a disgusting salt solution became, on first presentation after a novel sodium appetite was induced and before any salt was retasted, nearly as attractive as a sucrose lever, with mesolimbic activation. A cached value cannot do that. The CS must predict the outcome's identity, and its value must be recomputed against the current bodily state. The paper adds "defocusing" of outcome representations as a model-based route to apparent habit, and argues that dopamine can carry model-based evaluations. |
| [LIT-585](../../record/literature.d/LIT-585.md) | The Expected Value of Control: An Integrative Theory of Anterior Cingulate Cortex Function | Shenhav, Botvinick & Cohen (2013), Neuron 79(2):217-240. Control is allocated by a cost-benefit decision. The expected value of control (EVC) of a candidate control signal is its probability-weighted payoff, with future value discounted, minus an intrinsic cost rising with its intensity. dACC monitors state and outcome information and specifies the identity and intensity of the EVC-maximizing signal. Lateral PFC and subcortical structures implement it. The account integrates conflict monitoring, effort costs and the override of defaults (exploration, foraging, patience). | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-446](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-446.md)), from the PubMed Central author manuscript. Worth reading as the standard statement that cognitive control is allocated by a cost-benefit decision. The "expected value of control" (EVC) of a control signal, which has an identity (which task) and an intensity (how much), is its expected payoff minus an intrinsic cost that rises with intensity. Dorsal anterior cingulate cortex is proposed to monitor and to specify the EVC-maximizing signal, which lateral PFC and subcortical structures then implement. It is a review and synthesis: the model is stated, not fitted, and the authors say the ingredients are not new. |
| [LIT-586](../../record/literature.d/LIT-586.md) | Précis of Breakdown of Will | Ainslie (2005), Behavioral and Brain Sciences 28(5), the précis of Breakdown of Will (2001). People and other animals discount delayed rewards hyperbolically, so preferences reverse as a smaller, sooner reward comes near, and a person becomes a population of reward-seeking "interests" that must forestall one another. Willpower is the bundling of future choices by treating the present one as a precedent: an intertemporal repeated prisoner's dilemma, not an organ or a top-down ego. The same recursion predicts the will's costs: legalism, circumscribed "lapse districts", motivated blindness to lapses, and rules that favour countable goals over subtle ones. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-443](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-443.md)), as the substitute for the 2001 book ([LIT-583](../../record/literature.d/LIT-583.md)), which the owner suggested and which the précis follows chapter by chapter. Worth reading as the shortest complete statement of picoeconomics in Ainslie's own words. Hyperbolic discounting makes preference reverse as a smaller, sooner reward draws near, so a person behaves like a population of "interests" that bargain across time. Will is not a faculty above them but a recursive self-prediction: treating each choice as a precedent "bundles" future rewards, an intertemporal repeated prisoner's dilemma in which "credibility is power". The same mechanism predicts the will's side effects: legalism, "lapse districts", motivated misperception and compulsive over-control. |
| [LIT-587](../../record/literature.d/LIT-587.md) | The Normative Constitution of Agency | Korsgaard (2014), in Rational and Social Agency: The Philosophy of Michael Bratman (OUP), pp. 190–214. Two conceptions of agency are set side by side: natural (action is movement caused by the right mental state) and normatively constituted (action is movement backed by a unified agent, whose unity is achieved by following norms). Following the categorical and hypothetical imperatives gives action "the form of self-determined efficacy". Choosing maxims under that form constitutes one's practical identity. Self-consciousness of one's own attitudes sets the task of unification. Unity of agency "is an achievement, not a given", so people can literally act together. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-455](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-455.md)), in the author's posted manuscript, not the typeset chapter. Worth reading as the read substitute for Self-Constitution (2009, [LIT-597](../../record/literature.d/LIT-597.md)), which it summarises and extends, and as Korsgaard's direct engagement with Bratman. Agency is "normatively constituted": an action is a movement "backed by the agent as a whole", and that unity is achieved by following the categorical and hypothetical imperatives, which give action "the form of self-determined efficacy". The content of the maxims we choose under that form constitutes our "practical identities". So "we make ourselves into agents", and unity of agency, individual or collective, "is an achievement, not a given". Argued, not proved; the account of reason as unification is said to be "lifted" from her other work. |
| [LIT-588](../../record/literature.d/LIT-588.md) | A theoretical model of phase transitions in human hand movements | Haken, Kelso & Bunz (1985), Biological Cybernetics 51(5):347–356. The HKB model, as described by Kelso 2021 ([LIT-601](../../record/literature.d/LIT-601.md)): relative phase φ between two rhythmically moving fingers is the order parameter, with potential V(φ) = −a cos φ − b cos 2φ; anti-phase coordination loses stability as movement frequency rises and the hands switch to in-phase, and the φ dynamics is derived from two nonlinearly coupled oscillators. Unread: registered from metadata and a secondary account. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03, not read. No lawful full text was found. Unpaywall lists the article as closed; Springer's page offers it only by subscription or purchase; Europe PMC has the record (PMID 3978150) without full text; no author or repository deposit turned up in a web search. The read substitute is Kelso's 2021 retrospective on the model ([LIT-601](../../record/literature.d/LIT-601.md)), which gives the model's equations and regimes. A reader with Springer access could read the ten pages and promote this. |
| [LIT-589](../../record/literature.d/LIT-589.md) | Goal Striving, Need Satisfaction, and Longitudinal Well-Being: The Self-Concordance Model | Sheldon & Elliot (1999), JPSP 76(3):482–497. A goal is self-concordant when pursued for identified or intrinsic rather than external or introjected reasons. In three semester-long studies of students, self-concordance predicted sustained effort, which fully mediated its link to attainment; attainment raised well-being more when goals were self-concordant; and accumulated daily experiences of autonomy, competence and relatedness partly mediated attainment's effect on well-being. Paths survived controls for self-efficacy, implementation intentions, avoidance framing and life skills. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-448](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-448.md)); worth reading as the canonical statement of the self-concordance model and its evidence. Goals pursued for identified or intrinsic reasons get more sustained effort, so are better attained (effort fully mediates, in Studies 1 and 3), and attaining them raises well-being more, partly through accumulated daily experiences of autonomy, competence and relatedness. The evidence is three semester-long correlational studies of students (N = 169, 152, 73), with structural models refitted after modification indices, and self-concordance is a self-report of reasons that cannot separate integration from identification. |
| [LIT-590](../../record/literature.d/LIT-590.md) | Structures of Agency: Essays | Bratman (2007), Oxford University Press: twelve essays (1996–2006) on how planning structures human agency over time and ground self-governance. They include "Reflection, Planning, and Temporally Extended Agency" (on the Lockean role of plans), "Two Problems about Human Agency", "Autonomy and Hierarchy", "Three Theories of Self-Governance" and "Temptation Revisited". Unread: registered from the contents; the theory is read in Bratman's 2018 essay [LIT-567](../../record/literature.d/LIT-567.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref records of the book and its thirteen chapters, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. Oxford Academic is closed, and the Internet Archive scan (structuresofagen0000brat) is an access-restricted lending copy, not opened. No chapter was found as a lawful open article. Its theory of self-governance is read first-hand in Bratman's later essay "A Planning Agent's Self-Governance over Time" (2018, [LIT-567](../../record/literature.d/LIT-567.md)), which builds on its essays. Filed because it is the collection in which the planning theory of self-governance and its Lockean reading appear, and other filings in this batch cite it. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-591](../../record/literature.d/LIT-591.md) | Internal Family Systems Therapy | Schwartz & Sweezy (2019/2020), Guilford, 2nd ed., the authoritative text of Internal Family Systems therapy. IFS synthesises "the plural mind, or the idea that we all contain many different parts, and systems thinking". Extreme parts are protectors or burdened exiles, not pathology, and everyone has a Self that can lead the inner system once parts "step back". Therapy restores trust in the Self's leadership and unburdens exiles. Chapter 1 read; the rest unread. | Deferred — chapter 1 read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-457](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-457.md), Skimmed), from the sample chapter Guilford posts; the other nineteen chapters unread. No lawful full text could be reached: the book is sold by Guilford, the Internet Archive's scans are of the 1995 first edition and access-restricted, and Schwartz's 2013 Journal of Clinical Psychology article and the 2023 Family Process article (Brenner, Schwartz & Becker) are closed, the latter's author share-link behind a Cloudflare challenge. Chapter 1 gives the model's origin and its eight "essentials", and the IFS Institute's "Evolution of the Internal Family Systems Model" ([LIT-579](../../record/literature.d/LIT-579.md), read in full) gives Schwartz's short statement of parts, roles and the Self. Filed because it is the canonical text of IFS. A reading of chapters 2–4 (systems, the Self, burdens) and 19 (research) would place it. |
| [LIT-592](../../record/literature.d/LIT-592.md) | The Authentic Personality: A Theoretical and Empirical Conceptualization and the Development of the Authenticity Scale | Wood, Linley, Maltby, Baliousis & Joseph (2008), Journal of Counseling Psychology 55(3):385–399. Unread. Per its abstract it builds the 12-item Authenticity Scale on a tripartite, person-centred conception (self-alienation, authentic living, accepting external influence), supported by exploratory and multigroup confirmatory factor analysis, with discriminant validity from the Big Five, nonsignificant correlations with social desirability, test–retest r = .78 to .91, and strong relations of every subscale to self-esteem and well-being. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the abstract on the University of Manchester research portal and the repository records, not read. Every lawful copy found is out of reach: Manchester's portal lists a pre-peer-review manuscript (PRE-PEER-REVIEW.PDF, 143 KB) whose download is behind a JavaScript and cookie challenge, which I did not try to get past; Stirling's STORRE holds the published version under a permanent embargo ("The publisher does not allow this work to be made publicly available"); Warwick's WRAP and Lincoln's repository records carry no file; ResearchGate refused the request. A human reader can open the Manchester manuscript in a browser, or ask an author for a copy through STORRE; either would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-593](../../record/literature.d/LIT-593.md) | Intention, Plans, and Practical Reason | Bratman (1987), Harvard University Press; reissued CSLI 1999. The founding statement of the planning theory of intention. As Bratman summarises it in [LIT-567](../../record/literature.d/LIT-567.md), prior partial plans "settle practical issues, pose problems of means and the like, filter solutions to those problems, and guide action", supporting cross-temporal and social organisation within our cognitive limits. Unread: registered from catalogue records; the theory is read in the 2018 essay. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from library catalogue records, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. Harvard University Press (1987) and the CSLI reissue (1999) are not open, and the Internet Archive's two scans are access-restricted lending copies, not opened. Its planning theory is read first-hand in Bratman's own later statement, "A Planning Agent's Self-Governance over Time" (2018, [LIT-567](../../record/literature.d/LIT-567.md), read in full), which extends it. Filed because it is the canonical citation for the planning theory of intention. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-594](../../record/literature.d/LIT-594.md) | The Ethics of Authenticity | Taylor (1991), Harvard University Press, 142 pp.; first published in Canada the same year by Anansi as The Malaise of Modernity, an extended version of the 1991 CBC Massey Lectures. Unread. As commonly summarized (no publisher's description was reachable), it argues that the modern ideal of authenticity, being true to oneself, is a genuine moral ideal rather than mere narcissism or relativism, and that it is degraded when it is cut off from shared "horizons of significance" and from the dialogical way identities are formed. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from catalogue records, not read. No lawful full text was found: the book is in print with Harvard University Press, the Internet Archive copy (ethicsofauthenti00tayl, the 1992 printing) is an access-restricted lending copy, the publisher's digital edition at De Gruyter returned an empty bot-check response, and the full-text copies a web search turns up are file-sharing uploads, which were not opened. No shorter, lawfully readable work by Taylor stating the same argument was found to read in its place (see Standing in the record). The book is about 140 pages, so a human reader with a library copy can read it in an afternoon; that reading would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-595](../../record/literature.d/LIT-595.md) | “First we invented stories, then they changed us”: The Evolution of Narrative Identity | McAdams (2019), Evolutionary Studies in Imaginative Culture 3(1):1–18. Narrative identity is "the internalized and evolving story a person invents to explain how he or she has become the person he or she is becoming", giving a life "unity, moral purpose, and temporal coherence". Storytelling evolved with human hypersociality. The self develops from social actor (by age 2) to motivated agent (school years) to autobiographical author (adolescence). Agentic and redemptive stories go with well-being and generativity, and cultures supply master narratives. "Biology guides and culture fills in the details." | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-470](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-470.md)), as the read substitute for McAdams & McLean, "Narrative Identity" (2013; [LIT-572](../../record/literature.d/LIT-572.md)), which has no lawful copy and whose definition this essay takes over word for word. Worth reading as McAdams's own short statement of narrative identity: "the internalized and evolving story a person invents to explain how he or she has become the person he or she is becoming", which gives a life "unity, moral purpose, and temporal coherence". It places the life story as the third layer of a self that develops from actor to agent to author, and reports that story features (agency, redemption, coherence) predict well-being and generativity. A review essay: the empirical claims are cited, not shown, and the evolutionary story is offered as plausible, not tested. |
| [LIT-596](../../record/literature.d/LIT-596.md) | Three Theories of Self-Governance | Bratman (2004), Philosophical Topics 32(1/2):21–46; reprinted in Structures of Agency (OUP 2007), pp. 222–253. Unread. Per the publisher's abstract it revisits the Frankfurt–Watson exchange over the structures of free agency, treats it as a debate about self-governance, holds that agential authority comes from an attitude's role in the "Lockean organization" of temporally extended agency rather than from tracking the good, gives that role to self-governing policies, and proposes an intention-based theory. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref and Oxford Academic records, not read. No lawful full text was found: the article is paywalled at the Philosophy Documentation Center (purchase, $20) and the reprint at Oxford Academic, Unpaywall and Semantic Scholar list no open copy, and PhilPapers and ResearchGate refused automated requests (403). A human reader with access to Philosophical Topics 32 or to Structures of Agency (pp. 222–253) can read it; that reading would move this to Active and would supply [THEORY-029](../../record/theory.d/THEORY-029.md) with Bratman's self-governing policies first-hand. |
| [LIT-597](../../record/literature.d/LIT-597.md) | Self-Constitution: Agency, Identity, and Integrity | Korsgaard (2009), Oxford University Press, from her 2002 Locke Lectures. The argument is that we constitute ourselves as agents, and as the particular agents we are, by choosing under the constitutive principles of action, the categorical and hypothetical imperatives. On Plato's "constitutional model", good action is action that issues from a unified soul. The chapters run from agency and identity to "How to be a Person". Unread: registered from the contents; the account is read in [LIT-587](../../record/literature.d/LIT-587.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref records of the book and its ten chapters, not read: no lawful copy of the book could be reached. Oxford Academic is closed and the Internet Archive scan (selfconstitution0000kors) is an access-restricted lending copy. Korsgaard's drafts of the 2002 Locke Lectures it grew from are no longer on her Harvard site, but survive in the Internet Archive's captures of her old FAS page (people.fas.harvard.edu/~korsgaar/Korsgaard.LL1–LL6, 2002–2005). They are author-posted and lawful, but book-length and superseded, and were not read. Its account is read first-hand in her short restatement "The Normative Constitution of Agency" (2014, [LIT-587](../../record/literature.d/LIT-587.md), read in full), which extends it. Filed because it is the canonical citation for self-constitution. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied or the drafts are read, not on merit. |
| [LIT-598](../../record/literature.d/LIT-598.md) | The Markov blanket trick: On the scope of the free energy principle and active inference | Raja, Valluri, Baggs, Chemero & Anderson (2021), Physics of Life Reviews 39:49–72. Restating the FEP's derivation (Langevin dynamics, NESS, Helmholtz decomposition, Markov blanket, variational bound), the authors grant that it is true of things so defined. But blankets apply to every "thing", not everything (the candle flame is excluded); the partition is placed by modelling convenience; relational properties (heading, affordances) and constitutive self-organization (a membrane the cell makes) fall outside it. The blanket's real role is to supply the partition variational Bayes needs. Active inference presupposes perception and action in its generative models. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-450](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-450.md)), from the authors' PhilSci-Archive preprint (March 2021); the Physics of Life Reviews version was not seen. Worth reading as the scope critique of the free energy principle: granted its premises the principle is true, but it applies only to "things" already given a Markov-blanket partition, the partition is placed where the model needs it, and relational properties and constitutive self-organization fall outside it. The blanket is a "trick" that lets any thing be modelled as a variational autoencoder. Active inference, as a process theory, presupposes perception and action in the generative model it is handed. |
| [LIT-599](../../record/literature.d/LIT-599.md) | The Sources of Normativity | Korsgaard (1996), Cambridge University Press, ed. Onora O'Neill: the expanded Tanner Lectures (1992), with commentaries by G. A. Cohen, Raymond Geuss, Thomas Nagel and Bernard Williams and Korsgaard's reply. The four lectures follow the 1992 sequence: the normative question, reflective endorsement, the authority of reflection, and the origin of value and the scope of obligation. Unread: registered from the contents; the argument is read in the lectures, [LIT-564](../../record/literature.d/LIT-564.md). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from the Crossref records of the book and its chapters, not read: no lawful full text could be reached. Cambridge Core is closed, the author's Harvard deposit (DASH) answered with an AWS WAF human-verification page that was not passed, and the Internet Archive scan (sourcesofnormati0000kors) is an access-restricted lending copy. Its argument is read first-hand in the Tanner Lectures it expands (1992, [LIT-564](../../record/literature.d/LIT-564.md), read in full), which it extends. Filed because it is the canonical citation for practical identity, and because what it adds (the commentaries and Korsgaard's reply) is what the lectures lack. It stays Deferred until a copy is supplied, not on merit. |
| [LIT-600](../../record/literature.d/LIT-600.md) | Attention to Action: Willed and Automatic Control of Behavior | Norman & Shallice (1986), in Davidson, Schwartz & Shapiro (eds.), Consciousness and Self-Regulation vol. 4 (Plenum), pp. 1-18; first issued as CHIP Report 99 (UCSD, December 1980). Per the report's ERIC abstract: routine action runs off by "contention scheduling" among competing schemas, and a "supervisory attentional mechanism" biases that selection for novel, ill-learned, dangerous or planned action; "will becomes the application of attentional resources to the control of action". Unread. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from bibliographic records and from the ERIC abstract of the 1980 technical report, not read. The 1986 chapter is not open access: Unpaywall reports no open copy, and SpringerLink returned a JavaScript bot challenge, which was not pursued. The 1980 version (CHIP Report 99, University of California San Diego, catalogued by ERIC as ED205562, "Technical Report No. 8006", 43 pages) was the brief's lead. ERIC has a catalogue record and abstract but no full text: its fulltext URL returns 404. The copies on Academia.edu sit behind a bot challenge, and those on ResearchGate and Scribd are uploads this record does not use. No DTIC copy was found. A human could ask UCSD's library or the authors' institutions for the CHIP report, or read the chapter through a library, and move this to Active. |
| [LIT-601](../../record/literature.d/LIT-601.md) | The Haken–Kelso–Bunz (HKB) model: from matter to movement to mind | Kelso (2021), Biological Cybernetics 115(4):305–322, a 60th-anniversary retrospective. HKB models bimanual coordination by the relative phase φ, with dφ/dt = −a sin φ − 2b sin 2φ and potential V = −a cos φ − b cos 2φ: both in-phase and anti-phase are stable for b/a > 1/4, and anti-phase loses stability below it, as observed when movement frequency rises. Noise, symmetry breaking (Δω) and metastability extend it; with Kuramoto's model it becomes a generalized HKB for many agents. The same dynamics is reported within a body, between people and with machines, and in brain activity. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-467](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-467.md)), from the free-to-read publisher PDF. Filed in place of Kelso's book Dynamic Patterns ([LIT-578](../../record/literature.d/LIT-578.md)) and as the read account of the paywalled Haken–Kelso–Bunz paper ([LIT-588](../../record/literature.d/LIT-588.md)), both Deferred. Worth reading as Kelso's own short statement of coordination dynamics: the HKB order-parameter equation for relative phase, its stochastic and symmetry-breaking extensions, metastability as relative coordination, the generalized HKB–Kuramoto model, and the claim that one coordination dynamics holds within and between people. It is a retrospective by an author of the model, not a review, and it says so. |
| [LIT-602](../../record/literature.d/LIT-602.md) | The Intimate Contest for Self-Command | Schelling (1980), The Public Interest, Summer 1980, pp. 94-118. People behave sometimes "as if they had two selves" in "continual contest for control", and the tactics of self-management (removing the option, bright lines, monitoring, delays, raising the stakes) are the tactics of managing someone else. A single-dimension model of impatience fits poorly. Which self is authentic is left open. Failures of consumer self-management may be worth "on the order of a trillion dollars". | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-452](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-452.md)), in place of the 1984 Ely Lecture ([LIT-573](../../record/literature.d/LIT-573.md)), which has no lawful free copy. Worth reading as Schelling's own first statement of "egonomics": a person sometimes behaves "as if they had two selves" that alternate in command, and the tactics people use on themselves (mittens, alarm clocks across the room, bright lines, Christmas savings, deadlines, delays) are the tactics they use to manage another person. It refuses to say which self is authentic in general, and it gives an order-of-magnitude estimate that failures of consumer self-management cost as much as all other consumer-ignorance problems together. |
| [LIT-603](../../record/literature.d/LIT-603.md) | The Emperor's New Markov Blankets | Bruineberg, Dołęga, Dewhurst & Baltieri (2020 preprint; BBS 45:e183, 2022). Markov blankets entered variational inference as Pearl's conditional-independence shields, tools on a scientist's map. In the free-energy literature since Friston 2013 they became boundaries in the world between agent and environment: "Friston blankets". The two are different constructs; Friston blankets need extra assumptions (a chosen internal node, sensorimotor labels, steady state, sparse coupling) that the formalism does not supply. Using them for metaphysical conclusions ("inference within a model") requires metaphysical premises; used instrumentally ("inference with a model") they are innocent but philosophically idle. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-447](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-447.md)), but in its December 2020 PhilSci-Archive version; the Behavioral and Brain Sciences target article of 2021–2022 is, by the authors' own note, "heavily rewritten" and could not be read lawfully beyond its abstract. Worth reading as the critique that names the problem: "Pearl blankets", the conditional-independence tool of graphical models, are conflated with "Friston blankets", a realist construct meant to be the boundary between an agent and its world. The second cannot be read off the mathematics of the first, and its use to settle questions about life and mind needs metaphysical premises of its own. It re-runs the primordial-soup simulation of [LIT-526](../../record/literature.d/LIT-526.md) and shows how the blanket found there depends on modelling choices. |
| [LIT-604](../../record/literature.d/LIT-604.md) | Control theory: A useful conceptual framework for personality–social, clinical, and health psychology | Carver & Scheier (1982), Psychological Bulletin 92(1):111-135. A review arguing, by its title, that control theory is a useful framework for personality-social, clinical and health psychology. Unread: no lawful open copy and no published abstract could be reached. | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from bibliographic records, not read. No lawful free copy was found. Unpaywall reports no open copy. Semantic Scholar marks it closed and says the publisher has withheld the abstract. The APA landing page refused the request (HTTP 403). The authors' University of Miami pages offer their self-report scales, not their papers. No shorter work by Carver and Scheier stating the control-theory model was found in a lawful open copy. The only open copies seen were on ResearchGate and similar sharing sites, which this record does not use. A human with APA PsycNet access could read it and move it to Active. |
| [LIT-605](../../record/literature.d/LIT-605.md) | An Accumulator Model for Spontaneous Neural Activity Prior to Self-Initiated Movement | Schurger, Sitt & Dehaene (2012), PNAS 109(42). In Libet's spontaneous movement task, a leaky stochastic accumulator, δx = (I − kx)Δt + cξ√Δt, with weak constant urgency I and a threshold, fitted only to waiting times, reproduces the averaged readiness potential (r² = 0.96): time-locking to threshold crossings averages up the autocorrelated noise that produced them. Fast responses to random interruptions were preceded by a slow negativity, as predicted. The "neural decision to move now" is the late threshold crossing, about 150 ms before movement, so Libet's inference of an unconscious decision a half-second earlier is unfounded. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-462](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-462.md)); worth reading as the model that reinterprets the readiness potential. A leaky stochastic accumulator with a weak constant input ("urgency"), fitted with three parameters to 14 participants' waiting times in Libet's task, reproduces the shape of the averaged readiness potential (r² = 0.96) because averaging epochs time-locked to threshold crossings recovers the noise that happened to carry the accumulator there. Its prediction, that fast responses to unpredictable clicks are preceded by a slow negativity, was confirmed (13 participants). On this account the "neural decision to move now" is the threshold crossing about 150 ms before movement, not the onset of the readiness potential, and Libet's inference that the brain decides before awareness is "unfounded". The authors say plainly that this shows the readiness potential could reflect spontaneous fluctuation, not that it does. |
| [LIT-606](../../record/literature.d/LIT-606.md) | Thine Own Self: True Self-Concept Accessibility and Meaning in Life | Schlegel, Hicks, Arndt & King (2009), JPSP 96(2):473–490. Five studies: faster "me" judgments for one's own true-self traits (residualized on speed for control traits) predict presence of meaning in life, while actual-self accessibility does not, controlling affect, need satisfaction and (Study 4) AI-3 authenticity, which was itself uncorrelated with true-self accessibility. Subliminal priming of true-self traits raised reported meaning (Studies 3 and 5), for disliked traits as much as liked ones. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-469](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-469.md)); worth reading as the experimental case that the true self-concept, as distinct from self-reported authenticity, matters to felt meaning. In five student studies (N = 56 to 149), how quickly people classify their own "true self" traits predicts presence of meaning in life over and above actual-self accessibility, need satisfaction and AI-3 authenticity, and subliminally priming true-self traits raises reported meaning, even when the traits are disliked. Effects are small, meaning is self-report only, and the Study 5 interaction is printed with an F that cannot carry its stated p. |
| [LIT-607](../../record/literature.d/LIT-607.md) | Natural Gradient Works Efficiently in Learning | Amari (1998), Neural Computation 10(2):251–276. When the parameter space carries a Riemannian metric G, the steepest descent direction of a loss is the natural gradient G⁻¹∇L, not the ordinary gradient. For a statistical model the metric is the Fisher information, the only invariant one. Online natural gradient learning with step 1/t is Fisher efficient: it attains the Cramér–Rao bound asymptotically, as well as the best batch estimator. The paper computes the metric and its inverse for a simple perceptron, for the group of nonsingular matrices (blind source separation, giving the update ∇L·WᵀW) and for linear systems, proposes an adaptive learning rate with 1/t convergence, and conjectures that the natural gradient escapes the plateaus of backpropagation. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-472](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-472.md)); worth reading as the source of the natural gradient: steepest descent in a Riemannian parameter space is G⁻¹∇L (Theorem 1), the Fisher information is the metric on a statistical model, and online learning with the natural gradient and a 1/t rate is Fisher efficient (Theorem 2). Its efficiency proof needs the Fisher matrix at the optimum to be invertible, which is exactly what singular learning theory says fails for neural networks. |
| [LIT-608](../../record/literature.d/LIT-608.md) | Deep Equals Shallow for ReLU Networks in Kernel Regimes | Bietti & Bach (2021), ICLR 2021. Dot-product kernels on the sphere are diagonal in spherical harmonics, with one eigenvalue µ_k per degree k shared by N(d, k) ~ k^(d−2) harmonics. Theorem 1 reads the asymptotic decay of µ_k off the kernel function's expansion at ±1: a non-integer exponent ν there gives µ_k ~ k^(−d−2ν+1). The arc-cosine kernels have ν = 1/2 and 3/2, and composing them preserves ν, so the NTK of a deep ReLU network decays as k^(−d) and its random-feature kernel as k^(−d−2), exactly as for two layers. Deep and shallow ReLU kernels therefore have the same RKHS, the same as the Laplace kernel's for the NTK. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-475](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-475.md)): main text and Appendix A in full, the proofs in Appendices B and C followed at the level of their lemmas. Worth reading as the general rule behind the NTK eigenvalue decays the record holds: for a dot-product kernel on the sphere, the decay of its spherical-harmonic eigenvalues is fixed by the kernel function's non-integer exponent at the endpoints ±1 (Theorem 1). Deep ReLU kernels keep the shallow exponent, so depth leaves the spectrum's shape unchanged: µ_k ~ C(d, L) k^(−d) for the NTK and k^(−d−2) for the random-feature kernel, with only the constant growing in L (Corollaries 2–3). |
| [LIT-609](../../record/literature.d/LIT-609.md) | Bayesian hypothesis testing for psychologists: A tutorial on the Savage–Dickey method | Wagenmakers, Lodewyckx, Kuriyal & Grasman (2010), Cognitive Psychology 60(3):158–189. For nested models, H0: φ = φ0 inside H1, the Bayes factor BF01 equals the ratio of the posterior to the prior density of φ at φ0 under H1 (Eq. 12), if the nuisance parameters' prior under H1 tends to their prior under H0 as φ → φ0. So a Bayes factor can be read off MCMC samples from the larger model alone, with a nonparametric density estimate at one point. Order restrictions are handled by truncating prior and posterior. Three worked psychology examples, including hierarchical one- and two-sample t-tests. Limitations stated in §8: density estimates in the tails, nested models only, and the Borel–Kolmogorov paradox. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-484](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-484.md)); worth reading as the record's working statement of the Savage–Dickey density ratio: for nested models, the Bayes factor for a point null φ = φ0 is the height of the posterior for φ at φ0 divided by the height of the prior there (Eq. 12), provided the nuisance parameters' prior under the alternative, as φ → φ0, equals their prior under the null. Its derivation is Appendix A. Read it for the condition and for the limitations of §8 (density estimation in the tails, nested models only, the Borel–Kolmogorov paradox), not for the psychology examples, which are illustrations. |
| [LIT-610](../../record/literature.d/LIT-610.md) | The Weighted Likelihood Ratio, Linear Hypotheses on Normal Location Parameters | Dickey (1971), Annals of Mathematical Statistics 42(1):204–223. Unread. The paper the Savage–Dickey density ratio is named after: per the papers in the record that cite it, it treats the Bayes factor (the "weighted likelihood ratio") for linear hypotheses on normal location parameters, and states the result that the Bayes factor for a nested point hypothesis is the posterior-to-prior density ratio at the hypothesised value. The record reads that result through Wagenmakers et al. ([LIT-609](../../record/literature.d/LIT-609.md)). | Deferred — registered 2026-10-03 from metadata and from what the papers that cite it say, not read. Project Euclid hosts the Annals of Mathematical Statistics free to read, and OpenAlex marks this article diamond open access, but every request for the article page (by DOI, by the euclid.aoms handle and by the full-text URL) returned an Incapsula bot challenge, which I did not try to get past. OpenAlex lists no repository copy. A human reader with a browser can open the publisher's free copy, and a reading from it would move this to Active. |
| [LIT-611](../../record/literature.d/LIT-611.md) | Gemma Scope: Open Sparse Autoencoders Everywhere All At Once on Gemma 2 | Lieberum et al. (2024), Google DeepMind technical report. Gemma Scope releases JumpReLU sparse autoencoders trained on the attention, MLP and residual-stream activations of every layer of Gemma 2 2B and 9B, and of three layers of 27B: over 400 SAEs, over 2,000 counting sparsity variants, and over 30 million latents. Each latent fires only above a learned threshold θ_i, and training penalises the L0 count of active latents directly, through straight-through estimators. Evaluation is by sparsity–fidelity curves (delta LM loss and FVU against mean L0). Base-model SAEs transfer to the instruction-tuned model, wider SAEs split latents, and the authors state that no agreed metric of SAE quality exists. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-482](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-482.md)). Worth reading as the technical report for the open SAE suite that later SAE studies, including the anthology's, are run on, and as the clearest statement of what a JumpReLU SAE is. A JumpReLU SAE is a learned hard threshold per latent, z ⊙ H(z − θ), trained against squared reconstruction error plus λ‖f(x)‖₀ (Eqs. 3–4). The report releases over 400 such SAEs (over 2,000 counting the sparsity variants) on every layer and sublayer of Gemma 2 2B and 9B and on three layers of 27B. Its evaluations are sparsity–fidelity curves, with no criterion offered for choosing a width or a sparsity; the authors say there is no consensus on what measures SAE quality. |
| [LIT-612](../../record/literature.d/LIT-612.md) | The Convergence Rate of Neural Networks for Learned Functions of Different Frequencies | Basri, Jacobs, Kasten & Kritchman (2019), NeurIPS 2019. In the linearised dynamics of a wide two-layer ReLU network, on uniform data on a sphere, the Gram matrix H∞ is a convolution, so its eigenvectors are Fourier modes or spherical harmonics. Its eigenvalues are computed in closed form per frequency. Without bias, every odd frequency k ≥ 3 has eigenvalue zero, so the network cannot represent or learn it (Theorems 2 and 4). With a zero-initialised bias all frequencies pass. The eigenvalues fall as 1/k² on S¹ and, numerically, about 1/k^(d+1) on S^d, so the time to learn frequency k grows as k² and k³ on the circle and the 2-sphere. Deep and residual networks show the same exponents. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-481](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-481.md)): main text in full, the supplementary derivations of the S^d eigenvalues skimmed. Worth reading for the closed-form NTK eigenvalues per frequency and the exact null space they reveal. On the circle the bias-free two-layer ReLU kernel has eigenvalue 2(k²+1)/(π²(k²−1)²) on even k ≥ 2 and exactly zero on odd k ≥ 3 (Eq. 12), so such a network cannot represent cos 3θ at all (Theorems 2 and 4). A zero-initialised bias fills the null space, with 1/(π²k²) on odd k (Eq. 13). Convergence time then grows as k² on S¹ and about k³ on S², and shallow, deep and residual networks match: fitted exponents 1.93–2.15 on S¹ and 2.74–3.13 on S² (Figs. 6–7). |
| [LIT-613](../../record/literature.d/LIT-613.md) | Traces of Class/Cross-Class Structure Pervade Deep Learning Spectra | Papyan (2020), JMLR 21(252). Cross-entropy indexes every deepnet quantity by example, class and would-be class, a two-way MANOVA layout. In the spectra of features, backpropagated errors, gradients, the Fisher, the Hessian and weights, knockouts attribute the top outlier to the global mean, C−1 outliers to between-class second moment, a mini-bulk of C(C−1) to between-cross-class covariance, and the main bulk to within-cross-class covariance. Feature and error class means separate from the bulk and become orthogonal with depth. Gradient class means are approximately feature class means ⊗ error class means, layer by layer; K-FAC's single Kronecker product misaligns the Fisher's outliers and the class-wise CFAC aligns them. For multinomial logistic regression on a Gaussian class model the expected Fisher spectrum is derived exactly. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-474](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-474.md)); worth reading as the general account of class/cross-class structure in deepnet spectra: features, backpropagated errors, gradients, Fisher, Hessian and weights all show one outlier from the global mean, C−1 from class means, a mini-bulk of about C(C−1) from cross-class means, and a main bulk from within-class variation. Per layer, gradient class means are approximately Kronecker products of feature class means and error class means, which is why K-FAC, pairing every class with every class, misaligns the Fisher's outliers, and CFAC, pairing each class with itself, aligns them. Proved only for multinomial logistic regression on a Gaussian model. |
| [LIT-614](../../record/literature.d/LIT-614.md) | Bayesian model reduction | Friston, Parr & Zeidman (2018; v2 2019), arXiv:1805.07092. A review of Bayesian model reduction: given a fitted full model's prior P, approximate posterior Q and free energy F, the free energy of a model with any other prior P̃ is ln E_Q[P̃/P] + F, and its posterior follows by reweighting Q (Eqs. 9–10). Closed forms are given for Gaussian, Dirichlet, beta, gamma, categorical and multinomial priors. Three worked simulations (sparse regression, cluster pruning in a Gaussian mixture, network discovery in a linear dynamical system) and a review of uses: structure learning, hierarchical (PEB) inversion as a cascade of reductions, and "sleep-like" pruning in active-inference agents. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-477](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-477.md)); worth reading as the reference statement of Bayesian model reduction (BMR): the reduced free energy F[P̃:P] ≈ ln E_Q[P̃/P] + F[P] (Eq. 9), which the authors call a generalisation of the Savage–Dickey ratio "to any new prior", with closed forms for Gaussian, Dirichlet, beta, gamma, categorical and multinomial priors (Eqs. 11–12, Table 1). The Dirichlet form is what lets active-inference agents prune their own likelihood mappings. Its examples are simulations, and its review of applications is of the authors' own lab. |
| [LIT-615](../../record/literature.d/LIT-615.md) | An Active Inference Approach to Modeling Structure Learning: Concept Learning as an Example Case | Smith, Schwartenbeck, Parr & Friston (2020), Front. Comput. Neurosci. 14:41. A partially observed Markov decision process with eight animal "slots", some starting with flat likelihoods, learns new concepts without feedback: a slot is engaged only when its flat mapping explains a feature combination better than any learned concept. Bayesian model reduction on the learned prior over states then resets slots the data did not need. In 100 runs per condition the true number of concepts won in 45–80% of runs for 4–7 concepts and rarely for 2–3, and in every run when the likelihood was given. The agent also generalises to an unseen animal in one shot, and the paper simulates neural and dopaminergic responses. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-485](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-485.md)); worth reading as the record's worked example of structure learning in an active-inference agent: "model expansion" by spare hidden-state slots whose flat likelihoods win inference only for a truly novel stimulus, and "model reduction" by applying Bayesian model reduction to the agent's learned prior over states, so that unused slots are reset. It is a proof of concept with eight animals and six features. Reduction recovered the true number of concepts in 45–80% of 100 runs for 4–7 concepts, failed for 2–3, and succeeded every time only when the likelihood mapping was given rather than learned. |
| [LIT-616](../../record/literature.d/LIT-616.md) | A Widely Applicable Bayesian Information Criterion | Watanabe (2012), arXiv:1208.6338; JMLR 14:867–897 (2013). A model is singular when the parameter-to-distribution map is not one-to-one or the Fisher information is not positive definite: neural networks, mixtures, reduced-rank regression, HMMs. Its Bayes free energy is nL_n(w0) + λ log n − (m − 1) log log n + O_p(1), with λ the real log canonical threshold (RLCT), not d/2. WBIC, the posterior average of nL_n(w) at inverse temperature 1/log n, has the same leading terms even for singular, unrealizable models, equals BIC for regular ones, and needs no knowledge of the true distribution. Two temperatures give an estimate of λ. In reduced-rank regression WBIC picked the true rank in 100 of 100 trials and recovered theoretical RLCTs closely. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-473](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-473.md)); worth reading as the record's lawful, short statement of the core of singular learning theory, standing in for Watanabe's unread book ([LIT-354](../../record/literature.d/LIT-354.md)). It states the Bayes free energy asymptotics F = nL_n(w0) + λ log n − (m − 1) log log n + O_p(1), with λ the real log canonical threshold and m its multiplicity (Theorem 2), and defines λ through resolution of singularities (Theorem 1, Eq. 18). Its own result is WBIC, the posterior mean of nL_n(w) at inverse temperature 1/log n, which matches F to the λ log n term even for singular and unrealizable models (Theorem 4), equals BIC for regular ones (Theorem 5), and estimates λ from two temperatures (Corollary 3). The prior results are stated with citations to the book, not re-proved. |
| [LIT-617](../../record/literature.d/LIT-617.md) | The Full Spectrum of Deepnet Hessians at Scale: Dynamics with SGD Training and Sample Size | Papyan (2018), arXiv. Stochastic Lanczos without reorthogonalization (O(Mnp) time, O(p) memory) plus subspace-iteration deflation measures the full Hessian spectrum of VGG11 and ResNet18 on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST and CIFAR-10/100, over 16,000 trained models. It confirms a bulk with about C outliers, attributes the outliers to the Gauss–Newton term G and most of the bulk to the residual H, finds H's spectrum symmetric with a power-law tail rather than Marchenko–Pastur, and finds H never negligible. Version 2 decomposes G into class means (outliers), cross-class variation and within-class variation (two bulks) and tracks them across epochs and sample sizes. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-478](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-478.md)); worth reading as the measurement that the bulk-and-C-outliers spectrum of the Hessian persists at full scale (VGG11, 28M parameters), that the outliers belong to the Gauss–Newton/Fisher term G and the bulk mostly to the residual H, and, in its second version, that G splits into class means (outliers) and two bulks (cross-class and within-class variation). Its tools are stochastic Lanczos spectral density estimation without reorthogonalization, plus subspace-iteration deflation. |
| [LIT-618](../../record/literature.d/LIT-618.md) | Prevalence of Neural Collapse during the terminal phase of deep learning training | Papyan, Han & Donoho (2020), PNAS 117(40). Training past zero error (the terminal phase, TPT) drives four linked phenomena in the last layer: within-class variability collapses (Tr(Σ_WΣ_B†)/C → 0), centred class means become equinorm and equiangular with cosines −1/(C−1), a simplex ETF; classifier rows become proportional to the means; and the decision becomes nearest class mean. Measured on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, SVHN, CIFAR-10/100, STL10 and ImageNet with VGG, ResNet and DenseNet, 480 models. Test accuracy and DeepFool robustness keep improving during TPT. Theorems: NC1–NC2 imply NC3–NC4 for the Webb–Lowe and Soudry max-margin classifiers, and the simplex ETF uniquely maximizes the small-noise error exponent. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-476](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-476.md)); worth reading as the source of neural collapse: in the terminal phase of training, last-layer features collapse to their class means (NC1), the centred means form a simplex equiangular tight frame (NC2), the classifier aligns with them (NC3) and the decision becomes nearest class centre (NC4). Measured on 7 datasets × 3 architectures; NC1–NC2 imply NC3–NC4 for the Webb–Lowe and max-margin classifiers, and the simplex ETF is the unique optimal codebook in a vanishing-noise Gaussian channel. It explains Papyan's Hessian outliers as class means outgrowing collapsing within-class variation. |
| [LIT-619](../../record/literature.d/LIT-619.md) | Measurements of Three-Level Hierarchical Structure in the Outliers in the Spectrum of Deepnet Hessians | Papyan (2019), ICML. For a C-class network the Gauss–Newton term of the Hessian is G = (1/n)ΔΔᵀ, a second moment (not covariance) of logit derivatives δᵢ,c,c′ indexed by example, true class and logit. Grouping them into C² group means δc,c′ and C clusters with centres δc gives G = G₀ + G₁ + G₂ + G₃ (Eq. 28). The top-C outliers of G are those of G₁ = (C−1)Ave_c δcδcᵀ, because clusters are far apart and tight. Early in training the group means cluster by logit c′, later by true class c. Holds across MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10 and ImageNet with VGG, ResNet and DenseNet; the outliers exceed their approximations slightly, as random matrix theory would predict. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-479](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-479.md)); worth reading as the paper that explains the C outliers of the deepnet Hessian: the Gauss–Newton/Fisher term G is a second moment, not a covariance, of logit derivatives indexed by (example, class, logit), and the C outliers are the eigenvalues of G₁ = (C−1)·Ave_c δ_c δ_cᵀ, the C class-cluster centres, computable from a C×C Gram matrix without high-dimensional eigenanalysis. The match is empirical, over 18,000 trained models. |
| [LIT-620](../../record/literature.d/LIT-620.md) | Post hoc Bayesian model selection | Friston & Penny (2011), NeuroImage 56(4):2089–2099. If every model of interest is a full model with a different prior and the same likelihood, the evidence of any reduced model is p(y\|m_F) times the full posterior expectation of p(θ\|m_i)/p(θ\|m_F) (Eq. 3). So the reduced models never have to be fitted. A point-mass reduced prior gives the Savage–Dickey ratio (Eq. 6). Under Gaussian priors and posteriors the reduced free energy and posterior are closed-form in the full model's means and precisions (Eq. 9). In simulations an exhaustive search over 4,096 linear models finds the four true regressors, and 64 network models find the true three-connection graph. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-487](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-487.md)); worth reading as the source of Bayesian model reduction: if a set of models differ from a full model only in their priors, the evidence of each is the full model's evidence times the posterior expectation of the prior ratio (Eq. 3), so one inversion scores them all. Under Gaussian (Laplace) posteriors the reduced free energy is closed-form (Eq. 9). The Savage–Dickey ratio is the point-mass special case (Eq. 6), and ARD and model selection are the same evidence maximisation over prior hyperparameters. The demonstrations are simulations, and whether the reduced free energy of a nonlinear model is a good proxy for the free energy of the reduced model is left open by the authors. |
| [LIT-621](../../record/literature.d/LIT-621.md) | Shampoo: Preconditioned Stochastic Tensor Optimization | Gupta, Koren & Singer (2018), ICML. For a parameter tensor of order k, Shampoo keeps one preconditioner per dimension, each the gradient contracted with itself over all other dimensions, and multiplies the gradient along dimension i by (Hⁱ)^{-1/2k}. In the matrix case this is W ← W − η L^{-1/4} G R^{-1/4}, equivalent to preconditioning the flattened gradient by (L ⊗ R)^{1/4}, which upper-bounds the full-matrix AdaGrad preconditioner up to the gradient rank (Lemma 8). Regret is O(√T) in online convex optimization (Theorems 7, 10). On CIFAR-10/100 and LM1B it converges faster than SGD with momentum, Adam and AdaGrad at similar step time. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-480](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-480.md)); worth reading as the source of per-dimension Kronecker preconditioning: for a matrix parameter, left and right statistics L = Σ GGᵀ and R = Σ GᵀG, update W ← W − η L^{-1/4} G R^{-1/4}, i.e. a step preconditioned by (L ⊗ R)^{1/4}, which by Lemma 8 dominates the full AdaGrad matrix. Its statistic is the accumulated gradient outer product, not the Fisher; its guarantee is an O(√T) regret in online convex optimization; its deep-learning evidence is four training curves. |
| [LIT-622](../../record/literature.d/LIT-622.md) | Optimizing Neural Networks with Kronecker-factored Approximate Curvature | Martens & Grosse (2015), ICML. K-FAC approximates natural gradient descent in neural networks. Each Fisher block between layers i and j, E[āᵢ₋₁āⱼ₋₁ᵀ ⊗ gᵢgⱼᵀ], is replaced by E[āᵢ₋₁āⱼ₋₁ᵀ] ⊗ E[gᵢgⱼᵀ] (activations times backpropagated derivatives), whose error is a sum of third- and fourth-order cumulants. The inverse is then taken as block-diagonal or block-tridiagonal across layers. With factored Tikhonov damping, re-scaling by the exact Fisher, an adaptive momentum and a growing mini-batch, it needs orders of magnitude fewer iterations than tuned SGD with Nesterov momentum on three deep autoencoder benchmarks, and it is invariant to affine reparameterizations of each layer's inputs and pre-activations. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-486](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-486.md)); worth reading as the source of the Kronecker-factored Fisher: each layer's Fisher block E[āāᵀ ⊗ ggᵀ] is approximated by E[āāᵀ] ⊗ E[ggᵀ], so it inverts as two small matrices, and the inverse is further taken as block-diagonal or block-tridiagonal across layers. The factorization is justified by small higher-order cumulants, checked on one small tanh network; the speedups are on three deep autoencoders. |
| [LIT-623](../../record/literature.d/LIT-623.md) | Bayesian Interpolation | MacKay (1992), Neural Computation 4(3):415–447. Two levels of inference: fit each model, then rank models by their evidence P(D\|H). Under a Gaussian approximation the evidence is the best-fit likelihood times an Occam factor, the ratio of posterior to prior accessible parameter volume, so complex or finely tuned models penalise themselves (Eqs. 5–6). For linear interpolation with quadratic regularisers the evidence sets the regularising constant and the noise level through γ, the number of well-determined parameters (2αE_W = γ, 2βE_D = N − γ). It ranks polynomial, radial-basis, spline, Hermite and neural-network models on two data sets. In expectation, no fixed model's log evidence beats the true model's. | Active — read in full 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-471](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-471.md)); worth reading as the source of the evidence framework and the Occam factor in the form the field uses: evidence ≈ best-fit likelihood × Occam factor, the Occam factor being the ratio of posterior to prior accessible parameter volume, (2π)^{k/2} det^{-1/2}A · P(w_MP\|H) under a Gaussian approximation (Eqs. 5–6). It also gives the "number of good parameter measurements" γ = Σ λ_a/(λ_a + α), which sets the regularising constant (2αE_W = γ) and the noise level (2βE_D = N − γ) at the evidence maximum. The framework is Gull and Skilling's, as MacKay says, and every demonstration is one-dimensional interpolation. Flagged anthology-candidate for its regularisation and early-stopping arguments. |
| [LIT-624](../../record/literature.d/LIT-624.md) | Towards Understanding the Spectral Bias of Deep Learning | Cao, Fang, Wu, Zhou & Gu (2019). For a two-layer ReLU network trained by gradient descent on squared loss, the training residual projected onto the eigenfunctions of the NTK integral operator with the r_k largest eigenvalues decays like (1 − λ_(r_k))^T (Theorem 4.2). This holds for any labelling, provided the sample size beats the eigengap and the width is polynomial in λ_(r_k)^(−1). On uniform data on S^d the eigenfunctions are spherical harmonics, with µ_k = Ω(k^(−d−1)) for k ≫ d and Ω(d^(−k+1)) for d ≫ k on even degrees, and µ_k = 0 on odd k ≥ 3 (Theorem 4.3). Lower degrees are learned first, faster, and with fewer samples and narrower networks. | Active — read 2026-10-03 ([NOTE-483](../../record/notes.d/NOTE-483.md)): main text and Appendix A in full, the proofs in Appendix B followed at the level of their lemmas. Worth reading as the theorem that makes "spectral bias" a statement about NTK eigendirections: for a two-layer ReLU network trained by gradient descent, the residual projected onto the top r_k eigenfunctions of the NTK integral operator shrinks like (1 − λ_(r_k))^T (Theorem 4.2), for any target, with sample size governed by the eigengap λ_(r_k) − λ_(r_k+1). On uniform spherical data the eigenfunctions are spherical harmonics, the odd degrees above 1 have eigenvalue zero, and µ_k = Ω(max(k^(−d−1), d^(−k+1))) for even k (Theorem 4.3). |
